### Question 001: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 01 (LEARNING)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the cellular and molecular mechanisms underlying allograft rejection, specifically the roles of T cells (CD4+ helper T cells and CD8+ cytotoxic T cells) and MHC molecules in recognizing foreign antigens and initiating the immune response leading to graft damage. (理解移植物排斥反应的细胞和分子机制，特别是T细胞（CD4+辅助T细胞和CD8+细胞毒性T细胞）和MHC分子在识别外来抗原和启动导致移植物损伤的免疫反应中的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an unknown HLA type. The patient is started on standard immunosuppressive therapy including cyclosporine, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals acute tubular necrosis and interstitial inflammation with infiltration of mononuclear cells, including lymphocytes and macrophages. Immunofluorescence staining shows deposition of C4d in the peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the peripheral blood shows a significant increase in CD4+ T cells expressing CD69, an early activation marker. The patient's serum creatinine continues to rise despite dose adjustments of immunosuppressants.

Which of the following cellular and molecular events is MOST likely mediating the acute rejection process in this patient?

(A) Antibody-mediated complement activation leading to endothelial injury via C5b-9 formation.
(B) CD4+ T helper cells recognizing donor MHC class II molecules on recipient antigen-presenting cells (APCs), leading to cytokine release and activation of CD8+ T cells.
(C) CD8+ cytotoxic T cells directly recognizing donor MHC class I molecules on graft cells, leading to apoptosis of graft cells.
(D) Recipient B cells producing antibodies against donor ABO blood group antigens, causing hyperacute rejection.
(E) Natural killer (NK) cells recognizing graft cells lacking recipient MHC class I molecules, leading to antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC).

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease (ESRD) from diabetes, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for potential transplant rejection.
- **Transplant Type:** Kidney transplant from a deceased donor with unknown HLA type. HLA mismatch is a major risk factor for rejection.
- **Immunosuppression:** Standard triple therapy (cyclosporine, mycophenolate mofetil, prednisone) is used, but rejection still occurs. This indicates the rejection process is overcoming the immunosuppression.
- **Timing:** Three weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is characteristic of acute cellular rejection (ACR) or antibody-mediated rejection (AMR). Hyperacute rejection occurs within minutes to hours, and chronic rejection develops over months to years.
- **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated creatinine. These are classic signs of acute graft dysfunction.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Acute tubular necrosis (ATN) and interstitial inflammation with mononuclear cell infiltration (lymphocytes, macrophages). This points towards an inflammatory process damaging the graft.
- **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. C4d is a stable breakdown product of the classical complement pathway activation (C3 convertase to C5 convertase). Its presence suggests antibody-mediated complement activation, often associated with AMR. However, C4d can also be seen in some forms of ACR, particularly those involving T cell-dependent antibody production or T cell-mediated endothelial damage.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD4+ T cells expressing CD69. CD69 is an early activation marker for T cells, indicating recent T cell activation, strongly suggesting a T cell-mediated process.
- **Diagnosis:** The clinical presentation, biopsy findings (inflammation, ATN), C4d deposition, and T cell activation (CD69+ CD4+ T cells) are most consistent with acute rejection, likely a mixed cellular and antibody-mediated rejection (MCAMR) or predominantly cellular rejection with some antibody involvement. The question asks for the *most likely* mediating event.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Transplant Rejection Overview:** Allograft rejection is an immune response against foreign MHC molecules (alloantigens) present on the donor graft cells. The immune system recognizes these as non-self.
- **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** Primarily mediated by T cells.
    - **Sensitization Phase:** Recipient APCs (dendritic cells, macrophages, B cells) process donor MHC molecules (both class I and class II) presented on graft cells. These APCs migrate to recipient lymph nodes and present donor MHC peptides via MHC class II molecules to naive CD4+ T helper cells. Costimulatory signals (e.g., B7 on APC binding CD28 on T cell) are crucial for full T cell activation.
    - **Effector Phase:**
        - Activated CD4+ T helper cells (Th1 type) produce cytokines like IFN-γ and TNF-α, which activate macrophages and promote inflammation. They also provide help to CD8+ T cells.
        - Activated CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) recognize donor MHC class I molecules presented on graft cells (e.g., tubular epithelial cells, endothelial cells). Upon recognition, CTLs release perforin and granzymes, inducing apoptosis of the target graft cells. CTLs also express Fas ligand (FasL), which binds to Fas on target cells, triggering apoptosis.
- **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** Mediated by antibodies (primarily IgG) directed against donor HLA antigens or non-HLA antigens (e.g., MICA, endothelial cell antigens).
    - **Mechanisms:**
        - **Complement Activation:** Antibodies bind to donor endothelial cells, activating the classical complement pathway (C1q, C4, C2, C3, C5b-9). C5b-9 (membrane attack complex, MAC) causes endothelial cell lysis and inflammation. C3a and C5a are anaphylatoxins that recruit inflammatory cells. C4d deposition is a marker of classical pathway activation.
        - **ADCC:** Antibodies bind to donor cells, and NK cells (or other Fc receptor-bearing cells) recognize the Fc portion of the antibody, leading to target cell lysis.
        - **Endothelial Cell Activation:** Antibody binding can directly activate endothelial cells, leading to upregulation of adhesion molecules, procoagulant activity, and release of inflammatory mediators.
- **Role of CD69:** CD69 is one of the earliest markers of T cell activation, upregulated rapidly upon TCR stimulation. Its presence on CD4+ T cells strongly supports an ongoing T cell-mediated immune response.
- **C4d Deposition:** While classically associated with AMR, C4d deposition can occur in ACR, especially when T cells stimulate B cells to produce antibodies against donor antigens or when T cells directly damage endothelial cells, leading to complement activation. In this case, the prominent T cell activation (CD69+ CD4+ T cells) points towards a significant T cell component.

##### 3. Synthesis: Connecting Clues to the Answer
The patient presents with signs of acute graft dysfunction (fever, flank pain, rising creatinine) three weeks post-transplant. The biopsy shows inflammation and ATN. Flow cytometry reveals activated CD4+ T cells (CD69+). C4d deposition is present. These findings suggest a mixed rejection process, but the prominent T cell activation (CD69+ CD4+ T cells) points strongly towards a central role for T cells. Option (B) describes the initial sensitization phase where CD4+ T cells recognize donor MHC class II on recipient APCs, leading to activation and cytokine release, which is the fundamental initiating event for cellular rejection. Option (C) describes the effector phase of CD8+ T cells, which is also part of ACR, but the sensitization phase described in (B) is the prerequisite step involving CD4+ T cells recognizing MHC class II. Given the strong evidence of T cell activation (CD69+ CD4+ cells), the process initiated by CD4+ T cell recognition of donor MHC class II is the most likely *mediating* event described among the choices.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Antibody-mediated complement activation leading to endothelial injury via C5b-9 formation. This describes a key mechanism of AMR. While C4d deposition is present, suggesting some antibody involvement, the prominent finding of activated CD4+ T cells (CD69+) points more strongly towards a primary T cell-mediated process (ACR). AMR is less likely to be the *primary* driver given the strong T cell activation signal.
- (C): CD8+ cytotoxic T cells directly recognizing donor MHC class I molecules on graft cells, leading to apoptosis of graft cells. This is a crucial effector mechanism of ACR. However, the initial sensitization and activation phase involves CD4+ T cells recognizing MHC class II on APCs, which then orchestrate the response, including activating CD8+ T cells. Option (B) describes this initiating event more fundamentally. While CD8+ cells are involved, the question asks for the *most likely mediating* event, and the CD4+ T cell activation is a key initiating and coordinating step.
- (D): Recipient B cells producing antibodies against donor ABO blood group antigens, causing hyperacute rejection. Hyperacute rejection occurs within minutes to hours due to pre-formed antibodies (e.g., anti-ABO, anti-HLA). This patient's rejection occurred at 3 weeks, ruling out hyperacute rejection. ABO compatibility is typically checked before transplantation.
- (E): Natural killer (NK) cells recognizing graft cells lacking recipient MHC class I molecules, leading to antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC). NK cells are involved in missing-self recognition (graft cells lacking recipient MHC I) or ADCC (if antibodies are present). While NK cells can contribute to rejection, the primary mechanism in acute rejection, especially with T cell activation markers present, is T cell-mediated (ACR) or antibody-mediated (AMR). This option doesn't fit the clinical picture as well as T cell-mediated mechanisms. ADCC requires antibodies, and the primary driver here seems to be T cells.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR)                                  | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR)                               | Hyperacute Rejection                                  |
|----------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------|
| **Timing**           | Days to weeks (most common 1-3 weeks)                             | Days to weeks                                                     | Minutes to hours                                       |
| **Primary Mediators**| CD4+ T cells, CD8+ T cells                                       | Antibodies (IgG)                                                   | Pre-formed antibodies (Anti-ABO, Anti-HLA)             |
| **Key Mechanisms**   | T cell recognition of donor MHC I/II, cytokine release, CTL killing | Complement activation (C4d deposition, C5b-9), ADCC, endothelialitis | Complement activation, endothelial damage, thrombosis |
| **Biopsy Findings**  | Interstitial inflammation, mononuclear cell infiltrate, ATN        | Glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis, C4d deposition, thrombosis | Thrombosis, fibrinoid necrosis, neutrophil infiltrate |
| **T Cell Markers**   | Increased CD69+, CD4+, CD8+ T cells                               | May see T cell infiltrate, but antibodies are primary drivers     | Not typically T cell mediated                           |
| **C4d Deposition**   | Can be present, especially in mixed rejection                      | Characteristic finding                                             | Can be present                                         |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute transplant rejection is primarily mediated by T cells (ACR) or antibodies (AMR). ACR involves CD4+ T cell recognition of donor MHC class II on APCs, leading to activation and cytokine release, followed by CD8+ T cell-mediated cytotoxicity. AMR involves antibodies targeting donor HLA or non-HLA antigens, activating complement or causing ADCC. Hyperacute rejection is caused by pre-formed antibodies and occurs immediately. The timing (3 weeks), biopsy findings (inflammation), and evidence of T cell activation (CD69+ CD4+ T cells) in this case point towards acute rejection, with a significant T cell component likely initiating the process. Key differentials include AMR (consider C4d, endothelialitis) and hyperacute rejection (ruled out by timing).

<a id='question-002'></a>

### Question 002: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 02 (OBJECTIVES)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying acute rejection in organ transplantation, specifically the roles of T cells (CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes and CD4+ helper T cells) and antibodies (pre-formed or de novo) in mediating graft damage. (理解器官移植中急性排斥的免疫机制，特别是T细胞（CD8+细胞毒性T淋巴细胞和CD4+辅助T细胞）和抗体（预先存在的或新产生的）在介导移植物损伤中的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an unknown HLA type. His pre-transplant panel reactive antibody (PRA) screen was negative. Post-operatively, he is started on standard immunosuppression consisting of tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. On day 10 post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, and decreased urine output. Serum creatinine rises from a baseline of 1.5 mg/dL to 4.0 mg/dL. A renal biopsy is performed, revealing diffuse interstitial edema, inflammatory cell infiltrates primarily composed of lymphocytes (predominantly CD8+ T cells) and eosinophils, and evidence of tubular cell necrosis. Immunofluorescence microscopy shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes shows a significant increase in activated CD4+ and CD8+ T cells.

Which of the following immunological mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for this patient's acute kidney graft dysfunction?

(A) Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) due to pre-formed donor-specific antibodies (DSA) binding to endothelial cells, leading to complement activation and endothelial injury.
(B) Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) due to de novo DSA binding to endothelial cells, leading to complement activation and endothelial injury.
(C) T-cell mediated rejection (TCMR), specifically acute cellular rejection (ACR), characterized by CD8+ cytotoxic T cell-mediated tubular epithelial cell injury and CD4+ T cell-mediated inflammation.
(D) Chronic antibody-mediated rejection (CAMR), characterized by transplant glomerulopathy and peritubular capillaritis, typically occurring months to years post-transplant.
(E) Acute cellular rejection (ACR) mediated primarily by B cells and plasma cells infiltrating the graft, leading to antibody production against the graft.

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for potential transplant rejection.
*   **Immunosuppression:** Standard regimen (tacrolimus, mycophenolate, prednisone) is used, but rejection can still occur.
*   **Timing:** Day 10 post-transplant. This timeframe is characteristic of acute rejection (either cellular or antibody-mediated).
*   **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, rising creatinine. These are classic signs of acute graft dysfunction.
*   **Renal Biopsy Findings:**
    *   Diffuse interstitial edema and inflammatory cell infiltrates (lymphocytes, eosinophils).
    *   Tubular cell necrosis.
    *   Predominantly CD8+ T cells in infiltrates.
    *   C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries.
*   **Peripheral Blood Findings:** Increased activated CD4+ and CD8+ T cells.
*   **Pre-transplant PRA:** Negative, making pre-formed DSA less likely as the primary cause, although de novo DSA can still occur.

The combination of clinical signs (acute graft dysfunction), biopsy findings (lymphocytic infiltrate rich in CD8+ T cells, tubular injury), and peripheral blood findings (activated T cells) strongly points towards T-cell mediated rejection (TCMR), specifically acute cellular rejection (ACR). The presence of C4d deposition suggests a component of antibody-mediated injury, often seen in mixed acute rejection (ACMR), but the predominant histological features (CD8+ infiltrate, tubular injury) are characteristic of ACR. The question asks for the *most likely* mechanism.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** This is a T-cell mediated process. Alloreactive T cells (both CD4+ helper T cells and CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes) recognize donor HLA antigens presented on recipient antigen-presenting cells (APCs) or directly on graft cells.
    *   **CD4+ T cells:** Recognize HLA class II molecules on APCs. They release cytokines (like IFN-γ, TNF-α) that activate macrophages and recruit other inflammatory cells, contributing to interstitial inflammation and fibrosis.
    *   **CD8+ T cells:** Recognize HLA class I molecules on graft cells (especially tubular epithelial cells). They directly kill graft cells via perforin/granzyme pathways or Fas/FasL interactions, leading to tubular injury and dysfunction.
    *   **Histopathology:** Characterized by interstitial infiltrates (lymphocytes, macrophages, eosinophils), tubular epithelial cell damage/necrosis, and sometimes endothelialitis.
    *   **Immunology:** Driven by T-cell receptor (TCR) recognition of allogeneic MHC molecules.
*   **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** Mediated by antibodies (pre-formed DSAs or de novo DSAs) that bind to donor HLA antigens on graft endothelial cells.
    *   **Mechanisms:**
        *   **Complement Activation:** Antibody binding activates the classical complement pathway, leading to C3a/C5a production (inflammation) and formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC, C5b-9), causing endothelial cell injury. C4d deposition is a marker of complement activation.
        *   **Fc Receptor-Mediated Injury:** Antibodies bind to Fc receptors on effector cells (NK cells, macrophages, neutrophils), leading to antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) or phagocytosis of endothelial cells.
        *   **Endothelial Activation:** Antibody binding can directly activate endothelial cells, leading to upregulation of adhesion molecules and pro-coagulant factors.
    *   **Histopathology:** Characterized by microvascular inflammation (glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis), endothelialitis, C4d deposition, and sometimes thrombosis.
*   **Chronic Antibody-Mediated Rejection (CAMR):** Occurs later (months to years) and involves chronic endothelial injury, leading to transplant glomerulopathy (double contours in glomerular basement membranes) and interstitial fibrosis/tubular atrophy.
*   **Mixed Acute Rejection (ACMR):** Combines features of both ACR and AMR, often seen when both T cells and antibodies contribute to graft injury. C4d deposition is common in ACMR.

In this case, the prominent CD8+ T cell infiltrate and tubular necrosis are hallmarks of ACR. While C4d deposition is present, suggesting some AMR component, the overall picture is most consistent with ACR being the primary driver of the acute dysfunction.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) due to pre-formed donor-specific antibodies (DSA) binding to endothelial cells, leading to complement activation and endothelial injury.** Incorrect. The patient had a negative pre-transplant PRA screen, making pre-formed DSAs unlikely. While de novo DSAs are possible, the histological findings (prominent CD8+ infiltrate, tubular necrosis) are more typical of ACR than pure AMR, which usually features more prominent endothelialitis and microvascular inflammation.
- (B) **Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) due to de novo DSA binding to endothelial cells, leading to complement activation and endothelial injury.** Incorrect. Although de novo DSA can occur, the biopsy findings are more characteristic of ACR. AMR typically shows more prominent endothelialitis and microvascular inflammation (glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis) than described here. The presence of C4d supports some AMR component, but it doesn't negate the strong evidence for ACR.
- (D) **Chronic antibody-mediated rejection (CAMR), characterized by transplant glomerulopathy and peritubular capillaritis, typically occurring months to years post-transplant.** Incorrect. CAMR is a chronic process occurring much later than day 10 post-transplant. The histological features described (transplant glomerulopathy) are also different from those seen in this acute presentation.
- (E) **Acute cellular rejection (ACR) mediated primarily by B cells and plasma cells infiltrating the graft, leading to antibody production against the graft.** Incorrect. ACR is primarily mediated by T cells (CD4+ and CD8+), not B cells or plasma cells. B cells and plasma cells are characteristic of humoral (antibody-mediated) rejection.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR) | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) | Chronic AMR (CAMR) |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :----------------- |
| **Timing**              | Days to weeks post-transplant   | Days to weeks post-transplant      | Months to years    |
| **Primary Mediators**   | T cells (CD4+, CD8+)            | Antibodies (DSA)                  | Antibodies (DSA)   |
| **Key Histology**       | Lymphocytic infiltrate (CD8+), tubular injury, interstitial edema | Endothelialitis, microvascular inflammation (glomerulitis, capillaritis), C4d deposition | Transplant glomerulopathy, peritubular capillaritis, fibrosis |
| **C4d Deposition**      | May be present (ACMR)           | Characteristic                    | Characteristic     |
| **Patient Presentation** | Acute graft dysfunction        | Acute graft dysfunction           | Chronic graft dysfunction |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute rejection in transplantation can be T-cell mediated (ACR) or antibody-mediated (AMR), or a combination (ACMR). ACR is characterized by T-cell infiltrates and direct cell killing, while AMR involves antibodies targeting endothelial cells. Distinguish based on timing, clinical presentation, biopsy findings (especially cell types in infiltrates, location of injury, and presence of C4d), and pre-transplant sensitization status. ACR typically shows prominent CD8+ T cells and tubular injury, while AMR shows endothelialitis and microvascular inflammation with C4d deposition.

<a id='question-003'></a>

### Question 003: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 03 (problems)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying transplant rejection, specifically focusing on the role of T cells (CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes and CD4+ helper T cells) and antibodies (specifically donor-specific antibodies, DSAs) in acute and chronic rejection, and the distinction between hyperacute, acute, and chronic rejection types. (理解移植排斥的免疫机制，特别是T细胞（CD8+细胞毒性T淋巴细胞和CD4+辅助T细胞）和抗体（特别是供体特异性抗体，DSA）在急性和慢性排斥中的作用，以及超急性、急性和慢性排斥类型的区别。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an ABO blood type mismatch (recipient O, donor A). He is started on standard immunosuppression consisting of tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, elevated serum creatinine (from a baseline of 1.2 mg/dL to 4.5 mg/dL), and decreased urine output. A renal biopsy reveals diffuse interstitial edema, inflammatory cell infiltrates primarily composed of T lymphocytes (CD3+ cells, with a predominance of CD8+ cells), and evidence of tubular injury. Immunofluorescence microscopy shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Serum analysis reveals elevated levels of donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) against HLA class I antigens.

Which of the following is the MOST likely mechanism contributing to this patient's acute kidney transplant rejection?

(A) Pre-formed anti-donor ABO antibodies binding to donor endothelial cells, activating complement and causing immediate thrombosis.
(B) CD4+ T helper cells recognizing donor MHC class II molecules on recipient antigen-presenting cells, leading to cytokine release and recruitment of inflammatory cells.
(C) CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes recognizing donor MHC class I molecules on injured renal tubular epithelial cells, leading to direct cell lysis.
(D) Complement-mediated damage to the graft endothelium due to antibody-dependent cellular cytotoxicity (ADCC) by recipient NK cells.
(E) Chronic inflammation and fibrosis mediated by B cells producing non-DSAs against minor histocompatibility antigens, leading to gradual graft dysfunction.

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, ESRD due to diabetes, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for transplant rejection.
*   **ABO Mismatch:** Recipient O, Donor A. This indicates a potential for pre-formed antibodies (anti-A) in the recipient, which could cause hyperacute rejection, but the timing (3 weeks) makes this less likely as the primary cause.
*   **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus (calcineurin inhibitor), mycophenolate mofetil (purine synthesis inhibitor), prednisone (corticosteroid). Standard regimen, but rejection can still occur.
*   **Timing:** 3 weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is characteristic of acute rejection, specifically T-cell mediated rejection (TCMR) or antibody-mediated rejection (AMR).
*   **Clinical Presentation:** Fever, flank pain, elevated creatinine, decreased urine output. Classic signs of acute graft dysfunction/rejection.
*   **Renal Biopsy Findings:**
    *   Diffuse interstitial edema, inflammatory cell infiltrates (T lymphocytes, CD3+, CD8+ predominance), tubular injury. These findings are hallmarks of T-cell mediated rejection (TCMR).
    *   C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. This indicates complement activation, often associated with antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), but can also be seen in TCMR, especially when T cells activate complement pathways or in mixed rejection.
*   **Serum Analysis:** Elevated donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) against HLA class I antigens. This confirms the presence of antibodies targeting the donor graft, strongly suggesting antibody-mediated rejection (AMR).

*   **Synthesis:** The patient presents with signs and symptoms of acute rejection 3 weeks post-transplant. The biopsy shows features of both TCMR (T cell infiltrates, tubular injury) and AMR (C4d deposition, DSAs). The question asks for the *most likely* mechanism. While DSAs are present (suggesting AMR), the prominent T-cell infiltrate, particularly CD8+ cells, and tubular injury point strongly towards a significant T-cell mediated component. CD8+ T cells directly attack graft cells expressing donor MHC class I. The presence of DSAs and C4d suggests a mixed rejection picture (TCMR + AMR), but the CD8+ T cell infiltrate and tubular injury are classic features of TCMR. Option (C) directly addresses the role of CD8+ T cells in this context.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **Transplant Rejection Types:**
    *   **Hyperacute Rejection:** Occurs within minutes to hours. Mediated by pre-formed recipient antibodies (e.g., ABO, anti-HLA) against donor endothelium. Leads to complement activation, endothelial damage, thrombosis, and graft failure.
    *   **Acute Rejection:** Occurs days to months post-transplant. Can be T-cell mediated (TCMR) or antibody-mediated (AMR), or mixed.
        *   **TCMR:** Primarily mediated by recipient T cells recognizing donor MHC molecules. CD4+ T cells recognize MHC class II on APCs, releasing cytokines (IFN-γ, TNF-α) that activate macrophages and recruit other inflammatory cells. CD8+ T cells recognize MHC class I on graft cells (e.g., tubular epithelial cells) and directly kill them via perforin/granzyme or Fas/FasL pathways.
        *   **AMR:** Mediated by recipient antibodies (DSAs) against donor HLA antigens. DSAs bind to donor endothelial cells, activating complement (classical pathway) and/or recruiting NK cells via Fc receptors (ADCC), leading to endothelial injury, microvascular inflammation (glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis), thrombosis, and graft dysfunction. C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries is a marker of complement activation in AMR.
    *   **Chronic Rejection:** Occurs months to years post-transplant. Characterized by progressive fibrosis, vascular changes (arteriolar hyalinosis, intimal thickening), and gradual loss of graft function. Involves both cellular and humoral immunity, often with DSA production against non-HLA antigens (e.g., MICA, endothelial antigens) and T cell responses.

*   **Cellular Immunology:**
    *   **MHC Class I:** Expressed on all nucleated cells. Presents endogenous peptides (e.g., viral, tumor, self) to CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs).
    *   **MHC Class II:** Expressed primarily on professional antigen-presenting cells (APCs) like dendritic cells, macrophages, B cells. Presents exogenous peptides (e.g., bacterial, allergens) to CD4+ helper T cells.
    *   **T Cell Activation:** Requires two signals: TCR engagement with peptide-MHC complex (Signal 1) and co-stimulatory molecule interaction (e.g., CD28-B7) (Signal 2).
    *   **CD8+ CTL Function:** Recognize MHC class I-peptide complexes on target cells. Kill target cells via:
        *   Perforin/Granzyme pathway: Perforin creates pores in the target cell membrane, allowing granzymes (serine proteases) to enter and induce apoptosis.
        *   Fas/FasL pathway: CTLs express FasL, which binds to Fas receptor on target cells, triggering apoptosis.
    *   **CD4+ Helper T Cell Function:** Recognize MHC class II-peptide complexes on APCs. Secrete cytokines (e.g., IFN-γ, TNF-α, IL-2) that activate macrophages, B cells, and CTLs.

*   **Antibody-Mediated Rejection:** DSAs (typically IgG) bind to donor HLA antigens on endothelial cells. This can lead to:
    *   Complement activation (classical pathway): C3b deposition, C5b-9 (MAC) formation, endothelial damage. C4d is a stable breakdown product of C4b, deposited during complement activation.
    *   ADCC: NK cells bind to the Fc portion of IgG bound to target cells via FcγRIII (CD16), leading to NK cell activation and target cell lysis.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** This describes hyperacute rejection. The timing (3 weeks) is wrong for hyperacute rejection, which occurs within minutes to hours. While there is an ABO mismatch, the clinical presentation is consistent with acute rejection occurring weeks later, not immediate thrombosis.
- (B): **Incorrect.** While CD4+ T cells play a role in TCMR by activating macrophages and other cells, the biopsy findings specifically highlight CD8+ T cell predominance and direct tubular injury. CD8+ T cells are the primary mediators of direct cytotoxicity against graft parenchymal cells (like tubular epithelial cells) expressing donor MHC class I. Option (C) is a more specific and direct mechanism described by the biopsy findings.
- (D): **Incorrect.** This describes a mechanism of antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) via ADCC. While the patient has DSAs and C4d deposition (suggesting AMR), the biopsy findings emphasize T-cell infiltrates (especially CD8+) and tubular injury, which are more characteristic of TCMR. ADCC primarily targets endothelial cells, not typically causing the prominent tubular injury seen here.
- (E): **Incorrect.** This describes chronic rejection. The timeframe (3 weeks) is too early for chronic rejection, which develops over months to years. Chronic rejection is characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes, not the acute inflammatory infiltrate and tubular injury described in the biopsy.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Hyperacute Rejection (A) | Acute T-cell Mediated Rejection (TCMR) (C) | Acute Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) (D) | Chronic Rejection (E) |
| :------------------ | :----------------------- | :----------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------ | :-------------------- |
| **Timing**          | Minutes - Hours          | Days - Months                              | Days - Months                               | Months - Years        |
| **Mediator**        | Pre-formed Abs           | CD8+ CTLs, CD4+ Th cells                   | DSAs (IgG)                                  | Abs, T cells          |
| **Target**          | Endothelium              | Parenchymal cells (e.g., tubules), Endothelium | Endothelium                                 | Vessels, Parenchyma   |
| **Mechanism**       | Complement, ADCC, Thrombosis | Direct cytotoxicity, Cytokine release       | Complement, ADCC                            | Fibrosis, Vascular changes |
| **Biopsy Findings** | Thrombosis, Endothelial swelling | T cell infiltrates (CD8+ predominant), Tubular injury | Endothelialitis, C4d deposition, Glomerulitis | Fibrosis, Arterial hyalinosis |
| **Key Markers**     | Pre-formed Abs            | CD8+ T cells, IFN-γ                         | DSAs, C4d                                   | Fibrosis, Vascular changes |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute transplant rejection can be T-cell mediated (TCMR) or antibody-mediated (AMR), or mixed. TCMR is characterized by T-cell infiltrates (often CD8+ predominant) and parenchymal cell injury. AMR is characterized by DSAs, complement activation (C4d deposition), and endothelial injury. Differentiating between TCMR and AMR is crucial for management. Key differentials include hyperacute rejection (immediate, pre-formed Abs) and chronic rejection (late onset, fibrosis).

<a id='question-004'></a>

### Question 004: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 04 (concerning)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying acute rejection in organ transplantation, specifically the role of T cells (CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes and CD4+ helper T cells) and antibodies (specifically, the role of pre-formed antibodies against donor HLA antigens leading to hyperacute rejection, and the role of de novo antibodies in acute antibody-mediated rejection) in graft damage. (理解器官移植中急性排斥的免疫机制，特别是T细胞（CD8+细胞毒性T淋巴细胞和CD4+辅助T细胞）和抗体（特别是针对供体HLA抗原的预存抗体导致超急性排斥，以及针对供体HLA抗原的新生抗体在急性抗体介导性排斥中的作用）对移植物的损伤。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 58-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor. The donor's HLA type is A1/B8/DR3, and the recipient's HLA type is A2/B7/DR4. The patient is started on standard immunosuppression consisting of tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three days post-transplant, the patient develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. A kidney biopsy reveals diffuse interstitial edema, neutrophil infiltration, and fibrinoid necrosis of peritubular capillaries. Immunofluorescence microscopy shows linear deposition of C4d along the peritubular capillaries. Serum analysis shows elevated levels of anti-HLA class I antibodies.

Which of the following immunological mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for this patient's acute kidney graft dysfunction?

(A) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by recipient NK cells against donor endothelial cells expressing HLA class I antigens.
(B) Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) mediated by recipient T cells recognizing donor HLA class II antigens presented on graft cells.
(C) Direct allorecognition by recipient CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) recognizing donor HLA class I antigens on graft cells.
(D) Antibody-mediated complement activation and endothelial injury caused by pre-formed recipient antibodies against donor HLA class I antigens.
(E) T-helper cell-mediated activation of macrophages leading to chronic interstitial fibrosis and tubular atrophy.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 58-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for transplant rejection.
*   **HLA Mismatch:** Donor (A1/B8/DR3) vs. Recipient (A2/B7/DR4). Significant HLA mismatch increases the risk of rejection.
*   **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus (calcineurin inhibitor), mycophenolate mofetil (purine synthesis inhibitor), prednisone (corticosteroid). Standard regimen, but rejection can still occur.
*   **Timing:** 3 days post-transplant. This timeframe is characteristic of acute rejection, specifically hyperacute (minutes to hours) or acute (days to weeks) rejection.
*   **Clinical Presentation:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated creatinine. Classic signs of acute graft dysfunction/rejection.
*   **Biopsy Findings:**
    *   Interstitial edema, neutrophil infiltration: Suggests inflammation and injury.
    *   Fibrinoid necrosis of peritubular capillaries: Indicates vascular damage, often seen in antibody-mediated rejection.
    *   Linear C4d deposition along peritubular capillaries (Immunofluorescence): A hallmark of antibody-mediated rejection, indicating complement activation within the vessel walls.
*   **Serum Analysis:** Elevated anti-HLA class I antibodies. This confirms the presence of antibodies targeting the donor graft.

These clues strongly point towards an acute antibody-mediated rejection process, likely due to pre-formed antibodies against donor HLA antigens, leading to hyperacute or early acute rejection. The C4d deposition and fibrinoid necrosis are key indicators.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **Transplant Rejection Types:**
    *   **Hyperacute Rejection:** Occurs within minutes to hours. Mediated by pre-formed recipient antibodies (e.g., anti-ABO, anti-HLA) against donor antigens. Leads to rapid thrombosis, complement activation, endothelial damage, and graft failure. Characterized by fibrinoid necrosis, neutrophil infiltration, and C4d deposition.
    *   **Acute Rejection:** Occurs days to weeks post-transplant. Can be T-cell mediated (cellular rejection) or antibody-mediated (humoral rejection).
        *   **T-cell mediated:** CD4+ T cells recognize donor HLA class II on antigen-presenting cells (APCs) in the graft, leading to inflammation and direct cytotoxicity by CD8+ T cells recognizing donor HLA class I on graft cells. Histology shows interstitial inflammation, tubular injury, and sometimes vascular changes.
        *   **Antibody-mediated:** Mediated by recipient antibodies (pre-formed or de novo) against donor HLA antigens (class I or II) on endothelial cells. Activates complement (leading to C4d deposition) and recruits inflammatory cells, causing endothelial injury, microvascular inflammation (glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis), thrombosis, and graft dysfunction.
    *   **Chronic Rejection:** Occurs months to years post-transplant. Characterized by progressive fibrosis, tubular atrophy, and vascular changes (e.g., transplant glomerulopathy). Involves both cellular and humoral immunity.
*   **Mechanism of Antibody-Mediated Rejection:** Antibodies bind to donor HLA antigens on endothelial cells. This triggers:
    *   **Complement Activation:** The classical pathway is activated, leading to the deposition of C3 and C4d fragments on the endothelium, formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC), and recruitment of inflammatory cells.
    *   **Fc Receptor-Mediated Activation:** Antibodies bind to Fc receptors on effector cells (e.g., neutrophils, macrophages, NK cells), leading to their activation and release of cytotoxic mediators.
    *   **Endothelial Cell Activation:** Antibody binding can directly activate endothelial cells, promoting inflammation and coagulation.
*   **C4d Deposition:** C4d is a stable breakdown product of C4b, a component of the classical complement pathway. Its presence in peritubular capillaries is a strong indicator of complement activation and antibody-mediated injury.
*   **Anti-HLA Class I Antibodies:** These antibodies target HLA class I molecules (HLA-A, HLA-B, HLA-C) expressed on virtually all nucleated cells, including endothelial cells. Binding can activate complement and recruit effector cells, leading to endothelial damage and graft dysfunction.

In this case, the rapid onset (3 days), clinical signs of graft dysfunction, biopsy findings (fibrinoid necrosis, C4d deposition), and presence of anti-HLA class I antibodies strongly support antibody-mediated rejection, likely due to pre-formed antibodies causing a hyperacute or very early acute rejection pattern.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by recipient NK cells against donor endothelial cells expressing HLA class I antigens.** While ADCC can occur in transplant rejection (e.g., involving antibodies against HLA class I), the key finding here is *linear C4d deposition*, which is a hallmark of *complement activation* within the vessel wall, not primarily ADCC. ADCC involves NK cells binding to antibody-coated target cells via Fc receptors, leading to cytotoxicity. It doesn't typically cause linear C4d deposition.
- (B) **Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) mediated by recipient T cells recognizing donor HLA class II antigens presented on graft cells.** T cells do not mediate CDC. CDC is mediated by antibodies binding to target cells, followed by complement activation and MAC formation. While antibodies are involved in this case, the primary effector cells causing the damage are not T cells, and the mechanism involves more than just direct T cell cytotoxicity. Furthermore, the C4d deposition points to complement activation triggered by antibodies binding to *endothelial cells*, not T cells recognizing HLA class II on graft cells.
- (C) **Direct allorecognition by recipient CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) recognizing donor HLA class I antigens on graft cells.** This describes T-cell mediated rejection. While CTLs are important in rejection, the prominent features here (fibrinoid necrosis, linear C4d deposition, anti-HLA antibodies) are characteristic of antibody-mediated rejection, not primarily T-cell mediated rejection. T-cell mediated rejection typically shows more interstitial inflammation and less prominent vascular changes like fibrinoid necrosis and C4d deposition.
- (E) **T-helper cell-mediated activation of macrophages leading to chronic interstitial fibrosis and tubular atrophy.** This describes a mechanism involved in *chronic* rejection, not the acute presentation seen 3 days post-transplant. Chronic rejection is characterized by fibrosis and atrophy, which are not the primary findings described in the biopsy. The acute presentation with vascular damage and C4d deposition points to an acute process.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                     | Correct Answer (D) - Antibody-Mediated Rejection | Option A (ADCC)                                  | Option B (CDC by T cells)                     | Option C (T-cell mediated)                     | Option E (Chronic Rejection)                   |
| :-------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------ | :----------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Mediator**         | Antibodies (anti-HLA)                             | Antibodies, NK cells                              | Antibodies                                     | CD8+ T cells                                   | T cells, Macrophages                           |
| **Key Pathological Feature** | Fibrinoid necrosis, C4d deposition                | Cell-mediated cytotoxicity, less C4d deposition | Complement activation, less prominent vascular | Interstitial inflammation, tubular injury      | Fibrosis, tubular atrophy                      |
| **Timing**                  | Acute (days) or Hyperacute (minutes/hours)        | Acute                                            | Acute                                          | Acute                                          | Chronic (months/years)                         |
| **Mechanism**               | Complement activation, endothelial injury         | NK cell cytotoxicity via Fc receptors             | Antibody-mediated complement lysis             | Direct CTL killing, cytokine release           | Chronic inflammation, fibrosis                  |
| **Target Antigen**           | Donor HLA Class I (endothelial)                   | Donor HLA Class I (endothelial)                   | Donor HLA Class II (APCs)                       | Donor HLA Class I (graft cells)                | Various graft antigens                         |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute antibody-mediated rejection is characterized by the presence of anti-donor HLA antibodies, complement activation (indicated by C4d deposition), and endothelial injury (fibrinoid necrosis). This contrasts with T-cell mediated rejection, which primarily involves cellular infiltration and direct cytotoxicity, and chronic rejection, which involves fibrosis and atrophy. Remember that C4d deposition is a key histological marker for antibody-mediated rejection. Differentiate between hyperacute (pre-formed antibodies, minutes/hours), acute (days/weeks), and chronic (months/years) rejection patterns.

<a id='question-005'></a>

### Question 005: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 05 (definitions)
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Define and differentiate between hyperacute, acute, and chronic rejection in organ transplantation, including the timing, immunological mechanisms (antibodies, T cells), and histological features. (移植排斥反应的定义与区分：超急性、急性、慢性排斥反应，包括时间、免疫机制（抗体、T细胞）和组织学特征。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 55-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor. The patient's pre-transplant panel reactive antibody (PRA) screen was negative. Post-transplant, his creatinine levels begin to rise sharply within hours of the transplant surgery, reaching 8 mg/dL within 24 hours. A biopsy of the transplanted kidney reveals diffuse thrombosis of glomerular capillaries, endothelial cell swelling, and fibrin deposition. Immunofluorescence microscopy shows strong C4d deposition along the peritubular capillaries. The patient's serum creatinine continues to rise despite aggressive immunosuppression with tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and steroids.

Which of the following best describes the immunological mechanism underlying this patient's kidney failure?

(A) T-cell mediated cytotoxicity against donor MHC class I molecules on tubular epithelial cells, leading to interstitial fibrosis.
(B) Pre-formed donor-specific antibodies binding to donor endothelial MHC class I antigens, activating complement and causing endothelial damage.
(C) T-cell mediated activation of macrophages, leading to granuloma formation and fibrosis in the renal allograft.
(D) Antibody-mediated injury targeting the basement membrane antigens of the glomeruli, resulting in crescent formation.
(E) Chronic inflammation and fibrosis driven by low-level T-cell responses and repeated episodes of subclinical acute rejection.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 55-year-old male with ESRD needing kidney transplant.
- **Timing:** Kidney failure occurs *within hours* of transplantation. This rapid onset is a critical clue.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Rapid rise in creatinine (to 8 mg/dL within 24 hours) indicates acute graft dysfunction.
- **Biopsy Findings:**
    - Glomerular capillary thrombosis: Suggests vascular injury.
    - Endothelial cell swelling: Indicates endothelial damage.
    - Fibrin deposition: Points towards complement activation and coagulation cascade involvement.
    - Strong C4d deposition: A hallmark of antibody-mediated complement activation, specifically the classical pathway.
- **Pre-transplant PRA:** Negative PRA suggests no pre-existing antibodies against donor HLA antigens, but *de novo* antibody formation or non-HLA antibodies can still occur.
- **Immunosuppression:** Failure of standard immunosuppression further supports a mechanism resistant to T-cell inhibition.

These clues collectively point towards a rapid, antibody-mediated process involving complement activation and endothelial damage, characteristic of hyperacute rejection.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Hyperacute Rejection:** Occurs within minutes to hours after transplantation. It is mediated by pre-formed recipient antibodies (usually IgG) against donor antigens, most commonly ABO blood group antigens or HLA antigens. These antibodies bind to the donor endothelium, activating the classical complement pathway (C1q, C4, C2, C3, C5b-9). Complement activation leads to endothelial cell damage, platelet aggregation, thrombosis, inflammation, and rapid graft necrosis. Histologically, it shows diffuse thrombosis of small vessels, fibrinoid necrosis, and neutrophil infiltration. C4d deposition is a key diagnostic feature.
- **Acute Rejection:** Typically occurs days to weeks after transplantation. It can be T-cell mediated (cellular rejection) or antibody-mediated (humoral rejection). T-cell mediated rejection involves cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) recognizing donor MHC class I molecules on parenchymal cells (tubules) or helper T cells activating macrophages, leading to interstitial inflammation, tubular injury, and fibrosis. Antibody-mediated acute rejection involves donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) binding to donor HLA antigens on endothelial cells, activating complement and/or Fc receptors, causing endothelialitis, C4d deposition, and graft dysfunction.
- **Chronic Rejection:** Occurs months to years after transplantation. It is characterized by progressive fibrosis and scarring of the graft, often involving both cellular and humoral immune responses, as well as non-immune factors. Histologically, it shows interstitial fibrosis, tubular atrophy, and vascular changes like transplant glomerulopathy (in kidneys).

The vignette's rapid onset (hours), thrombosis, endothelial damage, fibrin deposition, and C4d deposition are classic features of hyperacute rejection, mediated by pre-formed antibodies.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Describes T-cell mediated *acute* rejection (cellular rejection). This typically occurs days to weeks post-transplant, involves CTLs attacking tubular cells, and leads to interstitial inflammation and fibrosis, not rapid thrombosis within hours. C4d deposition is usually absent or minimal in pure T-cell mediated rejection.
- (C): Describes a mechanism often seen in *chronic* rejection or granulomatous inflammation, involving macrophage activation. While T-cells are involved, the rapid onset and specific histological findings (thrombosis, C4d) are inconsistent with this.
- (D): Describes antibody-mediated injury targeting basement membranes, characteristic of Goodpasture syndrome or certain forms of glomerulonephritis, not typical transplant rejection. While antibodies are involved, the target (basement membrane) and histological features (crescent formation) differ from the vignette. Antibody-mediated *acute* rejection can target endothelial cells, but the hyperacute timing and thrombosis are more specific to pre-formed antibodies.
- (E): Describes *chronic* rejection, which occurs much later (months to years) and involves progressive fibrosis, not the acute thrombosis seen here. While repeated subclinical acute rejection can contribute to chronic rejection, the primary event described is hyperacute.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Hyperacute Rejection (Option B) | Acute T-cell Rejection (Option A) | Acute Antibody-Mediated Rejection | Chronic Rejection (Option E) |
|----------------------|---------------------------------|-----------------------------------|-----------------------------------|------------------------------|
| **Timing**           | Minutes to Hours                | Days to Weeks                    | Days to Weeks                    | Months to Years              |
| **Mediator**         | Pre-formed Antibodies (IgG)     | T-cells (CTLs, Th1)               | Donor-Specific Antibodies (DSA)   | T-cells, Antibodies, Non-Immune|
| **Target**           | Endothelium (ABO, HLA)          | Parenchymal Cells (Tubules)       | Endothelium (HLA)                 | Vessels, Parenchyma          |
| **Mechanism**        | Complement Activation, Thrombosis| Cytotoxicity, Inflammation        | Complement Activation, Endothelialitis| Fibrosis, Vascular Changes   |
| **Histology**        | Thrombosis, Fibrinoid Necrosis  | Interstitial Infiltrates, Tubulitis| Endothelialitis, C4d deposition    | Fibrosis, Tubular Atrophy    |
| **C4d Deposition**   | Strong                          | Absent/Minimal                    | Present                           | Variable                     |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Hyperacute rejection is a rapid (<24 hours) process mediated by pre-formed antibodies against donor antigens, leading to thrombosis and graft failure. Key features include rapid onset, endothelial damage, thrombosis, and C4d deposition. Differentiate it from acute rejection (days-weeks, T-cell or antibody mediated) and chronic rejection (months-years, fibrosis). Always consider the timing and histological findings (especially C4d) to distinguish rejection types.

<a id='question-006'></a>

### Question 006: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 06 (knowledge)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of T-cell activation and costimulatory signals (CD28-B7 interaction) in allograft rejection, specifically the mechanism by which cyclosporine A (CsA) inhibits T-cell activation. (理解T细胞活化和共刺激信号（CD28-B7相互作用）在移植物排斥中的作用，特别是环孢素A（CsA）抑制T细胞活化的机制。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor. His pre-transplant panel reactive antibody (PRA) score is 10%, indicating low sensitization. Post-transplant, he is started on a standard immunosuppressive regimen including cyclosporine A (CsA), mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks after transplantation, he develops acute rejection, characterized by fever, flank pain, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals interstitial edema, inflammatory cell infiltrates (predominantly T lymphocytes), and evidence of endothelialitis. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes shows a significant decrease in the number of CD4+ T cells expressing the CD25 activation marker compared to pre-transplant levels.

Which of the following mechanisms BEST explains the effect of cyclosporine A on T-cell activation in this patient?

(A) Inhibition of calcineurin, preventing the dephosphorylation and nuclear translocation of NFAT, thereby reducing IL-2 transcription.
(B) Inhibition of the proteasome, preventing the degradation of IκB, thereby blocking NF-κB nuclear translocation and reducing the transcription of pro-inflammatory cytokines.
(C) Inhibition of dihydrofolate reductase, preventing the synthesis of purines and pyrimidines required for DNA replication in rapidly proliferating lymphocytes.
(D) Inhibition of CD28 costimulatory signaling by binding to the intracellular domain of CD3, preventing the activation of downstream signaling pathways like PI3K and MAPK.
(E) Inhibition of MHC class II molecule expression on antigen-presenting cells (APCs), preventing the interaction between the T-cell receptor (TCR) and the peptide-MHC complex.

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the context for transplantation and potential rejection.
- **Immunosuppression:** Receiving CsA, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. This indicates an attempt to prevent rejection.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Acute rejection (fever, flank pain, elevated creatinine) three weeks post-transplant. This confirms the failure of immunosuppression to prevent rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Interstitial edema, T lymphocyte infiltrates, endothelialitis. These are classic histological signs of acute T-cell mediated rejection.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Decreased CD4+ T cells expressing CD25. CD25 (IL-2 receptor alpha chain) is an activation marker for T cells. Its decrease suggests CsA is inhibiting T-cell activation.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism of action of CsA on T-cell activation.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **T-cell Activation:** T-cell activation requires two signals:
    1.  Signal 1: TCR binding to peptide-MHC complex on APCs.
    2.  Signal 2 (Costimulation): CD28 on T cells binding to B7 molecules (CD80/CD86) on APCs.
- **Signal Transduction:** Signal 1 leads to activation of ZAP-70 and PLCγ, increasing intracellular Ca2+. Signal 2 leads to activation of PI3K and MAPK pathways.
- **NFAT Pathway:** Increased intracellular Ca2+ activates calcineurin, a phosphatase. Calcineurin dephosphorylates NFAT (Nuclear Factor of Activated T-cells), allowing it to translocate to the nucleus.
- **IL-2 Transcription:** In the nucleus, NFAT binds to promoter regions of genes, including IL-2, leading to its transcription. IL-2 is a crucial T-cell growth factor, promoting T-cell proliferation and differentiation.
- **Cyclosporine A (CsA) Mechanism:** CsA is a calcineurin inhibitor. It binds to cyclophilin, an intracellular protein. The CsA-cyclophilin complex inhibits calcineurin. Inhibition of calcineurin prevents the dephosphorylation and subsequent nuclear translocation of NFAT. Consequently, IL-2 gene transcription is significantly reduced, leading to impaired T-cell proliferation and function. This is the primary mechanism by which CsA prevents acute T-cell mediated rejection.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Correct.** This accurately describes the mechanism of CsA: inhibition of calcineurin, preventing NFAT nuclear translocation and IL-2 transcription. This aligns with the clinical presentation of acute rejection and the observed decrease in T-cell activation (CD25 expression).
- (B): **Incorrect.** Inhibition of the proteasome, preventing IκB degradation, is the mechanism of action of drugs like bortezomib. This primarily affects NF-κB signaling, important for inflammation and B-cell survival, but not the primary target of CsA in T-cell activation.
- (C): **Incorrect.** Inhibition of dihydrofolate reductase (DHFR) is the mechanism of action of methotrexate and trimethoprim. This inhibits nucleotide synthesis and affects rapidly dividing cells, including lymphocytes, but it's not how CsA works.
- (D): **Incorrect.** Inhibition of CD28 costimulatory signaling is the mechanism of action of CTLA4-Ig (abatacept). While costimulation is crucial for T-cell activation, CsA acts downstream of both Signal 1 and Signal 2 by targeting the NFAT pathway, which is essential for IL-2 production regardless of the specific costimulatory signals received. CsA does not directly bind CD3 or CD28.
- (E): **Incorrect.** Inhibition of MHC class II expression is the mechanism of action of drugs like chloroquine and hydroxychloroquine, which interfere with antigen processing and presentation in lysosomes. While this can impair T-cell activation, it is not the mechanism of CsA.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Cyclosporine A (CsA)                               | Methotrexate                     | Abatacept (CTLA4-Ig)             | Chloroquine/Hydroxychloroquine |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------- | :------------------------------- | :----------------------------- |
| **Mechanism**           | Calcineurin inhibitor -> ↓ NFAT -> ↓ IL-2          | DHFR inhibitor -> ↓ Nucleotides | Blocks CD28-B7 costimulation     | Inhibits MHC II antigen presentation |
| **Target**              | Calcineurin                                        | Dihydrofolate Reductase          | CD28/B7 interaction              | Lysosomal antigen processing   |
| **Primary Effect**      | Inhibits T-cell proliferation & activation         | Inhibits cell proliferation      | Inhibits T-cell costimulation    | Inhibits T-cell activation     |
| **Clinical Use (Immuno)** | Prevent organ transplant rejection, autoimmune dz | Prevent organ transplant rejection, autoimmune dz | Prevent organ transplant rejection | Autoimmune dz (e.g., RA, SLE) |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Cyclosporine A is a calcineurin inhibitor that prevents T-cell activation by blocking the NFAT pathway and subsequent IL-2 production. This is a key mechanism in preventing acute T-cell mediated organ transplant rejection. Differentiate CsA's mechanism from other immunosuppressants like methotrexate (DHFR inhibitor), abatacept (CD28 blocker), and chloroquine (inhibits antigen presentation).

<a id='question-007'></a>

### Question 007: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 07 (mechanisms)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the cellular and molecular mechanisms underlying acute cellular rejection in organ transplantation, specifically the role of T lymphocytes (CD8+ cytotoxic T cells and CD4+ helper T cells) and their recognition of allogeneic MHC molecules. (理解器官移植中急性细胞性排斥的细胞和分子机制，特别是T淋巴细胞（CD8+细胞毒性T细胞和CD4+辅助T细胞）的作用及其对异体MHC分子的识别。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with one HLA mismatch at the HLA-B locus. Post-transplant, he is started on standard immunosuppression with tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks after transplantation, he develops fever, flank pain, elevated serum creatinine (from a baseline of 1.5 mg/dL to 4.0 mg/dL), and decreased urine output. A renal biopsy reveals diffuse interstitial inflammation with lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular epithelial cell damage, and evidence of endothelialitis in peritubular capillaries. Immunofluorescence staining shows deposition of C4d in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the biopsy tissue shows a predominance of CD8+ T cells infiltrating the tubules and glomeruli, along with CD4+ T cells in the interstitium. The patient's serum tacrolimus level is found to be subtherapeutic (3 ng/mL, therapeutic range 8-12 ng/mL).

Which of the following mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for the observed acute rejection?

(A) Antibody-mediated rejection involving donor-specific antibodies (DSA) binding to HLA class II molecules on endothelial cells, leading to complement activation and endothelial injury.
(B) Acute cellular rejection mediated primarily by CD4+ T helper cells recognizing donor HLA class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells (APCs) in the graft, leading to cytokine release and macrophage activation.
(C) Acute cellular rejection mediated primarily by CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) recognizing donor HLA class I molecules directly on graft parenchymal cells (tubular epithelial cells), leading to cell lysis.
(D) Chronic rejection characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes (graft arteriosclerosis) due to persistent low-level inflammation and antibody deposition.
(E) Hyperacute rejection occurring within minutes to hours of transplantation, mediated by pre-formed anti-donor HLA antibodies causing widespread thrombosis and graft necrosis.

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for transplant rejection.
*   **Immunosuppression:** Standard regimen (tacrolimus, mycophenolate, prednisone) indicates an attempt to prevent rejection.
*   **Timeline:** 3 weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is typical for acute rejection (cellular or antibody-mediated).
*   **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, elevated creatinine, decreased urine output. These are classic signs of acute graft dysfunction, suggestive of rejection.
*   **Biopsy Findings:** Diffuse interstitial inflammation, lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular epithelial cell damage, endothelialitis, C4d deposition. These findings are characteristic of acute rejection. Endothelialitis and C4d deposition can be seen in both cellular and antibody-mediated rejection, but the prominent lymphocytic infiltrate and tubular damage point strongly towards cellular rejection.
*   **Flow Cytometry:** Predominance of CD8+ T cells in tubules/glomeruli, CD4+ T cells in interstitium. This cellular composition is highly characteristic of acute cellular rejection. CD8+ cells directly attack parenchymal cells, while CD4+ cells orchestrate the response.
*   **Subtherapeutic Tacrolimus:** Low drug level increases the risk of rejection by allowing T cell activation.
*   **HLA Mismatch:** One HLA-B mismatch provides an alloantigen target for T cell recognition.

The combination of clinical presentation (acute graft dysfunction), biopsy findings (lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular damage, endothelialitis, C4d), flow cytometry (CD8+ and CD4+ T cells), and subtherapeutic immunosuppression strongly points towards acute cellular rejection. The question asks for the *mechanism*.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** This is a T cell-mediated immune response against the transplanted organ. It typically occurs within the first few months post-transplant.
    *   **Sensitization:** Recipient T cells (both CD4+ and CD8+) recognize donor HLA molecules (alloantigens) presented by donor APCs (like dendritic cells) in the graft or by recipient APCs that have processed donor antigens. This recognition occurs via the T cell receptor (TCR) binding to the peptide-MHC complex. CD4+ T cells recognize HLA class II on APCs, while CD8+ T cells recognize HLA class I on most nucleated cells.
    *   **Effector Phase:**
        *   **CD4+ T cells:** Recognize donor HLA class II on APCs. Upon activation, they differentiate into effector T helper cells (Th1, Th2, Th17) and release cytokines (e.g., IFN-γ, TNF-α, IL-2). IFN-γ activates macrophages, which contribute to tissue damage. IL-2 promotes T cell proliferation.
        *   **CD8+ T cells:** Recognize donor HLA class I directly on graft parenchymal cells (e.g., tubular epithelial cells in the kidney). Upon activation, they become cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs). CTLs kill target cells through several mechanisms:
            *   **Perforin/Granzyme Pathway:** CTLs release perforin (forms pores in target cell membrane) and granzymes (enter through pores, activate caspases, inducing apoptosis).
            *   **Fas/FasL Pathway:** CTLs express Fas ligand (FasL), which binds to Fas receptor (CD95) on target cells, triggering apoptosis.
    *   **Histopathology:** Infiltration of lymphocytes (predominantly T cells) into the graft interstitium, tubules, and sometimes glomeruli. Damage to tubules (tubulitis), endothelialitis (inflammation of endothelial cells), and parenchymal cell necrosis are characteristic features.
*   **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** Mediated by antibodies (DSA) against donor HLA antigens. DSAs bind to HLA molecules on endothelial cells, activating complement (leading to C4d deposition) and recruiting inflammatory cells (e.g., neutrophils, macrophages), causing endothelial injury and microvascular inflammation.
*   **Chronic Rejection:** Occurs months to years post-transplant. Characterized by progressive fibrosis, vascular changes (graft arteriosclerosis), and gradual loss of graft function. Both cellular and humoral immunity contribute.
*   **Hyperacute Rejection:** Occurs within minutes to hours. Mediated by pre-formed antibodies against donor ABO blood group antigens or HLA antigens. Causes rapid thrombosis and graft necrosis.

In this case, the biopsy shows significant lymphocytic infiltration, tubular damage, and endothelialitis, with flow cytometry confirming CD8+ and CD4+ T cell presence. The mechanism involves T cells recognizing alloantigens. CD8+ T cells directly attack tubular cells, explaining the tubular damage. CD4+ T cells contribute by releasing cytokines and activating macrophages. Therefore, acute cellular rejection mediated by both CD4+ and CD8+ T cells is the most likely mechanism. Option (C) specifically highlights the crucial role of CD8+ CTLs in directly attacking parenchymal cells, which is a key feature of the pathology described.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Antibody-mediated rejection involves DSAs, complement activation (C4d deposition is seen, but it can also occur in ACR), and endothelial injury. While endothelialitis is present, the predominant finding is lymphocytic infiltration and *tubular* damage, along with CD8+ T cell infiltration into tubules, which is more characteristic of cellular rejection. AMR typically causes microvascular inflammation (glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis) and C4d deposition, but the primary effector cells are antibodies and complement, not T cells directly attacking tubules.
- (B): While CD4+ T cells play a role in ACR by releasing cytokines and activating macrophages, the vignette emphasizes the presence of CD8+ T cells infiltrating the tubules and causing damage. Option (C) better captures the direct cytotoxic attack on parenchymal cells, which is a major component of ACR pathology in this scenario. ACR is a combined CD4+ and CD8+ response, but CD8+ cells are critical for parenchymal cell injury.
- (D): Chronic rejection is a long-term process characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes, not the acute inflammation and cellular infiltrates seen 3 weeks post-transplant.
- (E): Hyperacute rejection occurs immediately post-transplant due to pre-formed antibodies and causes rapid thrombosis, not the lymphocytic infiltration and gradual onset seen here.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR) | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) | Hyperacute Rejection | Chronic Rejection |
| :------------------ | :----------------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :------------------- | :---------------- |
| **Timing**          | Days to months                 | Days to months                     | Minutes to hours     | Months to years   |
| **Mediator**        | T cells (CD4+, CD8+)            | Antibodies (DSA)                   | Pre-formed Abs      | T cells, Abs      |
| **Key Pathology**   | Lymphocytic infiltrates, tubulitis, endothelialitis | Endothelialitis, microvascular inflammation, C4d deposition | Thrombosis, necrosis | Fibrosis, graft arteriosclerosis |
| **Primary Target**  | Parenchymal cells, endothelium | Endothelium                        | Endothelium          | Vessels, parenchyma |
| **CD8+ T cells**    | Prominent in tubules/glomeruli  | Less prominent                     | Not involved         | Variable          |
| **C4d Deposition**  | Can be present                  | Characteristic                     | Not typical          | Can be present    |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute cellular rejection is primarily T cell-mediated, involving both CD4+ helper T cells and CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes recognizing donor HLA alloantigens. CD8+ T cells directly kill graft parenchymal cells (like tubular epithelial cells), while CD4+ T cells orchestrate the response. Differentiating ACR from AMR is crucial, often relying on biopsy findings (lymphocytic infiltrates vs. microvascular inflammation, C4d deposition) and serology (presence of DSAs). Hyperacute and chronic rejection have distinct timelines and pathological features.
