<a id='question-008'></a>

### Question 008: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 08 (rejection)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathophysiology of acute cellular rejection in organ transplantation, specifically the role of T lymphocytes (CD8+ cytotoxic T cells and CD4+ helper T cells) recognizing alloantigens presented by MHC molecules on graft cells, leading to direct cell killing and cytokine-mediated inflammation. (理解器官移植中急性细胞性排斥的病理生理机制，特别是T淋巴细胞（CD8+细胞毒性T细胞和CD4+辅助T细胞）识别移植细胞上MHC分子呈递的异体抗原，导致直接细胞杀伤和细胞因子介导的炎症。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease secondary to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with one HLA mismatch at the HLA-B locus. He is started on standard immunosuppression consisting of tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and graft tenderness. Serum creatinine rises from a baseline of 1.2 mg/dL to 4.5 mg/dL. A kidney biopsy reveals interstitial edema, inflammatory cell infiltrates predominantly composed of lymphocytes (many appearing as small, dark cells with scant cytoplasm), and evidence of tubular injury. Immunofluorescence staining shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes shows a significant increase in CD8+ T cells expressing CD69, an early activation marker.

Which of the following immunological mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for this patient's acute kidney graft dysfunction?

(A) Antibody-mediated rejection involving pre-formed donor-specific antibodies (DSA) binding to HLA antigens on endothelial cells, leading to complement activation and endothelial injury.
(B) Acute cellular rejection mediated primarily by CD4+ T helper cells recognizing donor MHC class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells within the graft, leading to cytokine release and macrophage activation.
(C) Acute cellular rejection mediated primarily by CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes recognizing donor MHC class I molecules directly on graft parenchymal cells (tubular epithelial cells), leading to direct cell lysis.
(D) Chronic rejection characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes (graft arteriosclerosis) due to long-term low-grade inflammation and T cell activation.
(E) Hyperacute rejection occurring within minutes to hours of transplantation due to pre-existing anti-donor ABO or HLA antibodies causing rapid graft thrombosis and necrosis.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, ESRD due to diabetic nephropathy, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for transplant rejection.
*   **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus (calcineurin inhibitor), mycophenolate mofetil (purine synthesis inhibitor), prednisone (corticosteroid). Standard regimen, but rejection can still occur.
*   **Timing:** 3 weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is characteristic of acute rejection (cellular or antibody-mediated). Hyperacute rejection occurs within minutes/hours, and chronic rejection develops over months/years.
*   **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, graft tenderness. Classic signs of acute graft dysfunction/rejection.
*   **Laboratory Findings:** Rising creatinine (4.5 mg/dL from 1.2 mg/dL). Indicates acute kidney injury/graft failure.
*   **Biopsy Findings:** Interstitial edema, lymphocytic infiltrates (small, dark cells = lymphocytes), tubular injury. Consistent with acute rejection.
*   **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. This finding is strongly associated with antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), but can also be seen in some forms of cellular rejection (especially T-cell mediated AMR or overlap syndromes). However, the predominant cellular infiltrate described points more strongly towards cellular rejection.
*   **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD8+ T cells expressing CD69. CD69 is an early activation marker for T cells. The increase in CD8+ cells suggests a cytotoxic T cell response.
*   **HLA Mismatch:** One HLA-B mismatch. This provides a target for alloimmune response.

The combination of timing (3 weeks), clinical signs of graft dysfunction, biopsy showing lymphocytic infiltrate and tubular injury, and increased activated CD8+ T cells strongly points towards acute cellular rejection. The C4d deposition might suggest an AMR component or T-cell mediated AMR, but the dominant cellular infiltrate and activated CD8+ T cells make direct CD8+ cytotoxicity the most likely primary mechanism.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** Primarily mediated by T lymphocytes recognizing donor MHC molecules (alloantigens) on graft cells.
    *   **CD4+ T cells:** Recognize donor MHC class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells (APCs) like dendritic cells and macrophages within the graft. They release cytokines (IFN-γ, TNF-α, IL-2) that activate macrophages and recruit other inflammatory cells, contributing to graft damage.
    *   **CD8+ T cells:** Recognize donor MHC class I molecules directly on graft parenchymal cells (e.g., tubular epithelial cells in the kidney). Upon recognition, they release cytotoxic granules (perforin, granzymes) and Fas ligand, inducing apoptosis (direct cell killing) of the target cells.
    *   **Pathology:** Infiltrates of lymphocytes (often T cells), endothelialitis, tubular injury, interstitial edema.
    *   **Timing:** Typically occurs within the first few weeks to months post-transplant.
*   **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** Mediated by antibodies (often pre-formed or de novo donor-specific antibodies - DSAs) binding to donor HLA antigens, primarily on endothelial cells.
    *   **Mechanisms:** Complement activation (classical pathway), antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) by NK cells, opsonization, and endothelial cell activation leading to thrombosis and ischemia.
    *   **Pathology:** Endothelialitis, microvascular inflammation (glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis), C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries, thrombosis.
    *   **C4d:** A marker of complement activation, often used to diagnose AMR, but its presence is not specific and can be seen in other conditions.
*   **Hyperacute Rejection:** Occurs within minutes to hours due to pre-existing antibodies (e.g., ABO, HLA) against the donor. Leads to rapid thrombosis and graft necrosis. Not consistent with the 3-week timeframe.
*   **Chronic Rejection:** Occurs over months to years. Characterized by fibrosis, graft arteriosclerosis (vascular changes), and progressive loss of graft function. Not consistent with the acute presentation.

In this case, the biopsy shows lymphocytic infiltrates and tubular injury, and flow cytometry shows activated CD8+ T cells. While C4d deposition is present (suggesting possible AMR component), the predominant cellular infiltrate and the specific finding of activated CD8+ T cells strongly implicate CD8+ cytotoxic T cell-mediated rejection as a major mechanism. CD8+ T cells directly target and kill graft parenchymal cells (tubular epithelial cells in the kidney), explaining the tubular injury and graft dysfunction.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Antibody-mediated rejection involves pre-formed DSAs binding to endothelial cells, causing complement activation and endothelial injury. While C4d deposition is present, the biopsy description emphasizes lymphocytic infiltrates and tubular injury, and the flow cytometry highlights activated CD8+ T cells, making direct cytotoxicity a more prominent feature than typical AMR. AMR typically presents with more prominent endothelialitis and microvascular inflammation. This option focuses on the antibody mechanism, which is less likely the *primary* mechanism given the overall picture, although AMR can coexist.
- (B): CD4+ T cells are involved in ACR, but they primarily act by releasing cytokines and activating macrophages, contributing to inflammation and indirect damage. The key finding here is the increase in activated CD8+ T cells and tubular injury, suggesting direct cytotoxicity by CD8+ cells is the predominant mechanism. While CD4+ cells contribute, CD8+ cells are the main effectors of direct parenchymal cell killing.
- (D): Chronic rejection is a long-term process characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes (graft arteriosclerosis). The patient's presentation is acute (3 weeks post-transplant) with signs of acute inflammation and cellular injury, not chronic changes.
- (E): Hyperacute rejection occurs immediately (minutes to hours) due to pre-existing antibodies, leading to rapid thrombosis. This patient's rejection occurred at 3 weeks, ruling out hyperacute rejection.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR) | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) | Hyperacute Rejection | Chronic Rejection |
| :------------------ | :----------------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :------------------- | :---------------- |
| **Timing**          | Days to months                 | Days to months                     | Minutes to hours     | Months to years   |
| **Mediator**        | T lymphocytes (CD4+, CD8+)      | Antibodies (DSAs)                  | Pre-formed Abs      | T cells, Abs      |
| **Primary Target**  | Parenchymal cells, endothelium | Endothelium                        | Endothelium          | Vasculature       |
| **Key Pathology**   | Lymphocytic infiltrate, tubular injury, endothelialitis | Endothelialitis, C4d deposition, microvascular inflammation | Thrombosis, necrosis | Fibrosis, arteriosclerosis |
| **Key Cells**       | CD8+ CTLs, CD4+ Th cells        | B cells, NK cells                  | N/A                 | T cells, Fibroblasts |
| **C4d Deposition**  | Possible, but less specific     | Characteristic                     | N/A                 | Possible          |
| **Patient Scenario** | 3 weeks post-transplant, rising creatinine, lymphocytic infiltrate, activated CD8+ T cells | Similar timing, but often more prominent endothelialitis, C4d | Immediate graft failure | Gradual graft failure |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute cellular rejection is primarily mediated by T cells (CD8+ cytotoxic T cells directly killing graft cells, CD4+ helper T cells orchestrating inflammation). The timing (weeks post-transplant), biopsy findings (lymphocytic infiltrate, tubular injury), and specific immunological markers (activated CD8+ T cells) are key features. Differentiate ACR from AMR (antibody-mediated, endothelialitis, C4d deposition), hyperacute rejection (immediate, pre-formed antibodies), and chronic rejection (long-term, fibrosis).

<a id='question-009'></a>

### Question 009: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 09 (Answer)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of T-cell help in B-cell activation and antibody production, specifically the requirement for CD40L on T cells binding to CD40 on B cells for isotype switching and affinity maturation, and the consequences of this interaction's disruption in transplantation. (理解T细胞辅助B细胞活化和抗体生成中的作用，特别是T细胞上的CD40L与B细胞上的CD40结合对于同型转换和亲和力成熟的重要性，以及这种相互作用在移植中的中断后果。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor. He is started on standard immunosuppressive therapy, including a calcineurin inhibitor (cyclosporine), an mTOR inhibitor (sirolimus), and mycophenolate mofetil. Six months post-transplant, he presents with recurrent fever, chills, and malaise. Laboratory tests reveal a significantly elevated serum creatinine, indicating graft dysfunction. Urinalysis shows proteinuria. A kidney biopsy is performed, revealing acute tubular necrosis and evidence of antibody-mediated rejection (AMR). Immunofluorescence staining shows C4d deposition in the peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood B cells shows a marked decrease in memory B cells and a significant reduction in IgG subclasses, particularly IgG3 and IgG4, while IgM levels are normal. The patient's serum is tested for donor-specific antibodies (DSA) against HLA class II antigens, which are found to be positive at high titers. The patient is subsequently treated with intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIG) and rituximab, leading to clinical improvement and resolution of the AMR.

Which of the following immunological mechanisms is most directly responsible for the observed reduction in IgG subclass switching and affinity maturation in this patient?

(A) Inhibition of B-cell receptor (BCR) signaling due to cyclosporine.
(B) Impaired T-cell help mediated by the absence of functional CD40 ligand (CD40L) expression on T cells.
(C) Direct cytotoxic effect of sirolimus on B cells, preventing their differentiation into plasma cells.
(D) Suppression of interleukin-4 (IL-4) production by regulatory T cells (Tregs), hindering B-cell class switching to IgE.
(E) Mycophenolate mofetil-induced inhibition of inosine monophosphate dehydrogenase (IMPDH), preventing B-cell proliferation.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, kidney transplant recipient, standard immunosuppression (cyclosporine, sirolimus, mycophenolate mofetil). This sets the stage for potential complications related to immunosuppression, including infection and rejection.
- **Presentation:** Recurrent fever, chills, malaise, graft dysfunction (elevated creatinine, proteinuria) 6 months post-transplant. Suggests rejection or infection.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Acute tubular necrosis, antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), C4d deposition. Confirms AMR as the cause of graft dysfunction. C4d deposition is a marker of complement activation, often seen in AMR.
- **Immunological Findings:** Decreased memory B cells, reduced IgG3/IgG4, normal IgM, positive high-titer DSA against HLA class II. This pattern is characteristic of Hyper-IgM syndrome, specifically X-linked Hyper-IgM syndrome (HIGM1), which is caused by defects in CD40L. The immunosuppressive drugs used (cyclosporine, sirolimus, mycophenolate) can sometimes mimic or exacerbate aspects of this condition, particularly concerning T-cell help for B cells. The key finding is the failure of IgG subclass switching (IgM normal, IgG3/4 low).
- **Treatment & Outcome:** IVIG and rituximab improve the condition. Rituximab depletes CD20+ B cells, which can paradoxically help in some AMR scenarios by removing autoreactive B cells, but it doesn't address the underlying T-cell help defect. IVIG provides passive immunity. The underlying defect causing the failure of IgG switching is the core question.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism responsible for the reduced IgG subclass switching and affinity maturation. This points directly to the T-cell help required for these processes.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **T-cell Help for B Cells:** B-cell activation, proliferation, isotype switching, and affinity maturation require interaction with activated T helper cells (specifically CD4+ T cells).
- **CD40L-CD40 Interaction:** A crucial interaction occurs when CD40 ligand (CD40L, also known as CD154) expressed on activated T cells binds to CD40 expressed on B cells. This interaction is essential for:
    - **Isotype Switching:** Switching from IgM/IgD to other isotypes (IgG, IgA, IgE). Germinal center B cells upregulate CD40, and T follicular helper (Tfh) cells express CD40L.
    - **Affinity Maturation:** Somatic hypermutation of immunoglobulin genes followed by selection of B cells with higher affinity receptors, occurring in germinal centers.
    - **Germinal Center Formation:** CD40L signaling promotes the formation of germinal centers where these processes occur.
- **Hyper-IgM Syndromes:** Primary immunodeficiencies characterized by normal or elevated IgM levels but low levels of IgG, IgA, and IgE. The most common form (X-linked Hyper-IgM syndrome, HIGM1) is caused by mutations in the *CD40LG* gene, leading to a deficiency or dysfunction of CD40L on T cells. This prevents the necessary T-cell help for B-cell isotype switching and affinity maturation.
- **Transplantation Context:** While the patient has a primary immunodeficiency (implied by the specific pattern of antibody deficiency), the immunosuppressive drugs used can also impair T-cell function, potentially exacerbating the defect in T-cell help. However, the core defect leading to the *specific* pattern of reduced IgG switching and affinity maturation (normal IgM) is the lack of functional CD40L-CD40 interaction. The clinical picture presented (AMR with specific antibody deficiency pattern) strongly suggests a defect in T-cell help for B cells, mimicking Hyper-IgM syndrome.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Inhibition of B-cell receptor (BCR) signaling due to cyclosporine.** Cyclosporine inhibits calcineurin, which is required for T-cell activation (specifically IL-2 production) and also affects B-cell function to some extent. However, its primary effect is on T cells, and while it can impair B-cell responses, it doesn't specifically block the CD40L-CD40 interaction required for isotype switching. The hallmark of cyclosporine effect is impaired T-cell help, which *can* lead to reduced antibody production, but not the specific pattern of normal IgM with low IgG subclasses seen here.
- (C) **Direct cytotoxic effect of sirolimus on B cells, preventing their differentiation into plasma cells.** Sirolimus (rapamycin) is an mTOR inhibitor that primarily affects T-cell proliferation and function. It does not have a direct cytotoxic effect on B cells. While it can impair overall immune responses, it doesn't specifically target the CD40L-CD40 pathway responsible for isotype switching.
- (D) **Suppression of interleukin-4 (IL-4) production by regulatory T cells (Tregs), hindering B-cell class switching to IgE.** IL-4 is crucial for B-cell switching to IgE. While Tregs can suppress immune responses, and IL-4 plays a role in switching, this option focuses specifically on IgE switching and Tregs. The vignette describes a broader defect in IgG switching (IgG3/IgG4 are low), not just IgE, and the primary defect is the lack of T-cell help via CD40L-CD40, not specifically IL-4 suppression by Tregs.
- (E) **Mycophenolate mofetil-induced inhibition of inosine monophosphate dehydrogenase (IMPDH), preventing B-cell proliferation.** Mycophenolate mofetil inhibits IMPDH, an enzyme crucial for purine synthesis, primarily affecting rapidly proliferating lymphocytes (both T and B cells). This leads to reduced overall lymphocyte numbers and function. While it impairs B-cell proliferation, it doesn't specifically target the CD40L-CD40 interaction required for isotype switching and affinity maturation. The specific pattern of normal IgM with low IgG subclasses points to a defect in the *quality* of T-cell help (isotype switching) rather than just a general reduction in B-cell numbers due to impaired proliferation.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Correct Answer (B) - CD40L Deficiency | Option (A) - Cyclosporine Effect | Option (C) - Sirolimus Effect | Option (D) - IL-4/Tregs | Option (E) - Mycophenolate Effect |
|-------------------------|----------------------------------------|---------------------------------|--------------------------------|------------------------|-----------------------------------|
| **Primary Mechanism**    | Lack of T-cell help via CD40L-CD40     | Impaired T-cell activation      | Impaired T-cell proliferation   | Impaired IgE switching | Impaired lymphocyte proliferation |
| **IgM Levels**          | Normal or High                        | Variable, often reduced         | Variable, often reduced         | Normal                 | Variable, often reduced         |
| **IgG Levels**          | Low (especially IgG3/IgG4)             | Low                             | Low                             | Variable               | Low                             |
| **IgA/IgE Levels**      | Low                                    | Low                             | Low                             | Low (specifically IgE) | Low                             |
| **Affinity Maturation** | Impaired                               | Impaired                        | Impaired                        | Impaired               | Impaired                        |
| **Cellular Target**     | B cells (indirectly via T cells)       | T cells                         | T cells                         | B cells (indirectly)   | T & B cells                      |
| **Key Pathway**         | CD40L-CD40                             | Calcineurin/IL-2                | mTOR                            | IL-4 signaling         | IMPDH                             |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The CD40L-CD40 interaction is essential for T-cell dependent B-cell activation, including isotype switching and affinity maturation. Defects in this pathway (e.g., X-linked Hyper-IgM syndrome) result in normal/high IgM but low IgG, IgA, and IgE. Immunosuppressive drugs can impair T-cell function, potentially mimicking or exacerbating this defect, but the specific pattern of antibody deficiency points to the underlying requirement for CD40L-CD40 signaling. Key differentials include other primary immunodeficiencies affecting T-cell help or B-cell intrinsic defects, and the effects of immunosuppressive drugs.

<a id='question-010'></a>

### Question 010: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 10 (questions)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying acute rejection in organ transplantation, specifically the role of T cells (CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes and CD4+ helper T cells) and antibodies (pre-formed or de novo) in mediating graft damage. (理解器官移植中急性排斥的免疫机制，特别是T细胞（CD8+细胞毒性T淋巴细胞和CD4+辅助T细胞）和抗体（预先存在的或新产生的）在介导移植物损伤中的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease secondary to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an unknown HLA type. The patient is started on standard immunosuppression consisting of tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals diffuse interstitial edema, inflammatory cell infiltrates predominantly composed of lymphocytes, and evidence of tubular epithelial cell necrosis. Immunofluorescence microscopy shows deposition of C4d in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the peripheral blood shows a significant increase in CD8+ T cells and CD4+ T cells, with evidence of recent T cell activation (CD69+). Serum analysis reveals the presence of donor-specific antibodies (DSA) against HLA class I antigens.

Which of the following immunological mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for this patient's acute kidney transplant rejection?

(A) Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) due to pre-formed DSAs binding to complement receptors on endothelial cells, leading to endothelial injury and thrombosis.
(B) Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) due to de novo DSAs binding to complement receptors on endothelial cells, leading to endothelial injury and thrombosis.
(C) T cell-mediated rejection (TCMR) primarily mediated by CD4+ helper T cells recognizing donor MHC class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells, leading to cytokine release and inflammation.
(D) T cell-mediated rejection (TCMR) primarily mediated by CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes recognizing donor MHC class I molecules on graft cells, leading to direct cell lysis.
(E) Chronic antibody-mediated rejection (CAMR) characterized by transplant glomerulopathy and peritubular capillaritis, typically occurring months to years post-transplant.

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease (ESRD) from diabetes, received kidney transplant. This sets the stage for transplant rejection.
- **Timing:** Three weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is characteristic of acute rejection (either T-cell mediated or antibody-mediated).
- **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated creatinine. These are classic signs of acute graft dysfunction, suggesting rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Diffuse interstitial edema, lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular epithelial cell necrosis. These findings are consistent with acute rejection, but can be seen in both TCMR and AMR.
- **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. C4d is a stable breakdown product of the classical complement pathway (C3 convertase). Its presence indicates recent complement activation, a hallmark of antibody-mediated rejection (AMR).
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD8+ and CD4+ T cells with activation markers (CD69+). This suggests a T cell response is also occurring, which is common in acute rejection, but doesn't exclude AMR.
- **Serum Analysis:** Presence of donor-specific antibodies (DSA) against HLA class I antigens. This is the definitive evidence for antibody-mediated rejection. The fact that the donor HLA type was unknown suggests the patient may have had pre-existing DSAs or developed them rapidly post-transplant (de novo).

The combination of clinical signs of acute graft dysfunction, biopsy findings suggestive of acute rejection, C4d deposition (indicating complement activation), and the presence of DSAs strongly points towards antibody-mediated rejection (AMR). The DSAs bind to donor HLA antigens on the graft endothelium, activating complement and triggering an inflammatory cascade leading to endothelial injury, microvascular inflammation (peritubular capillaritis), and graft dysfunction. The timing (3 weeks) is typical for acute AMR, which can occur due to pre-formed DSAs (e.g., from prior transplants, pregnancies, or blood transfusions) or develop rapidly post-transplant (de novo DSAs).

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Transplant Rejection Types:**
    - **Acute Rejection:** Occurs days to months post-transplant. Can be T-cell mediated (TCMR) or antibody-mediated (AMR).
        - **TCMR:** Primarily mediated by recipient T cells recognizing donor MHC molecules. CD4+ T cells recognize MHC class II on APCs, releasing cytokines (IFN-γ, TNF-α) that activate macrophages and recruit other inflammatory cells. CD8+ T cells recognize MHC class I on graft cells, leading to direct cytotoxicity (perforin/granzyme release) and apoptosis of graft cells. Histology shows lymphocytic infiltrates, interstitial edema, and tubular injury.
        - **AMR:** Mediated by recipient antibodies (DSAs) against donor HLA antigens. DSAs bind to HLA class I or II antigens on graft endothelial cells. This activates the classical complement pathway (C1q, C4, C2, C3, C5b-9) leading to endothelial cell injury, inflammation, and thrombosis. C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries is a key histological marker. DSAs can be pre-formed (present before transplant) or de novo (develop after transplant). Histology shows microvascular inflammation (peritubular capillaritis), endothelialitis, and sometimes thrombosis.
    - **Chronic Rejection:** Occurs months to years post-transplant. Characterized by fibrosis, vascular changes (chronic transplant vasculopathy), and progressive loss of graft function. Can involve both cellular and humoral immunity. Chronic AMR (CAMR) is a specific form characterized by transplant glomerulopathy and peritubular capillaritis.

- **Key Molecules:**
    - **MHC Class I (HLA-A, B, C):** Expressed on all nucleated cells. Recognized by CD8+ T cells.
    - **MHC Class II (HLA-DR, DQ, DP):** Expressed primarily on APCs (dendritic cells, macrophages, B cells). Recognized by CD4+ T cells.
    - **Complement System:** Activated by antibody binding to antigens (classical pathway). C4d is a marker of classical pathway activation.
    - **DSAs:** Antibodies specific for donor HLA antigens.

- **Mechanism of AMR:** DSAs bind to donor HLA antigens on endothelial cells -> Activate classical complement pathway -> C3 convertase formation -> C3 cleavage -> C4d deposition -> C5 convertase formation -> MAC formation (C5b-9) -> Endothelial cell injury/necrosis -> Leukocyte recruitment -> Inflammation -> Thrombosis -> Graft dysfunction.

##### 3. Role of C4d:** C4d is a breakdown product of C4b, a component of the classical complement pathway. Its deposition in peritubular capillaries is a strong indicator of complement activation mediated by antibody binding to the endothelium, a key feature of AMR. While C4d deposition can sometimes be seen in TCMR, its presence in the context of DSAs is highly specific for AMR.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) due to *pre-formed* DSAs binding to complement receptors on endothelial cells, leading to endothelial injury and thrombosis. This is plausible, as pre-formed DSAs are a common cause of AMR. However, the vignette doesn't explicitly state the DSAs were pre-formed. The patient had an unknown donor HLA type, which could imply pre-existing sensitization, but it's also possible the DSAs developed rapidly post-transplant (de novo). Option (B) is more general and encompasses both possibilities. The key finding is the presence of DSAs and C4d deposition, indicating AMR, regardless of whether they were pre-formed or de novo. While pre-formed DSAs are a major cause, de novo DSAs are also significant, especially in the early post-transplant period. Therefore, (B) is a better fit as it doesn't restrict the mechanism to only pre-formed antibodies.
- (C): T cell-mediated rejection (TCMR) primarily mediated by CD4+ helper T cells recognizing donor MHC class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells, leading to cytokine release and inflammation. While CD4+ T cells are involved in both TCMR and AMR (by activating macrophages and B cells), and the flow cytometry showed increased CD4+ T cells, the presence of DSAs and C4d deposition points strongly towards AMR as the primary mechanism. TCMR typically lacks significant C4d deposition and DSAs.
- (D): T cell-mediated rejection (TCMR) primarily mediated by CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes recognizing donor MHC class I molecules on graft cells, leading to direct cell lysis. CD8+ T cells are also involved in TCMR and were increased in the flow cytometry. However, like option (C), the definitive evidence of DSAs and C4d deposition makes AMR the most likely primary mechanism. TCMR is characterized by direct cellular cytotoxicity, not antibody-mediated complement activation.
- (E): Chronic antibody-mediated rejection (CAMR) characterized by transplant glomerulopathy and peritubular capillaritis, typically occurring months to years post-transplant. The patient is only three weeks post-transplant, which is too early for chronic rejection. CAMR is a late complication.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Acute T-cell Mediated Rejection (TCMR) | Acute Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) | Chronic Antibody-Mediated Rejection (CAMR) |
|-------------------------|-----------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------|
| **Timing**              | Days to months                         | Days to months                         | Months to years                           |
| **Primary Mediators**   | T cells (CD4+, CD8+)                    | Antibodies (DSAs)                       | Antibodies (DSAs)                         |
| **Key Histology**       | Lymphocytic infiltrates, edema, necrosis | Microvascular inflammation (capillaritis), endothelialitis, thrombosis | Transplant glomerulopathy, capillaritis, fibrosis |
| **Key Immunofluorescence**| Usually negative for C4d                | C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries | C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries |
| **Key Lab Finding**     | Increased T cell activation markers     | Presence of DSAs                        | Presence of DSAs                          |
| **Mechanism**           | Direct cell lysis, cytokine release     | Complement activation, endothelial injury | Chronic inflammation, fibrosis, vasculopathy |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute rejection in transplantation can be T-cell mediated (TCMR) or antibody-mediated (AMR). Key distinguishing features of AMR include the presence of donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) and C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries, indicating complement activation. TCMR is characterized by T cell infiltrates and lacks these specific markers. Chronic rejection occurs later and involves fibrosis and vascular changes. Differentiating between acute TCMR and AMR is crucial for appropriate management.

<a id='question-011'></a>

### Question 011: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 11 (versus)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying graft rejection, specifically the role of T cells (CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes and CD4+ helper T cells) in recognizing foreign MHC molecules on the graft and initiating an immune response, leading to direct cell-mediated cytotoxicity and indirect antigen presentation. (理解移植物排斥的免疫机制，特别是T细胞（CD8+细胞毒性T淋巴细胞和CD4+辅助T细胞）识别移植物上外源MHC分子并启动免疫应答的作用，导致直接细胞介导的细胞毒性和间接抗原呈递。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an unknown HLA type. The patient is started on standard immunosuppressive therapy including tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals acute tubular necrosis and interstitial inflammation with infiltration of mononuclear cells, including lymphocytes with cytotoxic granules. Immunofluorescence staining shows C4d deposition in the peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the peripheral blood shows a significant increase in CD8+ T cells expressing granzyme B.

Which of the following mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for the patient's acute kidney graft rejection?

(A) Antibody-mediated rejection involving pre-formed donor-specific antibodies (DSA) binding to endothelial cells.
(B) Chronic rejection characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes due to long-term immune activation.
(C) Acute cellular rejection mediated by recipient T cells recognizing foreign MHC molecules on graft cells.
(D) Opportunistic infection with Cytomegalovirus (CMV) causing CMV nephritis.
(E) Drug-induced nephrotoxicity secondary to the immunosuppressive regimen.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the context for potential graft rejection or complications.
- **Immunosuppression:** Standard triple therapy (tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, prednisone) is used, indicating an attempt to prevent rejection, but rejection can still occur.
- **Timing:** Three weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is typical for acute rejection, particularly acute cellular rejection (ACR). Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) can also occur acutely but often involves pre-existing or rapidly developing DSAs.
- **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated creatinine. These are classic signs of acute graft dysfunction, consistent with rejection or infection.
- **Renal Biopsy Findings:** Acute tubular necrosis (ATN) and interstitial inflammation with mononuclear cell infiltration (lymphocytes with cytotoxic granules). This points towards an inflammatory process damaging the graft tissue. The presence of cytotoxic lymphocytes strongly suggests a cell-mediated immune response.
- **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. While C4d deposition is a hallmark of AMR (indicating complement activation), it can also be seen in some forms of ACR, particularly in cases of mixed rejection or as a non-specific finding. However, the predominant histological findings (lymphocytic infiltration with cytotoxic granules) and the flow cytometry data are more indicative of cellular rejection.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD8+ T cells expressing granzyme B. Granzyme B is a key effector molecule released by cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) to induce apoptosis in target cells. This finding strongly supports a cell-mediated cytotoxic process targeting graft cells.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely mechanism. Given the constellation of findings (timing, symptoms, biopsy showing cytotoxic lymphocytes, increased CD8+ granzyme B+ cells), acute cellular rejection is the most fitting diagnosis.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Transplantation Immunology:** Graft rejection is an immune response against foreign antigens (primarily MHC molecules) on the transplanted organ.
- **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** This is primarily mediated by T lymphocytes.
    - **Mechanism:** Recipient T cells (both CD4+ helper T cells and CD8+ cytotoxic T cells) recognize foreign MHC molecules (alloantigens) presented by graft antigen-presenting cells (APCs) or graft cells themselves.
    - **CD4+ T cells:** Recognize foreign MHC class II molecules on APCs. They become activated, proliferate, and release cytokines (e.g., IFN-γ, TNF-α) that activate macrophages and promote inflammation, and they can also help activate CD8+ T cells.
    - **CD8+ T cells:** Recognize foreign MHC class I molecules expressed on virtually all nucleated graft cells. Upon activation, they directly kill graft cells expressing the foreign MHC I via cytotoxic mechanisms involving perforin and granzymes (like granzyme B). Granzyme B enters the target cell and activates caspases, leading to apoptosis.
    - **Histopathology:** Infiltration of lymphocytes (primarily T cells) into the graft parenchyma, often associated with endothelialitis (inflammation of blood vessels) and parenchymal cell damage (e.g., tubular necrosis).
    - **Timing:** Typically occurs within the first few months post-transplant.
- **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** Mediated by antibodies (primarily DSAs) directed against donor HLA antigens.
    - **Mechanism:** DSAs bind to MHC molecules on graft endothelial cells, activating complement and recruiting inflammatory cells (neutrophils, macrophages). This leads to endothelial injury, microvascular inflammation (microangiopathy), thrombosis, and graft ischemia.
    - **Hallmarks:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries (indicating complement activation), endothelialitis, microvascular inflammation (glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis).
- **Chronic Rejection:** Occurs months to years post-transplant. Characterized by progressive fibrosis, vascular changes (e.g., transplant glomerulopathy, chronic active antibody-mediated rejection), and eventual graft failure. It involves both cellular and humoral immune responses.
- **CMV Nephritis:** CMV infection is a common complication post-transplant, especially in patients on immunosuppression. It can cause graft dysfunction.
    - **Diagnosis:** Requires demonstration of CMV infection (e.g., CMV PCR in blood or tissue, CMV immunohistochemistry in biopsy).
    - **Histopathology:** Characteristic viral cytopathic effects (inclusion bodies), interstitial inflammation.
- **Drug-Induced Nephrotoxicity:** Immunosuppressants (especially calcineurin inhibitors like tacrolimus) can be directly toxic to the kidneys.
    - **Diagnosis:** Often based on clinical context, drug levels, and exclusion of other causes.
    - **Histopathology:** Can show ATN, interstitial fibrosis, arteriolar hyalinosis.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Antibody-mediated rejection involving pre-formed donor-specific antibodies (DSA) binding to endothelial cells.** While C4d deposition is present, which can suggest AMR, the biopsy findings (lymphocytic infiltration with cytotoxic granules) and the flow cytometry data (increased CD8+ granzyme B+ cells) are much more characteristic of ACR. Pre-formed DSAs are less common in deceased donor transplants compared to living related transplants or sensitized patients. The primary mechanism described here points strongly towards T-cell mediated cytotoxicity.
- (B) **Chronic rejection characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes due to long-term immune activation.** The patient is only three weeks post-transplant. Chronic rejection develops over months to years. The biopsy findings described (acute tubular necrosis, interstitial inflammation) are features of acute rejection, not chronic fibrosis or vascular changes like transplant glomerulopathy.
- (D) **Opportunistic infection with Cytomegalovirus (CMV) causing CMV nephritis.** CMV infection is a possibility post-transplant, but the biopsy findings described (lymphocytic infiltration with cytotoxic granules, increased CD8+ granzyme B+ cells) are not typical for CMV nephritis, which usually shows viral inclusions and different inflammatory cell patterns. CMV would require specific diagnostic tests (PCR, IHC) for confirmation.
- (E) **Drug-induced nephrotoxicity secondary to the immunosuppressive regimen.** While immunosuppressants can cause nephrotoxicity, the biopsy findings of interstitial inflammation with cytotoxic lymphocytes and the flow cytometry data strongly point towards an immune-mediated process (rejection) rather than direct drug toxicity. Drug toxicity typically causes ATN or interstitial fibrosis without the specific inflammatory infiltrate described.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Acute Cellular Rejection (C)                                  | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (A)                               | Chronic Rejection (B)                                     | CMV Nephritis (D)                                        | Drug Toxicity (E)                                       |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------------------------ | :-------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------ |
| **Timing**              | Days to months (typically < 3 months)                         | Days to months                                               | Months to years                                           | Variable, often weeks to months post-transplant           | Variable, can be early or late                           |
| **Primary Mediator**    | T cells (CD4+, CD8+)                                          | Antibodies (DSAs), Complement                                 | T cells, Antibodies, Fibrosis                              | Virus (CMV)                                              | Direct drug effect                                       |
| **Key Histopathology**  | Lymphocytic infiltrate, endothelialitis, parenchymal damage | Endothelialitis, Microvascular inflammation (glomerulitis), C4d | Fibrosis, Vascular changes (transplant glomerulopathy)     | Viral inclusions, Interstitial inflammation              | ATN, Interstitial fibrosis, Arteriolar hyalinosis        |
| **Key Lab Finding**     | Increased CD8+ Granzyme B+ cells                              | DSAs, C4d deposition                                          | Gradual decline in graft function, Biopsy findings        | CMV PCR/IHC positive                                      | Drug levels, Exclusion of other causes                   |
| **Mechanism**           | T cell recognition of allo-MHC, direct cytotoxicity            | Antibody binding to allo-MHC, complement activation, ADCC    | Chronic inflammation, Fibrosis, Vascular remodeling        | Viral replication, Cell lysis, Immune response to virus | Direct tubular or vascular damage                        |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute cellular rejection is primarily mediated by recipient T cells recognizing foreign MHC molecules on the graft, leading to direct cytotoxicity (CD8+) and inflammation (CD4+). Key diagnostic clues include the timing (within the first few months), biopsy findings (lymphocytic infiltrate, endothelialitis), and evidence of T-cell activation (e.g., increased CD8+ granzyme B+ cells). Differentiating ACR from AMR requires careful evaluation of clinical presentation, biopsy findings (especially C4d deposition and microvascular inflammation), and serology (presence of DSAs). CMV nephritis and drug toxicity are important differentials for acute graft dysfunction but have distinct pathological features.

<a id='question-012'></a>

### Question 012: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 12 (disease)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathogenesis and clinical presentation of Epstein-Barr Virus (EBV)-associated post-transplant lymphoproliferative disorder (PTLD), specifically the role of EBV latency proteins and T-cell dysfunction in immunosuppressed patients. (理解EBV相关移植后淋巴增生性疾病(PTLD)的发病机制和临床表现，特别是EBV潜伏蛋白和免疫抑制患者中T细胞功能障碍的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male, status post-liver transplantation 18 months ago for alcoholic cirrhosis, presents with fever, fatigue, and rapidly enlarging lymphadenopathy in the neck and axillae. His immunosuppressive regimen includes tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. He denies recent travel or exposure to sick contacts. Physical examination reveals generalized lymphadenopathy, hepatosplenomegaly, and a 5 cm mass in the right parotid gland. Laboratory studies show elevated lactate dehydrogenase (LDH) and normal liver function tests. A biopsy of the cervical lymph node reveals a diffuse proliferation of atypical lymphoid cells with a morphology consistent with B-cells. Immunohistochemistry is positive for CD20, CD30, and LMP-1 (Latent Membrane Protein 1), and negative for CD3 and CD15. EBV serology shows positive IgG and IgM antibodies. Flow cytometry reveals a monoclonal B-cell population.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying mechanism contributing to the development of this patient's condition?

(A) Reactivation of Cytomegalovirus (CMV) leading to endothelial damage and graft rejection.
(B) Impaired T-cell surveillance against EBV-infected B-cells due to immunosuppression.
(C) Development of acute cellular rejection mediated by donor-specific antibodies against HLA class II antigens.
(D) Overexpression of Fas ligand (FasL) on the surface of malignant B-cells, leading to apoptosis of surrounding T-cells.
(E) Production of IL-10 by regulatory T-cells (Tregs), suppressing the proliferation of EBV-specific cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs).

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, 18 months post-liver transplant for alcoholic cirrhosis. This establishes the context of immunosuppression and the time frame for potential complications.
- **Symptoms:** Fever, fatigue, rapidly enlarging lymphadenopathy (neck, axillae), parotid mass. These are classic signs of lymphoproliferative disease.
- **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, prednisone. This indicates significant T-cell and B-cell suppression, increasing susceptibility to opportunistic infections and malignancies.
- **Physical Exam:** Generalized lymphadenopathy, hepatosplenomegaly, parotid mass. Further supports widespread lymphoid proliferation.
- **Labs:** Elevated LDH (marker of cell turnover/lysis), normal LFTs (suggests the primary issue is not graft rejection or recurrence of liver disease).
- **Lymph Node Biopsy:** Diffuse proliferation of atypical B-cells (CD20+), CD30+, LMP-1+, CD3-, CD15-. This morphology and immunophenotype are highly suggestive of PTLD. LMP-1 is a key EBV latency protein. CD30 positivity is common in PTLD. CD3- and CD15- rule out Hodgkin lymphoma.
- **EBV Serology:** Positive IgG (past infection) and IgM (recent or ongoing infection/reactivation). EBV is strongly implicated.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Monoclonal B-cell population. Confirms the neoplastic nature of the proliferation.
- **Diagnosis:** The constellation of findings (post-transplant, immunosuppression, lymphoproliferation, B-cell phenotype, LMP-1 expression, EBV serology, monoclonal population) points strongly to EBV-associated Post-Transplant Lymphoproliferative Disorder (PTLD).

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **PTLD Pathogenesis:** PTLD is a spectrum of lymphoid proliferations occurring in transplant recipients, often driven by EBV infection/reactivation in the setting of immunosuppression.
- **EBV Role:** EBV infects B-cells. In immunocompetent individuals, EBV-infected B-cells are effectively controlled and eliminated by EBV-specific cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs).
- **Immunosuppression Impact:** Transplant recipients receive potent immunosuppressive drugs (like tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, prednisone) to prevent graft rejection. These drugs primarily target T-cells, impairing their ability to recognize and eliminate infected cells.
- **Mechanism in PTLD:** Reduced T-cell surveillance allows EBV-infected B-cells to escape apoptosis and proliferate uncontrollably. The EBV genome encodes several latent proteins, including LMP-1, which promotes B-cell proliferation and survival by mimicking CD40 signaling and inhibiting apoptosis. The lack of effective CTL response is the critical failure allowing this process.
- **Kaplan Review:** Kaplan Preclinical Immunology and Microbiology Review 2023, pp. 142-154, details PTLD pathogenesis, emphasizing the role of T-cell suppression and EBV latency proteins.
- **First Aid 2025:** First Aid 2025 (Immunology & Pharmacology: Transplant Immunology & Immunosuppression) discusses the increased risk of malignancies, including PTLD, in transplant patients due to immunosuppression and the role of EBV.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): CMV reactivation is a common complication post-transplant, but it typically causes symptoms like fever, pneumonitis, colitis, or retinitis. While it can contribute to graft dysfunction, it doesn't directly cause the specific lymphoproliferative disorder described here with B-cell markers and LMP-1 expression. CMV does not cause LMP-1 expression.
- (C): Acute cellular rejection involves T-cell mediated attack on the graft, often triggered by donor HLA antigens. While possible in transplant patients, the clinical presentation (lymphadenopathy, parotid mass, B-cell proliferation, LMP-1) is not typical for acute cellular rejection, which primarily affects the transplanted organ itself. Donor-specific antibodies are characteristic of antibody-mediated rejection.
- (D): FasL overexpression on tumor cells is a mechanism used by some cancers to evade immune surveillance by inducing apoptosis in T-cells that recognize them. While this can occur in some lymphomas, it is not the primary underlying mechanism for PTLD. The fundamental problem in PTLD is the *lack* of effective T-cell killing due to immunosuppression, allowing EBV-infected B-cells to proliferate unchecked. LMP-1 promotes survival *against* Fas-mediated apoptosis.
- (E): IL-10 production by Tregs is a mechanism of immune suppression. While Tregs play a role in maintaining tolerance and preventing graft rejection, their primary role in PTLD pathogenesis is not the suppression of EBV-specific CTLs. The main issue is the *overall* suppression of T-cell function by immunosuppressive drugs, leading to a general inability to control EBV-infected cells, rather than specific suppression of anti-EBV CTLs by Tregs.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | EBV-PTLD (Correct)                                  | CMV Reactivation (A)                               | Acute Cellular Rejection (C)                       | FasL-mediated T-cell killing (D)                  | Treg-mediated CTL suppression (E)                 |
|----------------------|------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------|
| **Primary Driver**   | EBV infection/reactivation + Immunosuppression        | CMV infection                                      | T-cell response to donor HLA antigens              | Tumor cell evasion mechanism                      | Treg function                                      |
| **Cell Type**        | Malignant B-cells                                   | Infected endothelial cells, monocytes, etc.        | T-cells attacking graft cells                    | Tumor cells (various types)                        | Tregs                                              |
| **Key Markers**      | CD20+, CD30+, LMP-1+, Monoclonal B-cell population | CMV inclusions, viral PCR                         | Infiltrating T-cells in graft biopsy              | FasL on tumor cells                                | CD4+CD25+FoxP3+ Tregs                             |
| **Clinical Manifest**| Lymphadenopathy, masses, organ involvement           | Fever, pneumonitis, colitis, retinitis              | Graft dysfunction, tenderness, biopsy findings   | Variable, depends on tumor type                    | Immune regulation, graft tolerance                 |
| **Mechanism**        | Impaired T-cell control of EBV+ B-cells              | Direct viral cytopathic effects, inflammation      | T-cell mediated cytotoxicity against graft cells | Induction of T-cell apoptosis                      | Suppression of effector T-cell responses            |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
PTLD is a major complication of solid organ transplantation, driven by EBV infection in the context of profound T-cell immunosuppression. The lack of effective T-cell surveillance allows EBV-infected B-cells to proliferate, often expressing EBV latency proteins like LMP-1. Key differentials include other post-transplant malignancies, opportunistic infections (like CMV), and graft rejection. Understanding the interplay between immunosuppression, EBV, and T-cell function is crucial.

<a id='question-013'></a>

### Question 013: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 13 (OVERVIEW)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological principles underlying organ transplantation, specifically the role of MHC matching, T-cell mediated rejection (acute cellular rejection), and the mechanisms of immunosuppression used to prevent rejection. (理解器官移植的免疫学原理，特别是MHC匹配的作用、T细胞介导的排斥反应（急性细胞排斥）以及用于预防排斥的免疫抑制机制。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease secondary to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor. The donor's blood type is A+, and the recipient's blood type is O+. The donor-specific crossmatch (DSC) is negative. The recipient is started on a standard immunosuppressive regimen consisting of tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, the patient develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals diffuse interstitial inflammation with lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular epithelial cell damage, and endothelialitis. Immunofluorescence staining shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes shows a decrease in CD4+ T cells and an increase in CD8+ T cells.

Which of the following is the most likely mechanism responsible for this patient's acute kidney transplant rejection?

(A) Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) due to pre-formed anti-HLA antibodies.
(B) Acute cellular rejection (ACR) mediated by recipient T cells recognizing donor MHC class I and class II molecules.
(C) Chronic rejection characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes.
(D) Hyperacute rejection caused by pre-existing antibodies against ABO blood group antigens.
(E) Infection-related graft dysfunction due to cytomegalovirus (CMV) infection.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease (ESRD) from diabetes, receives kidney transplant. This sets the stage for transplant rejection.
- **Transplant Details:** Donor A+, Recipient O+. DSC negative. This indicates ABO compatibility (A is compatible with O) and absence of pre-formed antibodies against donor HLA antigens detected by the crossmatch test.
- **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus (calcineurin inhibitor), mycophenolate mofetil (purine synthesis inhibitor), prednisone (corticosteroid). Standard regimen, but rejection can still occur.
- **Timeline:** 3 weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is typical for acute rejection, particularly acute cellular rejection (ACR).
- **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated creatinine. Classic signs of acute graft dysfunction/rejection.
- **Renal Biopsy Findings:** Diffuse interstitial inflammation with lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular epithelial cell damage, endothelialitis. These are hallmark features of ACR. Endothelialitis (inflammation of endothelial cells) is particularly suggestive of T-cell mediated injury.
- **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. C4d is a breakdown product of the complement C4 component. Its deposition indicates complement activation, which can occur in both AMR and ACR, but is a key feature of AMR. However, in the context of strong cellular rejection findings (lymphocytic infiltrates, endothelialitis), it can also be present in ACR, sometimes referred to as "antibody-mediated cellular rejection" or "mixed rejection". The *predominant* histological features point strongly towards ACR.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Decrease in CD4+ T cells, increase in CD8+ T cells. This pattern is characteristic of cellular rejection, where cytotoxic CD8+ T cells infiltrate the graft and cause damage.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *most likely mechanism*. Given the constellation of findings (timing, symptoms, biopsy showing lymphocytic infiltrates and endothelialitis, flow cytometry showing CD8+ T cell increase), ACR mediated by T cells is the most fitting diagnosis.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Transplantation Immunology Overview:** Organ transplantation involves introducing foreign tissue (allograft) into a recipient. The recipient's immune system recognizes the donor tissue as foreign, primarily due to differences in Major Histocompatibility Complex (MHC) molecules (HLA in humans).
- **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** This is the most common type of acute rejection. It is primarily mediated by T lymphocytes (both CD4+ helper T cells and CD8+ cytotoxic T cells) from the recipient that recognize donor MHC class I and class II molecules on the graft cells.
    - **Mechanism:** Recipient T cells are activated by donor MHC molecules presented by antigen-presenting cells (APCs) in the graft or draining lymph nodes. Activated CD4+ T cells release cytokines (like IFN-γ, TNF-α) that recruit and activate other immune cells, including macrophages and CD8+ T cells. Activated CD8+ T cells directly kill graft cells expressing donor MHC class I.
    - **Histopathology:** Characterized by interstitial inflammation (lymphocytic infiltrates), tubular damage, and endothelialitis (inflammation of blood vessel endothelium).
    - **Timing:** Typically occurs within the first few weeks to months post-transplant.
    - **Flow Cytometry:** Often shows an increase in activated T cells, particularly CD8+ T cells, in the peripheral blood or graft infiltrate.
- **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** Mediated by antibodies (pre-formed or de novo) directed against donor HLA or non-HLA antigens on the graft endothelium.
    - **Mechanism:** Antibodies bind to the graft endothelium, activating complement and recruiting inflammatory cells (neutrophils, macrophages). This leads to endothelial damage, microvascular inflammation (microvasculitis), thrombosis, and graft ischemia.
    - **Histopathology:** Characterized by microvasculitis (inflammation of small blood vessels), C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries, and thrombosis.
    - **C4d:** A marker of complement activation (specifically the classical pathway), often found in AMR.
- **Hyperacute Rejection:** Occurs within minutes to hours post-transplant. Caused by pre-existing antibodies (e.g., anti-ABO, anti-HLA) in the recipient that bind to the graft endothelium, leading to rapid complement activation, thrombosis, and graft necrosis. Prevented by pre-transplant crossmatching.
- **Chronic Rejection:** Occurs months to years post-transplant. Characterized by gradual loss of graft function due to fibrosis, vascular changes (e.g., transplant glomerulopathy, chronic active antibody-mediated rejection), and parenchymal atrophy.
- **Infection:** CMV infection is a common complication post-transplant, especially in patients on immunosuppression. It can cause graft dysfunction (CMV nephritis) but typically presents with different histological features (viral inclusions, cellular necrosis) and is diagnosed via viral detection methods (PCR, immunohistochemistry).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) due to pre-formed anti-HLA antibodies.** While C4d deposition is present (a hallmark of AMR), the *predominant* histological findings (diffuse interstitial inflammation, lymphocytic infiltrates, endothelialitis) and the flow cytometry results (increase in CD8+ T cells) are more characteristic of ACR. Pre-formed anti-HLA antibodies would typically cause hyperacute or accelerated acute rejection, and the negative DSC makes this less likely, although de novo antibodies can cause AMR. The overall picture points more strongly to ACR.
- (C) **Chronic rejection characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes.** Chronic rejection occurs much later (months to years) and has distinct histological features (fibrosis, vascular thickening, parenchymal atrophy), which are not described in the biopsy findings. The patient is only 3 weeks post-transplant.
- (D) **Hyperacute rejection caused by pre-existing antibodies against ABO blood group antigens.** Hyperacute rejection occurs within minutes to hours. This patient is 3 weeks post-transplant. Furthermore, the donor (A+) and recipient (O+) are ABO compatible (O can receive A), so ABO antibodies are not the cause. The negative DSC also argues against pre-formed anti-HLA antibodies causing hyperacute rejection.
- (E) **Infection-related graft dysfunction due to cytomegalovirus (CMV) infection.** CMV infection can cause graft dysfunction, but the biopsy findings (lymphocytic infiltrates, endothelialitis) are not typical for CMV. CMV nephritis usually shows characteristic viral inclusions and cellular necrosis. While CMV is a common complication, the specific histological and immunological findings presented strongly favor ACR.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR) | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) | Hyperacute Rejection | Chronic Rejection | CMV Infection |
|----------------------|---------------------------------|-----------------------------------|----------------------|-------------------|---------------|
| **Timing**           | Days to months (typically weeks) | Days to months                    | Minutes to hours     | Months to years   | Weeks to months |
| **Mediator**         | T cells (CD4+, CD8+)             | Antibodies                        | Pre-formed Abs      | T cells, Abs      | Virus         |
| **Histology**        | Infiltrates, endothelialitis, tubular damage | Microvasculitis, C4d, thrombosis | Thrombosis, necrosis | Fibrosis, vascular changes | Inclusions, necrosis |
| **C4d Deposition**   | Possible, but less prominent     | Hallmark                          | Present              | Possible          | Absent        |
| **Flow Cytometry**   | Increased CD8+ T cells           | Variable                           | N/A                  | Variable          | N/A           |
| **ABO Compatibility**| Not relevant                    | Not relevant                      | Critical             | Not relevant      | Not relevant  |
| **DSC**              | Negative                        | Can be negative (de novo Abs)      | Positive             | Not relevant      | Not relevant  |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute kidney transplant rejection typically occurs within the first few weeks post-transplant. Acute cellular rejection (ACR) is the most common type and is characterized histologically by lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular damage, and endothelialitis, mediated by recipient T cells recognizing donor MHC molecules. Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) is characterized by microvasculitis and C4d deposition. Hyperacute rejection occurs immediately due to pre-formed antibodies. Chronic rejection occurs late and involves fibrosis. CMV infection is a common complication but has distinct histological features. The key is to correlate the timing, clinical presentation, biopsy findings, and immunological markers to differentiate between these entities.

<a id='question-014'></a>

### Question 014: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 14 (Transplantation)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying acute graft rejection, specifically the role of T cells (CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes and CD4+ helper T cells) and antibodies in recognizing foreign MHC molecules and initiating an immune response against the transplanted organ. (理解移植排斥反应的免疫机制，特别是T细胞（CD8+细胞毒性T淋巴细胞和CD4+辅助T细胞）和抗体在识别外源性MHC分子并启动对移植器官的免疫应答中的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an unknown HLA type. He is started on standard immunosuppressive therapy consisting of tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals diffuse interstitial edema, inflammatory cell infiltrates primarily composed of lymphocytes, and evidence of tubular injury. Immunofluorescence staining shows deposition of C4d in the peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the peripheral blood shows a significant increase in CD8+ T cells expressing activation markers (CD69, CD25). Serological testing reveals the presence of donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) against HLA class I antigens.

Which of the following immunological mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for this patient's acute graft rejection?

(A) Antibody-mediated complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) targeting donor endothelial cells.
(B) CD4+ T helper cell-mediated activation of macrophages leading to fibrosis.
(C) CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocyte (CTL)-mediated direct killing of donor tubular epithelial cells.
(D) Innate lymphoid cell (ILC) activation leading to cytokine storm and graft damage.
(E) Neutrophil-mediated acute tubular necrosis (ATN) due to ischemia-reperfusion injury.

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for potential graft rejection.
- **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus (calcineurin inhibitor), mycophenolate mofetil (purine synthesis inhibitor), prednisone (corticosteroid). Standard therapy, but rejection can still occur.
- **Timing:** Three weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is typical for acute cellular rejection (ACR) or antibody-mediated rejection (AMR).
- **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated creatinine. Classic signs of acute graft dysfunction, suggestive of rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Diffuse interstitial edema, lymphocyte infiltrates (primarily lymphocytes), tubular injury. These findings are characteristic of acute rejection, particularly ACR.
- **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. This indicates complement activation, often seen in AMR, but can also be present in ACR, especially if there is antibody involvement or overlap.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD8+ T cells with activation markers (CD69, CD25). This strongly points towards a cellular immune response, specifically involving cytotoxic T lymphocytes.
- **Serology:** Donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) against HLA class I antigens. This confirms the presence of antibodies against the donor graft, which can contribute to AMR or be present alongside ACR.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely immunological mechanism.

The combination of lymphocytic infiltrate (especially CD8+ T cells), tubular injury, and the presence of DSAs points towards a complex rejection process. However, the prominent increase in activated CD8+ T cells strongly implicates direct cellular cytotoxicity as a major mechanism. While DSAs and C4d deposition suggest an antibody-mediated component, the cellular findings are very specific for T-cell mediated rejection. Option (C) directly addresses the role of CD8+ CTLs, which are key players in acute cellular rejection, directly targeting graft cells.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Transplantation Immunology:** Graft rejection is an immune response against foreign MHC molecules (alloantigens) on the donor tissue.
- **Acute Rejection:** Occurs within days to months post-transplant. Can be cellular (ACR) or antibody-mediated (AMR), or a mixed pattern.
- **ACR:** Primarily mediated by T cells.
    - **CD4+ T cells:** Recognize foreign MHC class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells (APCs) in the graft. They differentiate into helper T cells (Th1, Th2, Th17) and secrete cytokines (IFN-γ, TNF-α, IL-2) that activate macrophages, recruit other inflammatory cells, and promote inflammation and tissue damage. They can also help activate CD8+ T cells.
    - **CD8+ T cells (CTLs):** Recognize foreign MHC class I molecules directly on graft cells (e.g., tubular epithelial cells, endothelial cells). Upon activation, they directly kill target cells via perforin/granzyme release or Fas/FasL interaction.
- **AMR:** Mediated by antibodies (DSAs) against donor HLA antigens.
    - **Mechanisms:**
        - **Complement-Dependent Cytotoxicity (CDC):** Antibodies bind to donor endothelial cells, activate the classical complement pathway, leading to membrane attack complex (MAC) formation and cell lysis. C4d deposition is a marker of complement activation.
        - **Antibody-Dependent Cell-Mediated Cytotoxicity (ADCC):** NK cells or other effector cells bind to the Fc portion of antibodies coating donor cells and kill them.
        - **Non-complementary mechanisms:** Antibody binding can also cause endothelial activation, inflammation, and thrombosis without direct complement activation.
- **Patient's Case:** The biopsy shows lymphocytic infiltrates (consistent with ACR) and C4d deposition (suggestive of AMR). The flow cytometry shows increased activated CD8+ T cells, strongly supporting a role for CTLs. The presence of DSAs confirms an antibody response. This suggests a mixed rejection pattern, but the prominent cellular findings (lymphocyte infiltrate, activated CD8+ T cells) make direct CTL killing a highly likely mechanism.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Antibody-mediated complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) targeting donor endothelial cells. While the patient has DSAs and C4d deposition (indicating complement activation), the biopsy primarily shows lymphocytic infiltrates and tubular injury, not predominantly endothelial damage. The strong CD8+ T cell activation points more towards cellular rejection. CDC is a key mechanism in AMR, but the overall picture here suggests a significant cellular component, potentially even dominant.
- (B) CD4+ T helper cell-mediated activation of macrophages leading to fibrosis. CD4+ T cells are involved in ACR, but their primary role is cytokine production and activation of other cells. While they contribute to inflammation, the direct killing of graft cells is primarily mediated by CD8+ T cells. Fibrosis is more characteristic of chronic rejection.
- (D) Innate lymphoid cell (ILC) activation leading to cytokine storm and graft damage. ILCs can contribute to inflammation and rejection, but they are not considered the primary mediators of acute T-cell mediated or antibody-mediated rejection in this classic scenario. The specific findings (lymphocytic infiltrate, activated CD8+ T cells, DSAs) point strongly towards adaptive immune responses (T cells and antibodies).
- (E) Neutrophil-mediated acute tubular necrosis (ATN) due to ischemia-reperfusion injury. Ischemia-reperfusion injury is a common cause of early graft dysfunction, but it typically occurs immediately post-transplant and is characterized by neutrophil infiltration and tubular damage, not the lymphocytic infiltrate and activated CD8+ T cells seen three weeks later. This patient's presentation is consistent with immune-mediated rejection, not primarily ischemic injury.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR) | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) | Ischemia-Reperfusion Injury |
|-------------------------|---------------------------------|-----------------------------------|------------------------------|
| **Primary Mediators**   | CD8+ CTLs, CD4+ T cells         | DSAs, Complement, NK cells       | Neutrophils                  |
| **Key Biopsy Findings** | Lymphocytic infiltrate, Tubulitis | Endothelialitis, C4d deposition, Thrombosis | Neutrophil infiltrate, Tubular damage |
| **Timing**              | Days to months                  | Days to months                  | Immediately post-transplant   |
| **Cellular Markers**    | Increased CD8+ T cells          | DSAs, C4d                        | N/A                          |
| **Mechanism**           | Direct cell killing, Cytokines  | CDC, ADCC, Inflammation          | Oxidative stress, Inflammation |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute graft rejection involves both cellular (T-cell mediated) and humoral (antibody-mediated) components. The presence of activated CD8+ T cells and lymphocytic infiltrates strongly suggests a role for cytotoxic T lymphocytes in directly killing graft cells, a hallmark of acute cellular rejection. While DSAs and C4d deposition indicate an antibody-mediated component, the cellular findings are prominent in this case. Differentiating between ACR and AMR, or recognizing mixed rejection patterns, is crucial for management. Key differentials include infection, drug toxicity, and ischemia-reperfusion injury.
