<a id='question-015'></a>

### Question 015: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 15 (process)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the cellular and molecular mechanisms underlying allograft rejection, specifically the roles of T cells (CD4+ helper T cells and CD8+ cytotoxic T cells), antigen-presenting cells (APCs), MHC molecules, and costimulatory signals in the process of acute cellular rejection. (理解移植物排斥反应的细胞和分子机制，特别是T细胞（CD4+辅助T细胞和CD8+细胞毒性T细胞）、抗原提呈细胞（APCs）、MHC分子和共刺激信号在急性细胞排斥过程中的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an unknown HLA type. He is started on standard immunosuppression consisting of tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals diffuse interstitial inflammation with lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular epithelial cell damage, and endothelialitis. Immunofluorescence staining shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the biopsy tissue shows a predominance of CD8+ T cells infiltrating the tubules and glomeruli, along with CD4+ T cells in the interstitium. Serum levels of donor-specific antibodies (DSA) are negative.

Which of the following mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for the observed acute kidney allograft rejection?

(A) Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) involving pre-formed DSAs binding to endothelial cells.
(B) Acute cellular rejection (ACR) mediated primarily by CD4+ T helper cells recognizing donor MHC class II molecules on APCs.
(C) Acute cellular rejection (ACR) mediated primarily by CD8+ cytotoxic T cells recognizing donor MHC class I molecules on graft cells.
(D) Chronic rejection characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes due to prolonged low-grade inflammation.
(E) Hyperacute rejection occurring within minutes to hours post-transplant due to pre-existing anti-donor ABO or HLA antibodies.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease (ESRD) from diabetes, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for potential graft rejection.
- **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus (calcineurin inhibitor), mycophenolate mofetil (purine synthesis inhibitor), prednisone (corticosteroid). Standard regimen, but rejection can still occur.
- **Timing:** 3 weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is characteristic of acute rejection episodes, particularly acute cellular rejection (ACR). Hyperacute rejection occurs much earlier (minutes/hours), and chronic rejection develops over months to years.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated creatinine. Classic signs and symptoms of acute graft dysfunction, suggestive of rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings:**
    - Diffuse interstitial inflammation with lymphocytic infiltrates: Hallmark of cellular rejection.
    - Tubular epithelial cell damage: Indicates direct attack on graft parenchyma.
    - Endothelialitis: Inflammation of blood vessel endothelium, can be seen in both ACR and AMR.
    - C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries: Suggests complement activation, often associated with AMR, but can also be seen in ACR, especially in the context of T-cell mediated endothelial damage. However, the *predominance* of cellular infiltrates points more strongly towards ACR.
    - Flow cytometry: Predominance of CD8+ T cells infiltrating tubules/glomeruli, CD4+ T cells in interstitium. This cellular profile is highly characteristic of ACR. CD8+ T cells directly kill graft cells expressing donor MHC class I, while CD4+ T cells help orchestrate the response and can also contribute to damage.
- **Serology:** Negative donor-specific antibodies (DSA). This makes antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) less likely as the primary mechanism, although DSAs can develop later or be present at low levels not detected by standard assays. The absence of pre-formed DSAs rules out hyperacute rejection.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *most likely* mechanism. Given the timing, clinical presentation, biopsy findings (especially the cellular infiltrate profile), and negative DSAs, ACR mediated by cytotoxic T cells is the most fitting explanation.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Transplantation Immunology Overview:** Allograft rejection is an immune response against foreign MHC molecules (alloantigens) expressed on the donor graft. The immune system recognizes these as non-self.
- **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** Primarily mediated by T lymphocytes.
    - **Sensitization Phase:** Recipient APCs (dendritic cells, macrophages) process donor alloantigens (MHC I and MHC II) presented on graft cells. These APCs migrate to recipient lymph nodes and present the alloantigens to naive T cells via MHC molecules.
        - CD4+ T cells recognize donor MHC class II molecules presented by APCs. This requires costimulatory signals (e.g., B7 on APC binding to CD28 on T cell) and cytokine milieu (e.g., IL-12 promoting Th1 differentiation).
        - CD8+ T cells recognize donor MHC class I molecules presented by APCs (cross-presentation) or directly by graft cells. This also requires costimulation.
    - **Effector Phase:** Activated T cells migrate to the graft.
        - CD4+ T cells (Th1 type) secrete cytokines like IFN-γ and TNF-α, activating macrophages and promoting inflammation. They can also help activate CD8+ T cells.
        - CD8+ T cells directly recognize donor MHC class I molecules on graft cells (tubular epithelial cells, endothelial cells) and kill them via perforin/granzyme release or Fas/FasL interaction. This direct cytotoxicity is a key feature of ACR.
- **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** Mediated by antibodies (DSAs) against donor HLA antigens.
    - DSAs bind to donor endothelial cells, activating complement (leading to C4d deposition) and recruiting inflammatory cells (neutrophils, macrophages).
    - This causes endothelial damage, microvascular inflammation (glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis), thrombosis, and graft ischemia.
- **Hyperacute Rejection:** Occurs within minutes to hours due to pre-formed antibodies (e.g., ABO, HLA) against the donor endothelium, leading to immediate complement activation, thrombosis, and graft necrosis.
- **Chronic Rejection:** Develops over months to years, characterized by progressive fibrosis, vascular changes (graft arteriosclerosis), and loss of graft function. It involves both cellular and humoral immunity, as well as non-immune factors.

##### 3. Specifics from the Vignette & Correct Answer (C)
- The vignette describes a classic presentation of ACR occurring at 3 weeks post-transplant.
- The biopsy shows lymphocytic infiltrates (cellular), tubular damage, and endothelialitis.
- Flow cytometry confirms a predominance of CD8+ T cells infiltrating the graft parenchyma (tubules/glomeruli) and CD4+ T cells in the interstitium. This cellular profile strongly supports ACR.
- The negative DSA makes AMR less likely as the primary cause, although C4d deposition can occur in both ACR and AMR.
- Option (C) accurately describes the primary effector mechanism in this scenario: CD8+ cytotoxic T cells recognizing donor MHC class I molecules on graft cells, leading to direct cell killing and tissue damage.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- **(A) Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) involving pre-formed DSAs binding to endothelial cells.** Incorrect. The vignette explicitly states that donor-specific antibodies (DSA) are negative. While C4d deposition is present (suggestive of complement activation often seen in AMR), the predominant finding is cellular infiltration, particularly by CD8+ T cells, and the absence of DSAs makes AMR unlikely as the *primary* mechanism. AMR typically presents with features like glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis, and C4d deposition, but often with fewer infiltrating lymphocytes compared to ACR, and positive DSAs.
- **(B) Acute cellular rejection (ACR) mediated primarily by CD4+ T helper cells recognizing donor MHC class II molecules on APCs.** Incorrect. While CD4+ T cells play a crucial role in ACR by orchestrating the response and activating other immune cells (including CD8+ T cells and macrophages), the vignette highlights the *predominance* of CD8+ T cells infiltrating the tubules and glomeruli, which are the primary sites of damage. CD8+ T cells are the main effectors causing direct cytotoxicity against graft cells expressing MHC class I. Therefore, while CD4+ cells are involved, they are not the *primary* mediators of the direct graft cell killing described.
- **(D) Chronic rejection characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes due to prolonged low-grade inflammation.** Incorrect. Chronic rejection develops over months to years, not weeks. The biopsy findings described (acute inflammation, lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular damage) are characteristic of acute rejection, not the chronic fibrosis and vascular changes (like transplant glomerulopathy or interstitial fibrosis/tubular atrophy) seen in chronic rejection.
- **(E) Hyperacute rejection occurring within minutes to hours post-transplant due to pre-existing anti-donor ABO or HLA antibodies.** Incorrect. Hyperacute rejection occurs almost immediately after transplantation (minutes to hours) and is caused by pre-formed antibodies against donor antigens (like ABO or HLA). The patient presented 3 weeks post-transplant, ruling out hyperacute rejection. Furthermore, the vignette doesn't mention any history suggesting pre-formed antibodies (e.g., prior transfusions, pregnancies).

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR) | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) | Hyperacute Rejection | Chronic Rejection |
|----------------------|---------------------------------|------------------------------------|----------------------|-------------------|
| **Timing**           | Days to weeks post-transplant    | Days to months post-transplant     | Minutes to hours     | Months to years   |
| **Primary Mediator** | T cells (CD8+ cytotoxic, CD4+ helper) | Antibodies (DSAs)                 | Pre-formed Abs      | T cells, Abs, Fibrosis |
| **Key Pathology**    | Lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular damage, endothelialitis | Endothelialitis, Glomerulitis, C4d deposition, Thrombosis | Thrombosis, Necrosis | Fibrosis, Vascular changes |
| **Cellular Profile** | CD8+ > CD4+ infiltrates in parenchyma | Fewer lymphocytes, Neutrophils, Macrophages | N/A                 | Variable          |
| **DSA Status**       | Usually negative                | Usually positive                   | N/A                 | Variable          |
| **C4d Deposition**   | Can be present, but less specific | Characteristic                    | N/A                 | Variable          |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute cellular rejection (ACR) is primarily T-cell mediated, occurring days to weeks post-transplant. Key features include lymphocytic infiltrates (especially CD8+ T cells targeting graft cells) and graft dysfunction. Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) involves DSAs, complement activation (C4d), and endothelial damage, typically occurring later or concurrently. Hyperacute rejection is immediate due to pre-formed antibodies. Chronic rejection is a late complication involving fibrosis and vascular changes. Differentiating these types based on timing, pathology, and immunological markers (DSA, C4d, T cell subsets) is crucial for management.

<a id='question-016'></a>

### Question 016: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 16 (taking)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying acute graft rejection, specifically the role of T cells (CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes and CD4+ helper T cells) and antibodies (alloantibodies) in recognizing and attacking foreign MHC molecules on the transplanted organ, leading to tissue damage. (理解移植排斥反应的免疫机制，特别是T细胞（CD8+细胞毒性T淋巴细胞和CD4+辅助T细胞）和抗体（同种异体抗体）识别和攻击移植器官上的外来MHC分子，导致组织损伤的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an A blood type, while the recipient is blood type B. The patient is started on standard immunosuppressive therapy including a calcineurin inhibitor (cyclosporine), an antimetabolite (mycophenolate mofetil), and corticosteroids (prednisone). Three weeks post-transplant, the patient develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals diffuse interstitial edema, inflammatory cell infiltrates primarily composed of lymphocytes, and evidence of endothelial injury in peritubular capillaries. Immunofluorescence staining shows deposition of C4d in the peritubular capillaries. Serum analysis shows elevated levels of anti-HLA class I and class II antibodies.

Which of the following immunological mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for this patient's acute kidney graft rejection?

(A) Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) due to pre-formed anti-donor ABO antibodies.
(B) Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) due to de novo anti-donor HLA antibodies.
(C) T-cell mediated rejection (TCMR) involving CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes directly killing graft tubular epithelial cells.
(D) T-cell mediated rejection (TCMR) involving CD4+ helper T cells activating macrophages to damage the graft interstitium.
(E) Chronic antibody-mediated rejection (CAMR) characterized by transplant glomerulopathy and fibrosis.

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease (ESRD) from diabetic nephropathy, receives kidney transplant. This sets the stage for transplant rejection.
- **ABO Incompatibility:** Donor is A, recipient is B. This is a major ABO incompatibility, which typically leads to hyperacute rejection if pre-formed antibodies are present, or acute antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) if sensitization occurs post-transplant or if pre-formed antibodies are not strongly reactive enough to cause hyperacute rejection.
- **Timing:** Rejection occurs 3 weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is typical for acute rejection (either T-cell mediated or antibody-mediated). Hyperacute rejection occurs within minutes to hours. Chronic rejection occurs months to years later.
- **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated creatinine. These are classic signs of acute graft dysfunction, suggestive of rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Diffuse interstitial edema, lymphocyte infiltrates, endothelial injury in peritubular capillaries. These findings are consistent with both AMR and TCMR. Endothelial injury is particularly suggestive of AMR.
- **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. C4d is a stable breakdown product of the classical complement pathway activation. Its presence in peritubular capillaries is a strong indicator of antibody-mediated injury (AMR).
- **Serum Analysis:** Elevated anti-donor HLA class I and class II antibodies. This confirms the presence of antibodies directed against the graft, supporting AMR.
- **Immunosuppression:** The patient is on standard immunosuppression (calcineurin inhibitor, antimetabolite, steroids), which aims to prevent both TCMR and AMR, but rejection can still occur.

The combination of ABO incompatibility, timing (3 weeks), biopsy findings (lymphocyte infiltrates, endothelial injury, C4d deposition), and elevated anti-donor HLA antibodies strongly points towards acute antibody-mediated rejection (AMR). The ABO incompatibility likely led to sensitization and the development of de novo anti-donor HLA antibodies, or perhaps pre-existing antibodies were not fully screened for or were of lower titer, allowing the transplant but leading to subsequent AMR.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Transplant Rejection Overview:** Transplant rejection is an immune response against the foreign MHC molecules (HLA in humans) expressed on the donor graft cells. It can be mediated by T cells (TCMR) or antibodies (AMR).
- **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):**
    - **Mechanism:** Pre-formed or de novo antibodies (alloantibodies) bind to donor HLA antigens on endothelial cells of graft microvasculature. This triggers complement activation (classical pathway) and Fc receptor-mediated effector functions (e.g., NK cell activation, macrophage activation).
    - **Complement Activation:** Leads to C3 convertase formation, C3 cleavage, and deposition of C3d and C4d fragments in vessel walls. C5b-9 (MAC) formation causes endothelial cell lysis.
    - **Endothelial Injury:** Leads to microvascular inflammation (endotheliitis), thrombosis, ischemia, and ultimately graft dysfunction.
    - **Diagnosis:** Presence of donor-specific antibodies (DSA) in serum, C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries (renal transplant), and characteristic biopsy findings (endotheliitis, glomerululitis, thrombosis).
    - **Types:** Hyperacute (pre-formed antibodies, minutes/hours), Acute (occurring days/weeks post-transplant), Chronic (occurring months/years post-transplant).
- **T-cell Mediated Rejection (TCMR):**
    - **Mechanism:** Recipient T cells (CD4+ and CD8+) recognize donor MHC molecules (direct or indirect pathway).
        - **Direct Pathway:** Recipient T cells recognize intact donor MHC molecules on donor APCs.
        - **Indirect Pathway:** Recipient APCs process donor MHC molecules and present peptides on self-MHC class II molecules to CD4+ T cells, or cross-present donor MHC class I peptides on self-MHC class I molecules to CD8+ T cells.
    - **CD4+ T cells:** Recognize donor MHC class II molecules. Secrete cytokines (IFN-γ, TNF-α) that activate macrophages, leading to interstitial inflammation and fibrosis.
    - **CD8+ T cells:** Recognize donor MHC class I molecules. Directly kill graft cells (e.g., tubular epithelial cells) expressing donor MHC class I.
    - **Diagnosis:** Biopsy findings of lymphocytic infiltrates (interstitial or tubular), tubular injury, and sometimes vascular changes. Absence of significant C4d deposition.
- **ABO Incompatibility:** ABO blood group antigens are expressed on endothelial cells. Pre-formed anti-ABO antibodies (e.g., anti-A in a type B recipient receiving an A donor) can bind to donor endothelial cells, activate complement, and cause hyperacute rejection or acute AMR.
- **C4d Deposition:** A key histological marker for AMR, indicating classical complement pathway activation by antibodies bound to the graft endothelium.

In this case, the presence of ABO incompatibility, elevated anti-donor HLA antibodies, C4d deposition, and endothelial injury strongly supports AMR. The timing (3 weeks) fits acute AMR.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) due to pre-formed anti-donor ABO antibodies.** While ABO incompatibility is present (A donor to B recipient), hyperacute rejection (occurring within minutes to hours due to strong pre-formed anti-ABO antibodies) is not described. The rejection occurred at 3 weeks. Although pre-formed anti-ABO antibodies could contribute, the vignette emphasizes elevated *anti-HLA* antibodies and C4d deposition, which are hallmarks of AMR targeting HLA antigens, not just ABO antigens. Furthermore, ABO incompatibility often leads to *de novo* DSA formation against HLA antigens, even if pre-formed anti-ABO antibodies are not strongly reactive enough to cause hyperacute rejection. This option is plausible but less likely than (B) given the specific findings (anti-HLA antibodies, C4d).
- (C) **T-cell mediated rejection (TCMR) involving CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes directly killing graft tubular epithelial cells.** TCMR is a possibility in any transplant rejection scenario. However, the presence of C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries is a strong indicator of antibody-mediated injury, not TCMR. While CD8+ T cells can cause tubular injury in TCMR, the key finding of C4d points away from TCMR as the *primary* mechanism.
- (D) **T-cell mediated rejection (TCMR) involving CD4+ helper T cells activating macrophages to damage the graft interstitium.** Similar to (C), TCMR is possible, but the C4d deposition strongly implicates AMR. CD4+ T cell-mediated interstitial inflammation is characteristic of TCMR, but the endothelial injury and C4d deposition are more indicative of AMR.
- (E) **Chronic antibody-mediated rejection (CAMR) characterized by transplant glomerulopathy and fibrosis.** CAMR typically occurs months to years after transplantation. The rejection occurred at 3 weeks, which is characteristic of acute rejection. CAMR involves specific histological features like transplant glomerulopathy (double contours, mesangial proliferation) and interstitial fibrosis/tubular atrophy, which are not described in the biopsy findings provided.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Acute AMR (Correct Answer B)                                  | Acute TCMR (Distractors C, D)                                  | Hyperacute AMR (Related to A)                                  | Chronic AMR (Distractor E)                                     |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------------------------ |
| **Timing**              | Days to weeks post-transplant                                 | Days to weeks post-transplant                                 | Minutes to hours post-transplant                               | Months to years post-transplant                                |
| **Key Mechanism**       | Antibodies against donor HLA/endothelium                        | T cells (CD4+, CD8+) against donor MHC                         | Pre-formed antibodies against ABO/HLA                            | Antibodies against donor HLA, leading to fibrosis/glomerulopathy |
| **Biopsy Findings**     | Endotheliitis, C4d deposition, thrombosis, interstitial edema | Lymphocytic infiltrates (interstitial/tubular), tubular injury | Endotheliitis, thrombosis, C4d deposition (often severe)       | Transplant glomerulopathy, interstitial fibrosis/tubular atrophy |
| **Serum Markers**       | Donor-Specific Antibodies (DSA)                               | None specific (may see elevated cytokines)                    | Pre-formed DSA (ABO, HLA)                                      | DSA                                                            |
| **C4d Deposition**      | Present in peritubular capillaries                             | Absent or minimal                                              | Present in peritubular capillaries                             | May be present, but not the defining feature                   |
| **ABO Incompatibility** | Can trigger AMR (acute or hyperacute)                          | Not directly involved                                          | Primary cause of hyperacute rejection                           | Can contribute                                                 |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) is characterized by the presence of donor-specific antibodies (DSA), complement activation (indicated by C4d deposition), and endothelial injury. It typically occurs days to weeks post-transplant. Differentiating AMR from acute T-cell mediated rejection (TCMR) relies heavily on biopsy findings (C4d deposition, endotheliitis) and serological evidence of DSA. ABO incompatibility can lead to hyperacute or acute AMR. Chronic AMR occurs later and involves distinct histological features like transplant glomerulopathy.

<a id='question-017'></a>

### Question 017: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 17 (cells,)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of specific cell types (T cells, B cells, macrophages, NK cells) and their interactions in transplant rejection, particularly focusing on the mechanisms of acute cellular rejection mediated primarily by T lymphocytes (细胞介导的急性排斥反应).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an unknown HLA type. He is started on standard immunosuppression consisting of tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. A kidney biopsy reveals diffuse interstitial inflammation with lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular epithelial cell damage, and evidence of endothelialitis in peritubular capillaries. Immunofluorescence staining shows deposition of C4d in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the peripheral blood shows a decrease in CD4+ T cells and an increase in CD8+ T cells. Which of the following cell types is MOST directly responsible for the observed acute cellular rejection?

(A) B lymphocytes producing donor-specific antibodies
(B) Natural killer (NK) cells recognizing missing self-MHC class I
(C) Macrophages activated by complement deposition
(D) CD4+ T helper cells recognizing donor MHC class II
(E) CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes recognizing donor MHC class I

#### Correct Answer: (E)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for transplant rejection.
- **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus (calcineurin inhibitor, inhibits T cell activation), mycophenolate mofetil (inhibits purine synthesis, affects lymphocyte proliferation), prednisone (corticosteroid, broad immunosuppressant). Despite this, rejection occurs.
- **Timing:** Three weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is typical for acute rejection, particularly acute cellular rejection (ACR) or antibody-mediated rejection (AMR).
- **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated creatinine. These are classic signs of graft dysfunction, suggestive of rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Diffuse interstitial inflammation with lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular epithelial cell damage, endothelialitis in peritubular capillaries. This strongly points towards acute cellular rejection (lymphocytic infiltration, tubular damage, endothelialitis).
- **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. C4d is a marker of complement activation, often seen in antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), but can also be present in ACR, especially if there's some antibody involvement or overlap. However, the predominant histological picture is ACR.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Decrease in CD4+ T cells and increase in CD8+ T cells. This pattern is characteristic of acute cellular rejection, where cytotoxic T cells (CD8+) infiltrate the graft and cause damage.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the cell type MOST directly responsible for the observed acute cellular rejection.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Transplant Rejection:** The recipient's immune system recognizes the donor organ as foreign (alloantigens, primarily MHC molecules) and mounts an immune response.
- **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** Primarily mediated by T lymphocytes.
    - **Sensitization Phase:** Recipient T cells (CD4+ and CD8+) recognize donor MHC molecules (allo-MHC) presented by recipient antigen-presenting cells (APCs) or donor APCs migrating to recipient lymphoid organs. This leads to T cell activation and proliferation.
    - **Effector Phase:** Activated T cells migrate to the graft.
        - **CD4+ T helper cells (Th1 type):** Recognize donor MHC class II molecules on APCs in the graft. They secrete cytokines like IFN-γ and TNF-α, which activate macrophages and enhance MHC expression, further promoting inflammation and cytotoxicity. They also help activate CD8+ T cells.
        - **CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs):** Recognize donor MHC class I molecules expressed on graft cells (e.g., tubular epithelial cells, endothelial cells). Upon recognition, CTLs directly kill graft cells via perforin/granzyme release or Fas/FasL interaction. This direct cell killing is a hallmark of ACR.
- **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** Mediated by pre-formed or de novo donor-specific antibodies (DSAs). These antibodies bind to donor MHC molecules (or other antigens) on endothelial cells, activating complement and recruiting inflammatory cells (macrophages, NK cells), leading to endothelial damage (endothelialitis), microvascular inflammation, and thrombosis. C4d deposition is a key marker.
- **Role of Other Cells:**
    - **B lymphocytes:** Produce DSAs in AMR. While antibodies can contribute to ACR (e.g., by activating complement), the primary effector cells in classic ACR are T cells.
    - **Natural Killer (NK) cells:** Can contribute to rejection, especially in AMR (via antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity, ADCC) or if donor cells lack MHC class I (missing self). However, they are not the primary drivers of typical ACR.
    - **Macrophages:** Activated by T cell cytokines (IFN-γ) and complement components (C5a). They contribute to inflammation and tissue damage but are secondary effectors activated by T cells or antibodies/complement.

- **Analysis of the Case:** The biopsy shows lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular damage, and endothelialitis, characteristic of ACR. The flow cytometry shows an increase in CD8+ T cells, consistent with CTL-mediated cytotoxicity. While C4d deposition is present (suggesting some complement activation, possibly related to antibodies or T cell-mediated inflammation), the overall picture (histology, cell populations) strongly points to ACR driven primarily by T cells. The question asks for the cell type *most directly* responsible. CD8+ CTLs directly kill graft cells, which is the central mechanism of cellular damage in ACR. CD4+ T cells play a crucial role in initiating and amplifying the response, but CD8+ cells are the primary effectors causing direct cell death.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) B lymphocytes producing donor-specific antibodies: This describes the mechanism of antibody-mediated rejection (AMR). While C4d deposition is present, the predominant histological features (lymphocytic infiltrate, tubular damage, endothelialitis) and the increase in CD8+ T cells are more characteristic of ACR. B cells are primarily involved in AMR, not the direct cellular killing seen in ACR.
- (B) Natural killer (NK) cells recognizing missing self-MHC class I: NK cells can mediate rejection, particularly in the context of missing self (e.g., viral infection downregulating MHC I) or via ADCC in AMR. However, they are not the primary effector cells in typical acute cellular rejection driven by alloantigen recognition. The case describes classic T cell-mediated rejection.
- (C) Macrophages activated by complement deposition: Macrophages are involved in both ACR (activated by T cell cytokines) and AMR (activated by complement, antibodies). However, they are secondary effectors, not the primary cells directly recognizing and killing graft cells in ACR. The question asks for the *most direct* cause.
- (D) CD4+ T helper cells recognizing donor MHC class II: CD4+ T cells are crucial for initiating and orchestrating the immune response in ACR. They recognize donor MHC class II, secrete cytokines, and help activate CD8+ T cells and macrophages. However, they do not directly kill graft cells in the same way CD8+ T cells do. CD8+ T cells are considered the primary effector cells causing direct cytotoxicity in ACR.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR)                                  | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR)                                  |
|----------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Primary Mediators** | CD8+ Cytotoxic T Lymphocytes (CTLs), CD4+ T Helper Cells         | Donor-Specific Antibodies (DSAs)                                 |
| **Mechanism**        | Direct cell killing (CTLs), cytokine-mediated inflammation (Th1) | Complement activation, ADCC, endothelial damage                 |
| **Histology**        | Lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular damage, endothelialitis         | Endothelialitis, microvascular inflammation, C4d deposition      |
| **Key Cells**        | CD8+ T cells ↑, CD4+ T cells ↑                                | B cells ↑ (DSA production), Macrophages ↑, NK cells ↑ (ADCC)     |
| **Markers**          | Increased CD8+ cells, IFN-γ                                     | C4d deposition, DSAs                                             |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute cellular rejection is primarily mediated by T lymphocytes, specifically CD8+ cytotoxic T cells that directly kill graft cells expressing allo-MHC class I, and CD4+ T helper cells that orchestrate the response. Differentiate ACR from AMR based on histology (lymphocytic infiltrate vs. endothelialitis, C4d), cell populations (CD8+ ↑ vs. B cells ↑), and mechanisms (direct cytotoxicity vs. antibody/complement). Key differentials include AMR, chronic rejection, and infection.

<a id='question-018'></a>

### Question 018: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 18 (tissues,)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying graft rejection, specifically the role of T cells (CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes and CD4+ helper T cells) and antibodies in recognizing foreign MHC molecules and initiating immune responses against transplanted tissues. (理解移植排斥反应的免疫机制，特别是T细胞（CD8+细胞毒性T淋巴细胞和CD4+辅助T细胞）和抗体在识别外源性MHC分子并启动对移植组织的免疫应答中的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an unknown HLA type. The patient is started on standard immunosuppressive therapy including cyclosporine, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, elevated serum creatinine (from a baseline of 1.5 mg/dL to 4.0 mg/dL), and decreased urine output. A renal biopsy reveals acute tubular necrosis and interstitial edema with infiltration of mononuclear cells, predominantly T lymphocytes. Immunofluorescence staining shows deposition of C4d in the peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the peripheral blood shows a significant increase in CD8+ T cells expressing granzyme B and perforin. The patient's serum contains antibodies reactive against donor HLA class I antigens.

Which of the following immunological mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for this patient's acute kidney graft rejection?

(A) Antibody-mediated rejection involving complement activation and endothelial injury.
(B) Chronic rejection characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes mediated by CD4+ Th2 cells.
(C) Hyperacute rejection mediated by pre-formed donor-specific antibodies binding to endothelial complement receptors.
(D) Cellular rejection primarily mediated by CD4+ Th1 cells recognizing donor MHC class II molecules directly.
(E) Antibody-dependent cellular cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells recognizing antibody-coated graft cells.

#### Correct Answer: (A)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for potential graft rejection.
- **Time Course:** Three weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is characteristic of acute rejection, specifically acute cellular rejection (ACR) or acute antibody-mediated rejection (AMR). Hyperacute rejection occurs within minutes to hours, and chronic rejection develops over months to years.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Fever, flank pain, elevated creatinine, decreased urine output. These are classic signs and symptoms of acute graft dysfunction, suggesting rejection.
- **Renal Biopsy Findings:** Acute tubular necrosis, interstitial edema, mononuclear cell infiltration (T lymphocytes). This points towards acute rejection involving cellular infiltration.
- **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. C4d is a stable breakdown product of the classical complement pathway (C3 convertase). Its presence indicates recent complement activation, strongly suggesting antibody-mediated injury.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD8+ T cells expressing granzyme B and perforin. This indicates cytotoxic T lymphocyte (CTL) activation, characteristic of cellular rejection.
- **Serum Antibodies:** Antibodies reactive against donor HLA class I antigens. This confirms the presence of donor-specific antibodies (DSAs), the hallmark of antibody-mediated rejection (AMR).

**Synthesis:** The combination of clinical signs of acute graft dysfunction, biopsy findings of cellular infiltration, *and* evidence of complement activation (C4d deposition) *and* donor-specific antibodies points towards a mixed acute rejection episode, but the presence of C4d deposition and DSAs strongly implicates an antibody-mediated component. Option (A) directly addresses this antibody-mediated mechanism involving complement activation and endothelial injury, which is consistent with the C4d deposition. While cellular rejection (CD8+ T cells) is also present, the C4d deposition is a key indicator of AMR.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Transplantation Immunology Overview:** Graft rejection is an immune response against foreign MHC molecules (alloantigens) present on the donor tissue. The immune system recognizes these as non-self.
- **Types of Rejection:**
    - **Hyperacute:** Occurs minutes to hours post-transplant due to pre-formed antibodies (e.g., ABO incompatibility, prior sensitization). Antibodies bind to donor endothelial cells, activate complement, and cause thrombosis and necrosis.
    - **Acute:** Occurs days to weeks post-transplant. Can be cellular (T cell-mediated) or antibody-mediated.
        - **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** Primarily mediated by T cells (CD4+ helper T cells and CD8+ cytotoxic T cells) recognizing donor MHC molecules (class II and class I, respectively). CD4+ cells orchestrate the response, while CD8+ cells directly kill graft cells.
        - **Acute Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** Mediated by donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) binding to donor HLA antigens on endothelial cells. This triggers complement activation (classical pathway), leading to endothelial injury, inflammation, and thrombosis. C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries is a key histological marker.
    - **Chronic:** Occurs months to years post-transplant. Characterized by gradual loss of graft function due to fibrosis, vascular changes (e.g., transplant glomerulopathy, chronic active serosal vasculopathy), and inflammation. Both cellular and humoral immunity contribute.
- **Mechanism of Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** DSAs (typically IgG) bind to donor HLA class I or class II molecules on endothelial cells. This binding activates the classical complement pathway, leading to the formation of C3 convertase (C4b2a) and subsequent cleavage of C3. C3a and C5a are anaphylatoxins, recruiting inflammatory cells. The membrane attack complex (MAC, C5b-9) forms, causing endothelial cell lysis. C4d deposition occurs at the site of complement activation. Endothelial injury leads to microvascular inflammation (peritubular capillaritis), thrombosis, and ultimately graft dysfunction.
- **Mechanism of Cellular Rejection (CR):** Recipient T cells (CD4+ and CD8+) recognize donor MHC molecules presented by antigen-presenting cells (APCs) in the graft or by graft cells themselves. CD4+ T cells (Th1 phenotype) release cytokines like IFN-γ and TNF-α, activating macrophages and promoting inflammation. CD8+ T cells directly recognize donor MHC class I molecules on graft cells and kill them via perforin/granzyme release or Fas/FasL interaction.

##### 3. Role of C4d:** C4d is a degradation product of C4b, a component of the classical complement pathway C3 convertase (C4b2a). Its presence in peritubular capillaries indicates recent activation of the classical complement pathway, strongly suggesting antibody-mediated injury.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Correct.** This option accurately describes the antibody-mediated mechanism involving complement activation (indicated by C4d deposition) and endothelial injury, which is strongly supported by the presence of DSAs and C4d deposition in the biopsy. The clinical presentation and biopsy findings are consistent with AMR.
- (B): **Incorrect.** Chronic rejection typically occurs much later (months to years), involves fibrosis and vascular changes (like transplant glomerulopathy), and is often associated with both cellular and humoral responses, but not typically characterized by acute tubular necrosis and C4d deposition in this timeframe. CD4+ Th2 cells are more associated with allergic responses and parasitic infections, not the primary mediators of chronic transplant rejection, which involves Th1, macrophages, and fibrosis.
- (C): **Incorrect.** Hyperacute rejection occurs within minutes to hours due to pre-formed antibodies. The patient presented three weeks post-transplant, ruling out hyperacute rejection. While it involves antibodies and complement, the timing is wrong.
- (D): **Incorrect.** While cellular rejection is occurring (evidenced by T cell infiltration and CD8+ T cell activation), this option focuses *solely* on CD4+ Th1 cells recognizing MHC class II. The presence of C4d deposition and DSAs against HLA class I antigens points strongly to an antibody-mediated component, making option (A) a more complete and accurate description of the *most likely* mechanism driving the acute injury, especially given the C4d finding. Furthermore, CD8+ T cells are also key players in cellular rejection, recognizing MHC class I.
- (E): **Incorrect.** Antibody-dependent cellular cytotoxicity (ADCC) involves NK cells or other cytotoxic cells binding to Fc receptors on antibody-coated target cells. While DSAs can potentially mediate ADCC, the hallmark finding of C4d deposition strongly points towards complement activation as the primary mechanism of endothelial injury in this case, characteristic of AMR. ADCC is less commonly cited as the primary mechanism in acute AMR compared to complement-mediated endothelial damage.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Acute Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) | Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR) | Hyperacute Rejection | Chronic Rejection |
|----------------------|----------------------------------------|--------------------------------|----------------------|-------------------|
| **Timing**          | Days to weeks                          | Days to weeks                  | Minutes to hours     | Months to years   |
| **Mediator**        | Donor-Specific Antibodies (DSAs)        | T cells (CD4+, CD8+)           | Pre-formed Abs      | T cells, Abs      |
| **Key Pathological Finding** | C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries, Endothelialitis | T cell infiltration, Tubulitis | Thrombosis, Necrosis | Fibrosis, Vascular changes |
| **Target**          | Endothelial cells                      | Parenchymal cells, Endothelium | Endothelial cells   | Vessels, Parenchyma |
| **Mechanism**       | Complement activation, Endothelial injury | Cytotoxicity, Cytokine release | Complement activation | Inflammation, Fibrosis |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute kidney graft rejection can be antibody-mediated (AMR) or T cell-mediated (cellular rejection, ACR). The presence of donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) and C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries is diagnostic of AMR. While cellular rejection (T cell infiltration) may also be present concurrently, C4d deposition specifically points to complement activation driven by antibodies. Differentiate AMR from ACR based on histological findings (C4d, endothelialitis vs. tubulitis, T cell infiltrates) and the presence of DSAs. Differentiate acute rejection from hyperacute (timing, pre-formed Abs) and chronic rejection (timing, fibrosis).

<a id='question-019'></a>

### Question 019: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 19 (organs)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying acute rejection in solid organ transplantation, specifically the role of T cells (CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes and CD4+ helper T cells) and antibodies (specifically anti-donor HLA antibodies) in mediating graft damage. (理解实体器官移植中急性排斥的免疫机制，特别是T细胞（CD8+细胞毒性T淋巴细胞和CD4+辅助T细胞）和抗体（特别是抗供体HLA抗体）在介导移植物损伤中的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease secondary to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an HLA mismatch at the A locus. He is started on standard immunosuppression consisting of tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals diffuse interstitial edema, inflammatory cell infiltrates primarily composed of lymphocytes (CD8+ > CD4+), and evidence of endothelialitis in peritubular capillaries. Immunofluorescence microscopy shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Serum analysis shows a significant increase in anti-donor HLA class I antibodies.

Which of the following immunological mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for this patient's acute kidney graft dysfunction?

(A) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells against donor endothelial cells expressing HLA-G.
(B) Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) mediated by anti-donor HLA antibodies binding to donor endothelial cells, leading to membrane attack complex (MAC) formation.
(C) T-cell mediated cytotoxicity involving CD4+ helper T cells recognizing donor MHC class II molecules on recipient antigen-presenting cells (APCs).
(D) Direct allorecognition by recipient CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) recognizing intact donor MHC class I molecules on donor cells within the graft.
(E) Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) involving anti-donor HLA antibodies binding to donor endothelial cells, activating complement and recruiting neutrophils via Fc receptors.

#### Correct Answer: (E)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease (ESRD) due to diabetes, received kidney transplant. This sets the stage for transplant rejection.
- **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus (calcineurin inhibitor), mycophenolate mofetil (purine synthesis inhibitor), prednisone (corticosteroid). Standard regimen, but rejection can still occur.
- **Timing:** 3 weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is typical for acute rejection, particularly antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) or T-cell mediated rejection (TCMR).
- **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated creatinine. Classic signs of acute graft dysfunction/rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings:**
    - Diffuse interstitial edema, lymphocyte infiltrates (CD8+ > CD4+): Suggests T-cell mediated component.
    - Endothelialitis in peritubular capillaries: Hallmark of AMR, but can also be seen in severe TCMR.
    - C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries: Strong indicator of antibody-mediated injury involving complement activation.
- **Serum Analysis:** Increased anti-donor HLA class I antibodies. Confirms the presence of donor-specific antibodies (DSAs), the key feature of AMR.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely immunological mechanism. The combination of clinical signs, biopsy findings (especially C4d deposition and endothelialitis), and presence of DSAs points strongly towards AMR.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Transplant Rejection:** An immune response against the transplanted organ (allograft). Can be hyperacute, acute, or chronic.
- **Acute Rejection:** Occurs days to months post-transplant. Can be T-cell mediated (TCMR) or antibody-mediated (AMR).
- **TCMR:** Primarily mediated by recipient T cells recognizing donor MHC molecules (alloantigens).
    - **Direct Pathway:** Recipient T cells (CD8+ CTLs, CD4+ helper T cells) recognize intact donor MHC molecules presented directly by donor APCs (dendritic cells, macrophages) within the graft. CD8+ CTLs directly kill graft cells. CD4+ T cells help activate CTLs and macrophages.
    - **Indirect Pathway:** Recipient APCs process and present donor MHC peptides via recipient MHC molecules (MHC class II) to recipient CD4+ T cells. These CD4+ T cells then help activate recipient CD8+ CTLs.
- **AMR:** Mediated by pre-formed or de novo donor-specific antibodies (DSAs), primarily against HLA class I and class II molecules.
    - **Mechanisms:**
        - **Complement-Dependent Cytotoxicity (CDC):** Antibodies bind to donor endothelial cells, activate the classical complement pathway (C1q, C4, C2, C3), leading to the formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC, C5b-9), causing cell lysis. C4d is a stable breakdown product of C4b, deposited in tissues during complement activation, and is a key histological marker for AMR.
        - **Fc Receptor-Mediated Mechanisms:** Antibodies bind to donor endothelial cells, and the Fc portion of the antibody binds to Fc receptors (FcγR) on effector cells like neutrophils, macrophages, and NK cells. This leads to:
            - **Antibody-Dependent Cell-Mediated Cytotoxicity (ADCC):** Effector cells release cytotoxic granules, killing the target cell.
            - **Inflammation:** Effector cells release inflammatory mediators, causing endothelial damage and graft dysfunction.
        - **Non-Complement Mediated Mechanisms:** Antibodies can also cause endothelial activation and dysfunction without direct complement activation or ADCC.
- **Key Markers for AMR:** Presence of DSAs, C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries, endothelialitis.
- **In this case:** The patient has DSAs (anti-donor HLA class I antibodies), C4d deposition, and endothelialitis. These are the classic features of AMR. The lymphocyte infiltrate (CD8+ > CD4+) can be seen in both TCMR and AMR, but the presence of DSAs and C4d strongly points to AMR as the primary mechanism.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** HLA-G is a non-classical MHC class I molecule expressed on placental trophoblasts and some immune cells. It inhibits NK cell cytotoxicity. While it can be expressed on some tumors and potentially play a role in immune evasion, it is not the primary target in acute kidney transplant rejection, and NK cells are not the main effector cells in this scenario. The primary antibodies are anti-HLA class I (A, B, C) and class II (DR, DQ, DP).
- (B): **Incorrect.** While CDC is *a* mechanism of AMR, it is not the *most* comprehensive description of the process in this case. The vignette describes endothelialitis and the presence of DSAs, which activate multiple pathways, including Fc receptor-mediated mechanisms involving neutrophils. Option (E) encompasses both complement activation (leading to C4d deposition and potentially CDC) and Fc receptor-mediated inflammation/ADCC, providing a more complete picture of AMR.
- (C): **Incorrect.** This describes a component of TCMR (indirect pathway). While T cells are present in the infiltrate, the key findings of DSAs and C4d deposition point to AMR as the dominant mechanism. CD4+ T cells recognizing donor MHC II on *recipient* APCs is part of the indirect allorecognition pathway, not the primary mechanism of acute graft dysfunction in the presence of DSAs and C4d.
- (D): **Incorrect.** This describes the direct pathway of TCMR, where recipient CD8+ CTLs directly recognize donor MHC class I on donor cells. While TCMR can occur, the presence of DSAs and C4d deposition are hallmarks of AMR, making it the more likely primary mechanism in this clinical scenario. The biopsy shows endothelialitis, which is more characteristic of AMR than TCMR, although it can occur in severe TCMR.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Acute T-Cell Mediated Rejection (TCMR) | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) |
|----------------------|----------------------------------------|-----------------------------------|
| **Primary Mediators** | T cells (CD8+ CTLs, CD4+ T cells)      | Donor-Specific Antibodies (DSAs)  |
| **Key Histology**    | Infiltrates (T cells > macrophages), tubulitis | Endothelialitis, C4d deposition, infiltrates (neutrophils, macrophages) |
| **Serum Markers**    | Often none specific, maybe elevated IL-2 | Donor-Specific Antibodies (DSAs) |
| **Mechanism**        | Direct/Indirect allorecognition, CTL killing | CDC, ADCC, FcR-mediated inflammation |
| **Typical Timing**   | Days to weeks                          | Can occur anytime, often later than TCMR |
| **This Case**        | Less likely (no DSAs, C4d present)     | **Most likely (DSAs, C4d, endothelialitis)** |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute kidney transplant rejection can be T-cell mediated (TCMR) or antibody-mediated (AMR). AMR is characterized by the presence of donor-specific antibodies (DSAs), C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries, and endothelialitis. TCMR is characterized by T-cell infiltrates (often tubulitis) without significant C4d deposition or DSAs. This case presents classic features of AMR. Key differentials include TCMR and chronic rejection.

<a id='question-020'></a>

### Question 20: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 20 (graft)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the cellular and molecular mechanisms underlying acute graft rejection, specifically the role of T lymphocytes (CD8+ cytotoxic T cells and CD4+ helper T cells) in recognizing foreign MHC molecules on the graft and initiating an immune response leading to graft damage. (理解移植排斥反应的细胞和分子机制，特别是T淋巴细胞（CD8+细胞毒性T细胞和CD4+辅助T细胞）在识别移植排斥反应中的作用，即识别移植排斥反应中的外来MHC分子并启动免疫反应导致移植排斥反应。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an unknown HLA type. The patient is started on standard immunosuppressive therapy consisting of tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals diffuse interstitial inflammation with infiltration of mononuclear cells, tubular epithelial cell damage, and evidence of endothelialitis in peritubular capillaries. Immunofluorescence staining shows deposition of C4d in the peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the peripheral blood shows an increased percentage of activated CD8+ T cells expressing CD69 and CD107a.

Which of the following immunological mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for the patient's acute kidney graft rejection?

(A) Antibody-mediated rejection involving pre-formed donor-specific antibodies (DSA) binding to endothelial MHC class I molecules.
(B) Antibody-mediated rejection involving de novo DSA binding to endothelial MHC class II molecules.
(C) T cell-mediated rejection involving CD4+ helper T cells recognizing donor MHC class II molecules presented by recipient antigen-presenting cells (APCs).
(D) T cell-mediated rejection involving CD8+ cytotoxic T cells directly recognizing donor MHC class I molecules on graft cells.
(E) Antibody-mediated rejection involving complement activation leading to endothelial injury and thrombosis, characterized by C3d deposition.

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for potential graft rejection.
- **Immunosuppression:** Standard triple therapy (tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, prednisone) is used, but rejection can still occur.
- **Timing:** 3 weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is typical for acute cellular rejection (TCR) or acute antibody-mediated rejection (AMR).
- **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated creatinine. These are classic signs of acute graft dysfunction, suggestive of rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Diffuse interstitial inflammation, mononuclear cell infiltration, tubular epithelial cell damage, endothelialitis. These findings are characteristic of acute rejection, particularly T cell-mediated rejection (TCR). Endothelialitis is a key feature.
- **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. C4d is a marker of complement activation, often seen in antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), but can also be present in some forms of TCR, particularly if there is significant endothelial damage or overlap syndromes. However, the *primary* histological features point strongly towards TCR.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased activated CD8+ T cells (CD69, CD107a). This indicates a robust cytotoxic T cell response, a hallmark of TCR. CD107a (LAMP-1) is a marker of degranulation, indicating cytotoxic activity.

The combination of clinical presentation, biopsy findings (especially mononuclear cell infiltration and endothelialitis), and flow cytometry results (activated CD8+ T cells) strongly points towards T cell-mediated rejection, specifically involving CD8+ cytotoxic T cells.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Transplantation Immunology Overview:** Graft rejection is an immune response against foreign MHC molecules (alloantigens) on the transplanted organ. Rejection can be classified as hyperacute, acute, or chronic, and mediated by T cells (cellular rejection) or antibodies (humoral/antibody-mediated rejection).
- **Acute Cellular Rejection (TCR):** Primarily mediated by recipient T lymphocytes recognizing donor MHC molecules.
    - **CD4+ T cells:** Recognize donor MHC class II molecules presented by recipient APCs (e.g., dendritic cells, macrophages). They release cytokines (IFN-γ, TNF-α) that activate macrophages and CD8+ T cells, contributing to inflammation and graft damage.
    - **CD8+ T cells:** Recognize donor MHC class I molecules directly on graft cells (parenchymal cells, endothelial cells). Upon recognition, they become activated, proliferate, and directly kill graft cells via cytotoxic mechanisms (perforin/granzyme release, Fas/FasL interaction). Activated CD8+ T cells are a key feature of TCR biopsies.
- **Acute Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** Mediated by antibodies (often pre-formed or de novo DSAs) binding to donor HLA antigens on endothelial cells.
    - **Mechanism:** Antibody binding activates complement (classical pathway) and recruits effector cells (NK cells, macrophages) via Fc receptors. This leads to endothelial injury, inflammation, microvascular thrombosis (glomerulopathy), and graft dysfunction.
    - **Markers:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries is a key histological marker, indicating complement activation. DSAs can be detected in serum.
- **Distinguishing TCR vs. AMR:** While C4d deposition can occur in both, the predominant histological features are crucial. TCR typically shows interstitial inflammation, mononuclear cell infiltration, and tubular damage, often with endothelialitis. AMR typically shows microvascular inflammation (glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis), endothelial injury, and thrombosis. The flow cytometry finding of activated CD8+ T cells strongly favors TCR.

In this case, the biopsy shows interstitial inflammation, mononuclear cell infiltration, tubular damage, and endothelialitis, consistent with TCR. The flow cytometry confirms activated CD8+ T cells. Therefore, CD8+ T cell-mediated rejection is the most likely mechanism.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Antibody-mediated rejection involving pre-formed donor-specific antibodies (DSA) binding to endothelial MHC class I molecules.** While AMR is possible, the biopsy findings (diffuse interstitial inflammation, mononuclear cells, tubular damage, endothelialitis) are more typical of TCR. Also, the flow cytometry points to activated CD8+ T cells, not primarily antibodies. Pre-formed DSAs typically cause hyperacute rejection or early AMR, not usually presenting at 3 weeks with these histological features.
- (B) **Antibody-mediated rejection involving de novo DSA binding to endothelial MHC class II molecules.** Similar to (A), the histological and flow cytometry findings are more consistent with TCR. De novo DSAs can cause AMR, but the classic features of AMR (microvascular inflammation, thrombosis) are less prominent here, and the activated CD8+ T cells point away from AMR as the *primary* mechanism.
- (C) **T cell-mediated rejection involving CD4+ helper T cells recognizing donor MHC class II molecules presented by recipient antigen-presenting cells (APCs).** CD4+ T cells are indeed involved in TCR, orchestrating the immune response. However, the flow cytometry specifically highlights activated CD8+ T cells, which are the primary effector cells causing direct cytotoxicity against graft cells in this scenario. While CD4+ cells contribute, the direct cytotoxic damage is attributed to CD8+ cells. Option (D) is more specific to the effector mechanism highlighted by the data.
- (E) **Antibody-mediated rejection involving complement activation leading to endothelial injury and thrombosis, characterized by C3d deposition.** C4d deposition indicates complement activation, which is characteristic of AMR. However, C3d is a later product of complement activation than C4d, and C4d is the specific marker found. More importantly, the histological features (diffuse interstitial inflammation, mononuclear cells, tubular damage, endothelialitis) and the activated CD8+ T cells strongly favor TCR over AMR. C4d deposition can sometimes be seen in TCR, but it's not the defining feature, and the other findings point elsewhere.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | T Cell-Mediated Rejection (TCR)                                  | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR)                                     |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Mediators**   | T lymphocytes (CD4+, CD8+)                                      | Antibodies (DSAs)                                                     |
| **Target Antigens**     | Donor MHC Class I & II                                           | Donor HLA antigens (primarily on endothelium)                         |
| **Key Histology**       | Interstitial inflammation, mononuclear cells, tubular damage, endothelialitis | Microvascular inflammation (glomerulitis, capillaritis), thrombosis, endothelial injury |
| **Key Immunofluorescence** | Less specific, sometimes C4d deposition                         | C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries                             |
| **Key Flow Cytometry**  | Activated CD8+ T cells (CD69, CD107a), sometimes CD4+ T cells | Less specific for T cells, may show B cells or NK cells              |
| **Typical Timing**      | Days to weeks post-transplant                                    | Days to months post-transplant                                        |
| **Example Scenario**    | Case vignette with interstitial inflammation, endothelialitis, activated CD8+ T cells | Case vignette with microvascular inflammation, C4d deposition, DSAs |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute graft rejection can be T cell-mediated (TCR) or antibody-mediated (AMR). TCR is characterized by T cell infiltration (especially CD8+ cytotoxic T cells) causing direct cell damage, often with interstitial inflammation and endothelialitis. AMR is characterized by antibodies targeting endothelial antigens, activating complement and recruiting effector cells, leading to microvascular injury and thrombosis, often marked by C4d deposition. Distinguishing features on biopsy and flow cytometry are crucial for diagnosis and management. Key differentials include infection and drug toxicity, which can also cause graft dysfunction.

<a id='question-021'></a>

### Question 21: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 21 (individual)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of MHC class I and class II molecules in allogeneic T cell activation and the specific mechanisms by which immunosuppressive drugs target these pathways, particularly focusing on the differential effects of cyclosporine and tacrolimus on IL-2 production and T cell activation. (理解 MHC I 类和 II 类分子在异体 T 细胞活化中的作用，以及免疫抑制药物如何靶向这些途径的具体机制，特别是环孢素和他克莫司对 IL-2 产生和 T 细胞活化的不同影响。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease secondary to diabetic nephropathy undergoes a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an A blood type, while the recipient is blood type O. He is started on a standard immunosuppressive regimen including tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, chills, and flank pain. Urinalysis reveals pyuria and bacteriuria. Urine culture grows Gram-negative rods that are oxidase-positive, catalase-positive, and ferment lactose, producing pink colonies on MacConkey agar. The patient is treated with intravenous ceftriaxone, but his condition worsens, developing septic shock. Blood cultures are drawn and grow the same organism. Flow cytometry analysis of the patient's peripheral blood lymphocytes shows a significant decrease in CD4+ T cells compared to pre-transplant levels, but CD8+ T cell counts remain relatively stable. Serum tacrolimus levels are found to be subtherapeutic.

Which of the following mechanisms BEST explains the patient's increased susceptibility to this specific Gram-negative bacterial infection post-transplant?

(A) Impaired complement activation due to the formation of immune complexes with the donor kidney antigens.
(B) Reduced production of interferon-gamma (IFN-γ) by CD8+ T cells, leading to decreased macrophage activation.
(C) Inhibition of calcineurin-dependent signaling, preventing the transcription of the IL-2 gene and subsequent T cell proliferation, primarily affecting CD4+ T cells.
(D) Direct cytotoxic effect of tacrolimus on polymorphonuclear leukocytes (PMNs), impairing bacterial killing.
(E) Antibody-mediated rejection leading to endothelial damage and increased bacterial adherence.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, kidney transplant recipient (immunosuppressed).
*   **Transplant Details:** Deceased donor, ABO incompatibility (Donor A, Recipient O) - suggests potential for antibody-mediated rejection, but the primary issue seems infectious.
*   **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, prednisone. Tacrolimus is a calcineurin inhibitor.
*   **Clinical Presentation:** Fever, chills, flank pain 3 weeks post-transplant - classic signs of infection, potentially UTI or pyelonephritis.
*   **Microbiology:** Gram-negative rods, oxidase-positive, catalase-positive, lactose fermenter (pink on MacConkey) - strongly suggests *Escherichia coli* (E. coli), a common cause of UTIs, especially in immunocompromised hosts.
*   **Clinical Course:** Worsening condition despite ceftriaxone, septic shock, positive blood cultures (same organism) - indicates severe systemic infection.
*   **Immunology:** Decreased CD4+ T cells, stable CD8+ T cells, subtherapeutic tacrolimus levels. This points towards a specific immunosuppressive effect.
*   **Question:** Mechanism of increased susceptibility to *this specific Gram-negative bacterial infection*.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **Tacrolimus Mechanism:** Tacrolimus (FK506) is a calcineurin inhibitor. It binds to FKBP12 (FK506 binding protein 12), and this complex inhibits calcineurin. Calcineurin is a phosphatase that normally dephosphorylates NFAT (Nuclear Factor of Activated T-cells). Dephosphorylated NFAT translocates to the nucleus and acts as a transcription factor, crucial for the expression of genes encoding cytokines like IL-2, IL-4, IFN-γ, and TNF-α. By inhibiting calcineurin, tacrolimus prevents NFAT activation, thereby blocking the transcription of IL-2 and other key cytokines. IL-2 is essential for T cell proliferation and differentiation.
*   **T Cell Subsets:** Tacrolimus primarily inhibits the activation and proliferation of CD4+ T helper cells because their activation and proliferation are highly dependent on IL-2 production (autocrine stimulation) and IL-2 signaling. While CD8+ T cells also require IL-2, their activation can be driven by other signals (e.g., direct antigen presentation via MHC class I, IFN-γ). The vignette specifically notes a decrease in CD4+ T cells, consistent with the primary effect of tacrolimus.
*   **E. coli Infection:** *E. coli* is a facultative anaerobic Gram-negative bacterium. It is a common cause of urinary tract infections (UTIs), pyelonephritis, and sepsis, particularly in individuals with compromised immune systems or urinary tract abnormalities. Cell-mediated immunity, particularly involving activated T helper cells (CD4+), is crucial for controlling intracellular pathogens and modulating macrophage function to clear extracellular bacteria like *E. coli*.
*   **Link:** The immunosuppression induced by tacrolimus, specifically the inhibition of IL-2 production and CD4+ T cell proliferation, impairs the host's ability to mount an effective cell-mediated immune response against *E. coli*. This increased susceptibility to Gram-negative bacterial infections is a well-known complication of calcineurin inhibitor therapy.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Impaired complement activation due to immune complexes. While ABO incompatibility can lead to pre-formed antibodies and immune complexes, this primarily relates to antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) or hyperacute rejection, not the specific susceptibility to *E. coli* infection described. Complement activation is important for bacterial killing (e.g., MAC formation, opsonization), but the primary defect here is T cell-mediated immunity suppression by tacrolimus. AMR typically presents earlier or with signs of vascular injury, not primarily as a Gram-negative sepsis.
- (B): Reduced IFN-γ by CD8+ T cells, leading to decreased macrophage activation. While IFN-γ is important for macrophage activation to kill intracellular pathogens, *E. coli* is primarily an extracellular pathogen. Furthermore, tacrolimus primarily affects CD4+ T cells and IL-2 production more profoundly than CD8+ T cell IFN-γ production, although IFN-γ production can be affected. The vignette shows stable CD8+ T cell counts, making this less likely the primary mechanism compared to the profound CD4+ T cell suppression.
- (D): Direct cytotoxic effect of tacrolimus on PMNs. Tacrolimus's primary target is T cells via the calcineurin pathway. It does not have a direct cytotoxic effect on polymorphonuclear leukocytes (neutrophils). Neutrophils are crucial for early bacterial killing, but their function is not the primary target of tacrolimus.
- (E): Antibody-mediated rejection leading to endothelial damage and increased bacterial adherence. AMR involves antibodies against donor HLA antigens, leading to endothelial injury and inflammation. While this can occur, it doesn't directly explain the increased susceptibility to *E. coli* infection in the way that impaired T cell function does. The clinical picture is more consistent with overwhelming bacterial sepsis than AMR, although AMR can increase infection risk secondarily. The primary mechanism of tacrolimus is T cell suppression, not directly related to AMR pathogenesis.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Correct Answer (C) - Tacrolimus Effect | Distractor (A) - Immune Complexes | Distractor (B) - IFN-γ/CD8+ | Distractor (D) - PMN Toxicity | Distractor (E) - AMR |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------- | :------------------------------- | :------------------------ | :--------------------------- | :------------------ |
| **Primary Mechanism**   | Inhibition of calcineurin -> ↓ IL-2 -> ↓ CD4+ T cell proliferation | Immune complex formation -> Complement activation/AMR | ↓ IFN-γ -> ↓ Macrophage activation | Direct PMN killing          | Antibody-mediated endothelial damage |
| **Target Cell**         | T lymphocytes (primarily CD4+)          | Endothelium, Complement system  | Macrophages, CD8+ T cells | PMNs                         | Endothelial cells   |
| **Key Molecule**        | Calcineurin, NFAT, IL-2                 | Antibodies, HLA antigens, C3b   | IFN-γ, MHC Class I        | N/A                          | Donor HLA antibodies |
| **Effect on Infection** | Impaired cell-mediated immunity, ↑ susceptibility to opportunistic infections (including Gram-negatives) | Primarily rejection, secondary ↑ infection risk | Impaired intracellular killing | Impaired bacterial killing | Primarily rejection, secondary ↑ infection risk |
| **Vignette Support**    | Tacrolimus use, ↓ CD4+ T cells, *E. coli* infection | ABO incompatibility, but clinical picture is sepsis | Stable CD8+ T cells, *E. coli* is extracellular | No evidence of PMN toxicity | No specific signs of AMR |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Tacrolimus and cyclosporine are calcineurin inhibitors that primarily suppress T cell activation by blocking IL-2 production, leading to impaired cell-mediated immunity and increased susceptibility to opportunistic infections, including intracellular pathogens and certain extracellular bacteria like *E. coli*. This effect is more pronounced on CD4+ T cells. Differentiate this mechanism from other immunosuppressants (e.g., mycophenolate mofetil inhibits purine synthesis affecting all lymphocytes, prednisone has broad anti-inflammatory effects) and other causes of infection in transplant recipients (e.g., CMV, *Pneumocystis jirovecii*, *Aspergillus*). Remember that impaired T cell function increases risk for both intracellular and extracellular pathogens, while B cell defects increase risk for encapsulated bacteria.
