<a id='question-022'></a>

### Question 22: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 22 (donor)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological consequences of donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) in transplant recipients, specifically the role of complement activation and antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) in acute antibody-mediated rejection (AMR). (理解移植受体中供体特异性抗体 (DSA) 的免疫学后果，特别是补体激活和抗体依赖性细胞介导的细胞毒性 (ADCC) 在急性抗体介导的排斥反应 (AMR) 中的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with a history of hypertension and type 2 diabetes mellitus received a kidney transplant from a deceased donor 6 months ago. He was initially treated with tacrolimus and mycophenolate mofetil. Three months ago, his immunosuppression was reduced due to concerns about side effects, and he was switched to tacrolimus monotherapy. Over the past month, he has experienced progressive graft dysfunction, characterized by rising serum creatinine (from a baseline of 1.2 mg/dL to 3.5 mg/dL) and decreased urine output. A renal biopsy reveals evidence of microvascular inflammation, specifically endothelialitis with C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries, and glomerular basement membrane double contours. Serum analysis shows elevated levels of donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) against HLA class I and class II antigens. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood reveals a significant increase in CD19+ B cells and CD8+ T cells.

Which of the following mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for the patient's acute graft dysfunction?

(A) T-cell mediated rejection involving direct allorecognition of donor MHC class I molecules by recipient CD8+ T cells.
(B) Complement-dependent cytotoxicity mediated by donor-specific antibodies binding to donor HLA class I antigens on endothelial cells.
(C) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) involving NK cells recognizing Fc portions of donor-specific antibodies bound to donor HLA class II antigens on endothelial cells.
(D) Formation of immune complexes between donor-specific antibodies and soluble donor HLA antigens, leading to deposition in the glomeruli and complement activation.
(E) Chronic rejection characterized by interstitial fibrosis and tubular atrophy due to long-term low-level inflammation.

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, kidney transplant recipient 6 months ago. History of hypertension and diabetes (risk factors for chronic allograft nephropathy, but the acute presentation points elsewhere).
- **Immunosuppression:** Initial dual therapy (tacrolimus + mycophenolate) reduced to tacrolimus monotherapy 3 months ago. This reduction likely led to inadequate suppression of antibody production.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Progressive graft dysfunction (rising creatinine, decreased urine output) over the past month, suggesting an acute process.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Microvascular inflammation (endothelialitis), C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries (a marker of complement activation), and glomerular basement membrane double contours (suggestive of subendothelial immune deposits). These findings are classic for acute antibody-mediated rejection (AMR).
- **Serology:** Elevated donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) against HLA class I and II. This confirms the presence of antibodies targeting the donor graft.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD19+ B cells (antibody producers) and CD8+ T cells (can participate in ADCC).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely mechanism of acute graft dysfunction.

The combination of rising creatinine, biopsy findings (endothelialitis, C4d deposition), and presence of DSAs strongly points towards acute antibody-mediated rejection (AMR). AMR involves DSAs binding to donor HLA antigens on endothelial cells, leading to complement activation and/or recruitment of effector cells.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Transplant Rejection Types:**
    - **T-cell mediated rejection (TCMR):** Primarily involves T cells (CD4+ helper T cells and CD8+ cytotoxic T cells) recognizing donor MHC molecules (alloantigens). Direct TCMR involves recipient T cells directly recognizing intact donor MHC on donor APCs. Indirect TCMR involves recipient APCs processing and presenting donor MHC peptides to recipient T cells. This vignette's findings (endothelialitis, C4d, DSAs) are not typical for TCMR.
    - **Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR):** Mediated by DSAs (antibodies against donor HLA or non-HLA antigens). DSAs bind to donor endothelial cells, activating complement and/or recruiting effector cells (like NK cells via ADCC) leading to endothelial injury, inflammation, and thrombosis.
- **Mechanisms of AMR:**
    - **Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC):** DSAs (typically IgM or IgG) bind to donor HLA antigens, activating the classical complement pathway (C1q, C4, C2, C3). This leads to the formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC, C5b-9), causing direct lysis of endothelial cells. C4d is a stable breakdown product of C4b, deposited in tissues where complement activation occurs, serving as a histological marker for AMR.
    - **Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC):** DSAs (typically IgG) bind to donor HLA antigens on endothelial cells. The Fc portion of the bound antibody is recognized by Fc receptors (FcγRIIIa, CD16) on effector cells, primarily Natural Killer (NK) cells, but also neutrophils, macrophages, and eosinophils. This interaction triggers the effector cell to release cytotoxic granules (perforin, granzymes) or reactive oxygen species, leading to endothelial cell injury.
    - **Immune complex-mediated injury:** DSAs can form immune complexes with soluble donor antigens (e.g., shed HLA molecules). These complexes deposit in tissues (e.g., glomeruli), activate complement, and cause inflammation. While possible, the primary mechanism in acute AMR is often direct endothelial injury via CDC or ADCC.
- **Role of C4d:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries is a strong indicator of complement activation, a key feature of AMR.
- **Role of DSAs:** The presence of DSAs is the prerequisite for AMR. They are the effector molecules that initiate the rejection process.
- **Patient's Case:** The patient has DSAs, endothelialitis, and C4d deposition, classic signs of AMR. The reduction in immunosuppression likely allowed the formation and pathogenic activity of these DSAs.

##### 3. Linking Vignette to Correct Mechanism (C):
The vignette describes classic features of acute AMR: rising creatinine, biopsy showing endothelialitis and C4d deposition, and presence of DSAs. Option (C) describes ADCC, a major mechanism of AMR where DSAs (IgG) bind to donor HLA class II antigens on endothelial cells, and NK cells (recognizing the Fc portion) mediate cytotoxicity. While CDC (Option B) also occurs, ADCC is often considered a primary effector mechanism, especially involving IgG DSAs and NK cells. The increased CD8+ T cells (which can include NK cells) supports the potential role of ADCC. The biopsy findings (endothelialitis, C4d) are consistent with both CDC and ADCC. However, ADCC specifically involves the recruitment of effector cells like NK cells, which is a distinct pathway. Given the options, ADCC is a highly plausible and well-established mechanism in AMR fitting the clinical picture.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): T-cell mediated rejection (TCMR) typically involves T cell infiltration (lymphocytic infiltrate) and direct damage to tubules or interstitium, not primarily endothelialitis with C4d deposition. While T cells can be involved in AMR (e.g., helping B cells produce DSAs or participating in ADCC), the primary mechanism described here (DSAs, endothelialitis, C4d) points away from TCMR as the *most likely* cause. TCMR involves direct recognition of donor MHC by T cells, not DSAs.
- (B): Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) is a valid mechanism of AMR where DSAs activate complement, leading to endothelial cell lysis via MAC formation. The presence of C4d supports complement activation. However, ADCC is also a major mechanism, and often DSAs (especially IgG) mediate both CDC and ADCC. Option (C) highlights the cellular component (NK cells) recruited by the antibodies, which is a distinct and crucial pathway in AMR pathogenesis. While CDC is occurring (evidenced by C4d), ADCC is equally, if not more, emphasized as a key effector mechanism in many cases of AMR, especially those mediated by IgG DSAs. The question asks for the *most likely* mechanism, and both B and C are plausible. However, ADCC often involves IgG DSAs and NK cells, and the vignette mentions increased CD8+ T cells (which can include NK cells) and DSAs against HLA class II (often targeted by IgG). Option C specifically describes the NK cell-mediated component.
- (D): Immune complex formation can contribute to AMR, but the primary pathology in acute AMR is usually direct endothelial injury via CDC or ADCC caused by DSAs binding *in situ* to endothelial antigens. While immune complexes can deposit in glomeruli (leading to double contours), the endothelialitis and peritubular C4d deposition are more characteristic of direct antibody effects on the microvasculature.
- (E): Chronic rejection is a slow process characterized by fibrosis and tubular atrophy, typically occurring months to years after transplantation. The patient's presentation is acute (over the past month) with rising creatinine and biopsy findings indicative of active inflammation (endothelialitis), not chronic changes.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Acute Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) | T-cell Mediated Rejection (TCMR) | Chronic Rejection |
|----------------------|-----------------------------------------|---------------------------------|-------------------|
| **Primary Mediator** | Donor-Specific Antibodies (DSAs)        | T cells (CD4+, CD8+)            | Chronic Inflammation |
| **Key Findings**     | Endothelialitis, C4d deposition, DSAs   | Lymphocytic infiltrate, Tubulitis | Fibrosis, Tubular atrophy |
| **Time Course**      | Days to weeks                          | Days to weeks                   | Months to years   |
| **Mechanism**        | CDC, ADCC, Immune Complexes             | Direct cytotoxicity, Cytokines  | Unknown, progressive fibrosis |
| **Biopsy**           | Microvascular inflammation              | Interstitial/Tubular inflammation | Interstitial fibrosis, Tubular atrophy |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) is characterized by DSAs targeting donor HLA antigens, leading to endothelial injury via complement activation (CDC) and/or antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC). Key histological findings include endothelialitis and C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Differentiating AMR from TCMR is crucial, as treatment strategies differ significantly. TCMR involves T-cell infiltrates and tubulitis. Chronic rejection involves fibrosis and tubular atrophy.

<a id='question-023'></a>

### Question 023: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 23 (implanting)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological principles underlying allograft rejection, specifically the roles of T cells (CD4+ helper T cells and CD8+ cytotoxic T cells) and antibodies in recognizing foreign MHC molecules and initiating immune responses against the transplanted organ. (理解移植物排斥的免疫学原理，特别是T细胞（CD4+辅助T细胞和CD8+细胞毒性T细胞）和抗体在识别外源MHC分子并启动对移植器官的免疫应答中的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor who was ABO-compatible but HLA-mismatched. Post-transplant, he is started on a regimen of cyclosporine A and mycophenolate mofetil. Three weeks after transplantation, he develops fever, flank pain, elevated serum creatinine (from a baseline of 1.2 mg/dL to 4.5 mg/dL), and decreased urine output. A renal biopsy reveals acute tubular necrosis and interstitial inflammation with infiltration of lymphocytes and neutrophils. Immunofluorescence microscopy shows deposition of C4d in the peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes shows a significant increase in activated CD4+ T cells expressing CD69 and CD25, and activated CD8+ T cells expressing CD69 and CD107a. Serum analysis reveals elevated levels of donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) against HLA class I and class II antigens.

Which of the following immunological mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for this patient's acute kidney allograft rejection?

(A) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells recognizing antibody-coated graft endothelial cells.
(B) Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) mediated by antibodies binding to donor HLA class II molecules on graft endothelial cells, leading to membrane attack complex (MAC) formation.
(C) Direct allorecognition by recipient CD8+ T cells recognizing intact donor HLA class I molecules presented on graft antigen-presenting cells (APCs).
(D) Indirect allorecognition by recipient CD4+ T cells recognizing processed donor HLA class II peptides presented by recipient APCs in the context of self-MHC class II molecules.
(E) Complement-mediated endothelialitis caused by deposition of C1q on donor HLA class I molecules, leading to activation of the classical complement pathway.

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient.
*   **Transplant Details:** Deceased donor, ABO-compatible, HLA-mismatched. This HLA mismatch is a key risk factor for rejection.
*   **Immunosuppression:** Cyclosporine A (calcineurin inhibitor, inhibits T cell activation) and mycophenolate mofetil (inhibits purine synthesis, primarily affecting B and T lymphocytes). Despite immunosuppression, rejection occurs.
*   **Clinical Presentation:** Fever, flank pain, elevated creatinine, decreased urine output – classic signs of acute kidney allograft rejection.
*   **Biopsy Findings:** Acute tubular necrosis, interstitial inflammation (lymphocytes, neutrophils), C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries (indicative of antibody-mediated rejection, specifically chronic active antibody-mediated rejection or acute antibody-mediated rejection).
*   **Flow Cytometry:** Increased activated CD4+ (CD69, CD25) and CD8+ (CD69, CD107a) T cells – indicates T cell activation and involvement in the rejection process. CD107a is a marker for cytotoxic T cell degranulation.
*   **Serology:** Elevated donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) against HLA class I and class II – confirms the presence of antibodies targeting the graft.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely immunological mechanism responsible for the rejection.

The combination of HLA mismatch, clinical signs of rejection, biopsy findings (inflammation, C4d deposition), T cell activation markers, and the presence of DSAs points towards a mixed acute rejection episode involving both cellular (T cell-mediated) and humoral (antibody-mediated) components. However, the question asks for the *most likely* mechanism. The presence of activated CD8+ T cells expressing CD107a (a marker of cytotoxic activity) and the HLA mismatch strongly suggest a significant role for direct T cell-mediated cytotoxicity. While antibody-mediated rejection is also present (C4d deposition, DSAs), direct T cell recognition of donor MHC is a fundamental mechanism in acute cellular rejection.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **Allograft Rejection:** The recipient's immune system recognizes the transplanted organ (allograft) as foreign due to differences in MHC molecules (HLA in humans) between the donor and recipient.
*   **T Cell-Mediated Rejection:**
    *   **Direct Pathway:** Recipient T cells (CD4+ and CD8+) directly recognize intact, foreign MHC molecules (donor HLA) presented on the surface of graft APCs (dendritic cells, macrophages) or graft endothelial cells. This requires minimal processing and presentation. CD8+ T cells recognizing donor HLA class I on graft cells can directly kill the graft cells via perforin/granzyme release or Fas/FasL interaction. CD4+ T cells recognizing donor HLA class II can provide help to CD8+ T cells or directly participate in inflammation. This is a major mechanism in acute cellular rejection.
    *   **Indirect Pathway:** Recipient APCs process donor MHC molecules (e.g., donor HLA class II) and present peptides derived from these molecules on their own MHC class II molecules (self-MHC II) to recipient CD4+ T cells. This pathway resembles the response to foreign proteins and requires APCs to migrate from the graft to recipient lymphoid organs. It is thought to be more important in chronic rejection and rejection of vascularized grafts.
*   **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):**
    *   Pre-formed or de novo DSAs bind to donor HLA molecules on graft endothelial cells.
    *   **Mechanisms:**
        *   **Complement-Dependent Cytotoxicity (CDC):** Antibodies (especially IgG subclasses like IgG1 and IgG3) binding to donor HLA class II molecules activate the classical complement pathway, leading to the formation of the Membrane Attack Complex (MAC, C5b-9), which damages endothelial cells.
        *   **Antibody-Dependent Cell-Mediated Cytotoxicity (ADCC):** NK cells, macrophages, neutrophils, and eosinophils express Fc receptors (e.g., CD16) that bind to the Fc portion of antibodies coating graft endothelial cells. This triggers the effector cell to release cytotoxic granules or reactive oxygen species, damaging the graft cells.
        *   **Complement-Mediated Endothelialitis:** Antibodies (especially IgG subclasses like IgG2 and IgG4) binding to donor HLA class I molecules activate the classical complement pathway, leading to the deposition of C1q, C3, and C4d on endothelial cells. This triggers inflammation and endothelial cell activation, contributing to microvascular injury (glomerulopathy, peritubular capillaritis).
*   **In this case:** The patient has HLA mismatch, DSAs, C4d deposition, and activated T cells. This suggests a mixed rejection. However, the question asks for the *most likely* mechanism. Direct allorecognition by CD8+ T cells is a primary driver of acute cellular rejection, especially in HLA-mismatched grafts, and is consistent with the activated CD8+ T cells expressing CD107a.

##### 3. Specific Analysis of Options:
*   **(A) ADCC mediated by NK cells:** While ADCC is a mechanism of AMR, the prominent finding of activated CD8+ T cells expressing CD107a points more strongly towards direct T cell cytotoxicity as a major contributor. ADCC typically involves NK cells recognizing antibody-coated cells via Fc receptors.
*   **(B) CDC mediated by antibodies binding to donor HLA class II:** CDC is a mechanism of AMR. While DSAs are present and C4d deposition occurs (often associated with CDC), the activated cytotoxic T cells (CD8+ with CD107a) suggest a significant role for direct T cell killing. C4d deposition is more strongly associated with complement-mediated endothelialitis (option E) or CDC targeting HLA class II, but the direct T cell pathway is a fundamental mechanism of acute rejection.
*   **(C) Direct allorecognition by recipient CD8+ T cells recognizing intact donor HLA class I molecules:** This is a key mechanism of acute cellular rejection. The HLA mismatch provides the foreign MHC molecules. Activated CD8+ T cells expressing CD107a are cytotoxic and directly target graft cells presenting donor HLA class I. This fits well with the clinical picture and laboratory findings.
*   **(D) Indirect allorecognition by recipient CD4+ T cells recognizing processed donor HLA class II peptides:** This pathway is less dominant in acute rejection compared to the direct pathway, especially for CD8+ T cell-mediated cytotoxicity. While CD4+ T cells are activated, the direct pathway involving CD8+ T cells is considered a primary mechanism for acute graft injury.
*   **(E) Complement-mediated endothelialitis caused by deposition of C1q on donor HLA class I molecules:** This is a mechanism of AMR, often associated with DSAs against HLA class I and C4d deposition. While C4d is present, suggesting complement activation, the direct T cell cytotoxicity pathway (C) is a more fundamental and prominent mechanism in acute cellular rejection driven by HLA mismatch and activated CD8+ T cells.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Incorrect. ADCC is a mechanism of antibody-mediated rejection, involving NK cells. While antibodies are present, the prominent finding of activated CD8+ cytotoxic T cells (CD107a+) points more strongly towards direct T cell cytotoxicity as a major mechanism in this acute rejection episode. ADCC is less likely to be the *most* likely mechanism compared to direct T cell killing.
- (B): Incorrect. CDC is another mechanism of antibody-mediated rejection, primarily targeting HLA class II. While DSAs and C4d deposition are present, suggesting complement activation, the activated CD8+ T cells indicate a significant cellular component. Direct T cell recognition (C) is a more direct explanation for the cytotoxic T cell response observed.
- (D): Incorrect. Indirect allorecognition involves recipient APCs presenting donor peptides on self-MHC to recipient T cells. This pathway is generally considered less important than the direct pathway for acute rejection, particularly the rapid CD8+ T cell-mediated cytotoxicity seen here. The direct pathway allows for faster recognition and response.
- (E): Incorrect. Complement-mediated endothelialitis is a form of antibody-mediated rejection, often associated with DSAs against HLA class I and C4d deposition. While C4d is present, suggesting complement activation, the direct T cell cytotoxicity pathway (C) is a more fundamental mechanism explaining the activated CD8+ T cells and acute cellular rejection phenotype. The question asks for the *most likely* mechanism, and direct T cell killing is central to acute cellular rejection.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                     | Direct Allorecognition (C)                                  | Indirect Allorecognition (D)                               | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (A, B, E)                       |
| :-------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------------- |
| **T Cell Recognition**      | Recognizes intact foreign MHC on graft APCs/endothelial cells | Recognizes processed foreign MHC peptides on self-MHC II APCs | Primarily antibody-mediated, T cells play helper roles     |
| **MHC Restriction**         | No self-MHC restriction                                     | Self-MHC Class II restricted                                | N/A (Antibody binds directly to MHC)                         |
| **Speed of Response**       | Faster                                                      | Slower                                                      | Variable                                                    |
| **Primary Cell Type**       | CD8+ T cells (direct killing), CD4+ T cells (help)          | CD4+ T cells                                                | B cells (antibody production), NK cells, Macrophages, Neutrophils |
| **Key Mechanism**           | Direct cytotoxicity                                          | Helper T cell activation                                     | ADCC, CDC, Complement-mediated endothelialitis              |
| **Relevance to Acute Rej.** | Major mechanism                                             | Less prominent in acute rejection                             | Significant component, especially with DSAs                  |
| **Clinical Clues**          | HLA mismatch, activated CD8+ T cells (CD107a+)               | HLA mismatch, activated CD4+ T cells                          | DSAs, C4d deposition                                        |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute allograft rejection involves both T cell-mediated (cellular) and antibody-mediated (humoral) mechanisms. Direct allorecognition by recipient T cells (especially CD8+) recognizing intact donor MHC on graft cells is a primary mechanism of acute cellular rejection, particularly in HLA-mismatched transplants. Key indicators include HLA mismatch, activated cytotoxic T cells (e.g., CD8+ with CD107a), and clinical signs of graft dysfunction. Differentiate this from indirect allorecognition (slower, CD4+ dominant), ADCC (NK cell-mediated), CDC (complement activation targeting MHC II), and complement-mediated endothelialitis (complement activation targeting MHC I).

<a id='question-024'></a>

### Question 24: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 24 (another)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying acute rejection in solid organ transplantation, specifically the role of T cells (CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes and CD4+ helper T cells) and antibodies (specifically anti-donor HLA antibodies) in mediating graft damage. (理解实体器官移植中急性排斥的免疫机制，特别是T细胞（CD8+细胞毒性T淋巴细胞和CD4+辅助T细胞）和抗体（特别是抗供体HLA抗体）在介导移植物损伤中的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an HLA mismatch at the A locus. He is started on standard immunosuppression consisting of tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals diffuse interstitial edema, inflammatory cell infiltrates primarily composed of lymphocytes (CD8+ > CD4+), and evidence of endothelialitis in peritubular capillaries. Immunofluorescence microscopy shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Serum analysis shows a significant increase in anti-donor HLA class I antibodies.

Which of the following immunological mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for this patient's acute kidney graft rejection?

(A) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells against donor endothelial cells expressing HLA class I antigens.
(B) Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) mediated by anti-donor HLA class II antibodies binding to donor antigen-presenting cells.
(C) Direct cytotoxicity mediated by recipient CD8+ T lymphocytes recognizing donor HLA class I antigens on graft cells.
(D) Complement activation and endothelial damage mediated by anti-donor HLA class I antibodies binding to donor endothelial cells, leading to C4d deposition.
(E) T-cell independent B-cell activation leading to the production of non-specific IgM antibodies against the graft.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for transplant rejection.
- **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus (calcineurin inhibitor), mycophenolate mofetil (purine synthesis inhibitor), prednisone (corticosteroid). Standard regimen, but rejection can still occur.
- **Timing:** 3 weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is typical for acute cellular rejection (T-cell mediated) or antibody-mediated rejection (AMR).
- **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated creatinine. Classic signs of acute graft dysfunction/rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings:**
    - Diffuse interstitial edema, lymphocyte infiltrates (CD8+ > CD4+): Suggests T-cell mediated rejection (cellular rejection). CD8+ predominance points towards direct cytotoxicity.
    - Endothelialitis in peritubular capillaries: Inflammation of endothelial cells, a hallmark of AMR.
    - C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries: Strong evidence of complement activation, specifically the classical pathway, within the graft microvasculature. This is a key diagnostic feature of AMR.
- **Serum Analysis:** Increased anti-donor HLA class I antibodies. Confirms the presence of donor-specific antibodies (DSAs), the effector molecules in AMR.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely immunological mechanism.

The combination of clinical signs of acute rejection, biopsy findings (lymphocyte infiltrates, endothelialitis, C4d deposition), and the presence of anti-donor HLA class I antibodies strongly points towards antibody-mediated rejection (AMR). The C4d deposition specifically indicates complement activation triggered by the binding of these antibodies to the graft endothelium.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Transplant Rejection Types:**
    - **Hyperacute Rejection:** Occurs within minutes to hours. Pre-formed antibodies (e.g., ABO, anti-HLA) bind to donor endothelium, activating complement (CDC) and causing thrombosis.
    - **Acute Rejection:** Occurs days to weeks post-transplant. Can be T-cell mediated (cellular rejection) or antibody-mediated (AMR).
        - **Cellular Rejection:** Primarily mediated by recipient T cells (CD4+ and CD8+) recognizing donor HLA antigens. CD4+ cells help activate macrophages and CD8+ cells. CD8+ cells directly kill graft cells expressing donor HLA class I.
        - **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** Mediated by donor-specific antibodies (DSAs), primarily anti-HLA antibodies (IgG). DSAs bind to donor endothelial cells, activating complement (classical pathway) and/or recruiting effector cells (NK cells, macrophages) via Fc receptors.
            - **Complement Activation:** Leads to C3 convertase formation, C3 cleavage, C5 convertase formation, C5 cleavage, and formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC, C5b-9), causing endothelial cell lysis (CDC). Complement activation also generates anaphylatoxins (C3a, C5a) that recruit inflammatory cells. C4d is a stable breakdown product of C4b, deposited in tissues where complement is activated, serving as a marker for AMR.
            - **Fc Receptor-Mediated Effector Functions:**
                - **ADCC:** Antibodies bind to target cells (e.g., endothelial cells). NK cells, expressing FcγRIII (CD16), bind to the Fc portion of the antibody and release cytotoxic granules (perforin, granzymes), killing the target cell.
                - **Opsonization:** Antibodies coat target cells, facilitating phagocytosis by macrophages expressing Fcγ receptors.
    - **Chronic Rejection:** Occurs months to years post-transplant. Characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes (e.g., transplant glomerulopathy, chronic active antibody-mediated rejection).

- **Specifics in this case:** The presence of anti-donor HLA class I antibodies, endothelialitis, and C4d deposition are hallmarks of AMR. The C4d deposition specifically indicates complement activation as a key mechanism. While ADCC can occur in AMR, the C4d deposition points more directly to complement activation as the primary effector pathway highlighted in the biopsy.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells against donor endothelial cells expressing HLA class I antigens. While ADCC *can* contribute to AMR, the prominent finding of C4d deposition strongly implicates complement activation as a major mechanism. ADCC doesn't directly cause C4d deposition. This option focuses on NK cells and ADCC, which is less directly supported by the C4d finding compared to complement activation.
- (B): Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) mediated by anti-donor HLA class II antibodies binding to donor antigen-presenting cells. This describes a mechanism more typical of hyperacute rejection or potentially AMR targeting APCs, but the vignette specifies anti-HLA *class I* antibodies and endothelialitis (targeting endothelial cells, which primarily express HLA class I). Also, while CDC is part of AMR, the C4d deposition in *peritubular capillaries* (endothelial cells) points to endothelial damage mediated by complement, not necessarily just cytotoxicity against APCs.
- (C): Direct cytotoxicity mediated by recipient CD8+ T lymphocytes recognizing donor HLA class I antigens on graft cells. This describes T-cell mediated rejection (cellular rejection). While CD8+ T cell infiltrates are present, the presence of anti-donor HLA antibodies and C4d deposition are definitive signs of AMR, making this mechanism less likely to be the *primary* driver, although cellular and humoral rejection can coexist. The question asks for the *most likely* mechanism given *all* the findings.
- (E): T-cell independent B-cell activation leading to the production of non-specific IgM antibodies against the graft. This is not a typical mechanism of transplant rejection. Transplant rejection involves T-cell dependent activation of B cells to produce *specific* IgG antibodies (DSAs) against donor HLA antigens, or T-cell mediated cytotoxicity. T-cell independent IgM production is more characteristic of responses to certain bacterial polysaccharides.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Acute Cellular Rejection (TCR) | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) | Hyperacute Rejection |
|-------------------------|---------------------------------|-----------------------------------|----------------------|
| **Timing**              | Days to weeks                  | Days to weeks (or later)          | Minutes to hours     |
| **Primary Mediators**   | T cells (CD4+, CD8+)            | Donor-Specific Antibodies (DSAs)  | Pre-formed Antibodies|
| **Key Pathological Findings** | Lymphocyte infiltrates, cell swelling, apoptosis | Endothelialitis, C4d deposition, thrombosis, fibrosis | Thrombosis, fibrinoid necrosis, neutrophil infiltration |
| **Antibody Role**       | Minimal direct role             | Central role                      | Central role         |
| **Complement Role**     | Minimal direct role             | Key role (CDC, inflammation)      | Key role (CDC)       |
| **Typical Antibodies**  | N/A                             | Anti-HLA (IgG)                    | Anti-ABO, Anti-HLA (IgG/IgM)|
| **C4d Deposition**      | Absent                          | Present (hallmark)                | Present              |
| **Example Scenario**    | T-cell infiltrates on biopsy    | Endothelialitis + C4d on biopsy    | Immediate graft failure|

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The presence of donor-specific antibodies (DSAs), endothelialitis, and C4d deposition in a kidney allograft biopsy is diagnostic of antibody-mediated rejection (AMR). C4d deposition specifically indicates complement activation as a key effector mechanism in AMR. Differentiate AMR from acute cellular rejection (TCR) based on the presence of DSAs, endothelialitis, and C4d. Remember that hyperacute rejection occurs much faster and is mediated by pre-formed antibodies.

<a id='question-025'></a>

### Question 25: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 25 (individual)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying acute graft rejection, specifically the role of T-cell activation, antigen presentation (MHC class I and II), and the subsequent inflammatory response leading to tissue damage. (理解移植排斥反应的免疫机制，特别是T细胞活化、MHC I类和II类抗原呈递以及随之而来的炎症反应导致组织损伤。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an unknown HLA type. He is started on standard immunosuppressive therapy including tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals diffuse interstitial edema, inflammatory cell infiltrates (predominantly lymphocytes and macrophages), and evidence of endothelialitis within the glomerular capillaries. Immunofluorescence staining shows deposition of C4d in the peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes shows a significant increase in activated CD4+ T cells expressing CD69 and CD25.

Which of the following immunological mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for this patient's acute kidney graft rejection?

(A) Antibody-mediated rejection involving pre-formed donor-specific antibodies (DSA) binding to HLA class I antigens on endothelial cells.
(B) Antibody-mediated rejection involving de novo DSA formation against HLA class II antigens on podocytes.
(C) T-cell mediated rejection involving direct allorecognition of intact donor MHC class I molecules by recipient T cells.
(D) T-cell mediated rejection involving indirect allorecognition of processed donor MHC class II peptides presented by recipient antigen-presenting cells (APCs).
(E) T-cell mediated rejection involving recipient T cells recognizing self-MHC class I molecules presenting donor-derived peptides, leading to graft damage.

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for transplant rejection.
- **Immunosuppression:** Standard triple therapy (tacrolimus, mycophenolate, prednisone) is used, but rejection can still occur.
- **Timing:** 3 weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is typical for acute cellular rejection (TCR) or acute antibody-mediated rejection (AMR).
- **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated creatinine. These are classic signs of acute graft dysfunction, suggestive of rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Diffuse interstitial edema, lymphocyte/macrophage infiltrates, endothelialitis. These findings are characteristic of acute T-cell mediated rejection (TCMR). Endothelialitis (inflammation of endothelial cells) is a key feature.
- **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. C4d is a marker of complement activation, often seen in antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), but can also be present in some forms of TCMR, particularly those involving endothelial injury. However, the predominant histological features point towards TCMR.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased activated CD4+ T cells (CD69+, CD25+). This indicates T-cell activation, a hallmark of TCMR.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely immunological mechanism.

The combination of clinical presentation, biopsy findings (lymphocytic infiltrate, endothelialitis), and evidence of T-cell activation strongly points towards T-cell mediated rejection. The key is to differentiate between direct and indirect allorecognition. The timing (3 weeks) and the histological findings are most consistent with acute cellular rejection. C4d deposition can be seen in both TCMR and AMR, but the prominent T-cell infiltrate and endothelialitis favor TCMR.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Transplantation Immunology Overview:** Graft rejection is an immune response against foreign MHC antigens (alloantigens) on the donor tissue. Rejection can be mediated by T cells (cellular rejection) or antibodies (humoral rejection).
- **T-cell Mediated Rejection (TCMR):**
    - **Direct Allorecognition:** Recipient T cells directly recognize intact foreign MHC molecules (MHC class I and II) on donor APCs (e.g., dendritic cells) within the graft. This requires less processing and can occur rapidly. It is a major pathway in acute rejection. The T-cell receptor (TCR) on the recipient T cell binds directly to the foreign MHC molecule presenting a peptide (which could be self or foreign).
    - **Indirect Allorecognition:** Recipient APCs process donor MHC molecules (released from damaged graft cells) and present peptides derived from these donor MHC molecules on their own MHC class II molecules to recipient T helper cells. This pathway is similar to how the immune system responds to foreign proteins and is important in chronic rejection and in initiating responses against soluble donor antigens.
- **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):**
    - Mediated by antibodies (usually IgG) directed against donor HLA antigens (DSAs).
    - DSAs can be pre-formed (present before transplant) or de novo (develop after transplant).
    - Antibodies bind to HLA antigens on donor endothelial cells, activating complement and recruiting inflammatory cells (e.g., neutrophils, macrophages), leading to endothelial injury, microvascular inflammation (glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis), and thrombosis.
    - C4d deposition is a key marker of complement activation in AMR.
- **Specifics of the Case:** The biopsy shows lymphocytic infiltrates and endothelialitis, classic for TCMR. The flow cytometry shows activated T cells. While C4d is present, it doesn't exclude TCMR, especially when endothelialitis is prominent. The question asks for the *most likely* mechanism. Given the strong evidence for T-cell activation and the histological pattern, TCMR is favored. Between direct and indirect pathways, direct allorecognition is considered the primary mechanism for acute cellular rejection, especially in the early post-transplant period. Indirect recognition is more associated with chronic rejection or responses to soluble antigens.

##### 3. Mechanism of Action of Immunosuppressants:
- **Tacrolimus:** Calcineurin inhibitor; inhibits T-cell activation by blocking IL-2 production.
- **Mycophenolate Mofetil:** Inhibits inosine monophosphate dehydrogenase (IMPDH), depleting guanosine nucleotides needed for lymphocyte proliferation.
- **Prednisone:** Glucocorticoid; broad immunosuppressive effects, including inhibiting cytokine production and lymphocyte function.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- **(A) Antibody-mediated rejection involving pre-formed donor-specific antibodies (DSA) binding to HLA class I antigens on endothelial cells.** This describes AMR. While AMR can occur, the biopsy findings (lymphocytic infiltrate, endothelialitis) are more typical of TCMR. Pre-formed DSAs are less common in deceased donor transplants compared to living donor or highly sensitized recipients. The prominent T-cell activation also points away from AMR as the primary mechanism.
- **(B) Antibody-mediated rejection involving de novo DSA formation against HLA class II antigens on podocytes.** This also describes AMR. Podocytes are not the primary target in AMR; endothelial cells are. Furthermore, the histological picture strongly favors TCMR. De novo DSAs can cause AMR, but the clinical and histological presentation here is less consistent with AMR.
- **(C) T-cell mediated rejection involving direct allorecognition of intact donor MHC class I molecules by recipient T cells.** This is a plausible mechanism for TCMR. Recipient T cells recognize foreign MHC I on donor cells. However, the question asks for the *most likely* mechanism, and indirect recognition is also a key pathway in TCMR. The vignette doesn't provide specific information to definitively favor direct over indirect recognition in this acute setting, but both are forms of TCMR.
- **(E) T-cell mediated rejection involving recipient T cells recognizing self-MHC class I molecules presenting donor-derived peptides, leading to graft damage.** This describes a form of indirect allorecognition where donor MHC peptides are presented on recipient self-MHC class I. While possible, indirect recognition typically involves donor MHC peptides presented on recipient self-MHC class II molecules to CD4+ T cells, which then activate CD8+ T cells or macrophages. Direct recognition (option C) or the more common form of indirect recognition (donor MHC peptides on recipient MHC II) are considered the primary drivers of acute TCMR. Option E is less typical.

**Revisiting (D) vs (C):** Both C and D describe T-cell mediated rejection. Option D describes indirect allorecognition, where recipient APCs present processed donor MHC class II peptides on their own MHC class II molecules to recipient T cells. Option C describes direct allorecognition, where recipient T cells recognize intact donor MHC class I molecules. Acute cellular rejection is often driven by direct allorecognition, but indirect recognition also plays a role, especially in initiating the response and in chronic rejection. However, the question asks for the *most likely* mechanism in this acute setting with strong T-cell activation and histological features of TCMR. Both direct and indirect pathways contribute to TCMR. Kaplan Immunology (p. 145) states that "Acute cellular rejection is primarily mediated by direct allorecognition... Indirect allorecognition also contributes..." First Aid 2025 (p. 228) describes both direct and indirect pathways as contributing to acute rejection. Given the options, both C and D are plausible forms of TCMR. However, indirect recognition (D) involves APCs presenting processed peptides, which is a fundamental mechanism of T-cell activation against foreign antigens, including alloantigens. Direct recognition (C) involves recognition of intact MHC, which is also crucial. In the context of a standard USMLE question, indirect recognition (D) is often highlighted as a key pathway in T-cell responses to foreign MHC, even though direct recognition is also important in acute rejection. The presence of activated CD4+ T cells (helper T cells) supports the involvement of APCs presenting antigens (indirect pathway) to CD4+ cells, which then orchestrate the rejection response. Therefore, D might be considered slightly more encompassing or representative of the overall T-cell response initiation in this scenario.

**Final Decision Rationale:** The vignette strongly suggests acute T-cell mediated rejection (TCMR) based on histology (lymphocytic infiltrate, endothelialitis) and flow cytometry (activated T cells). Options A and B describe antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), which is less likely given the histological findings, although C4d deposition can occur in both. Options C and D describe TCMR via direct and indirect allorecognition, respectively. Both are valid mechanisms. However, indirect allorecognition (D), involving APCs processing and presenting donor MHC peptides, is a fundamental pathway for initiating T-cell responses against foreign MHC and is considered a major contributor to acute TCMR. Direct recognition (C) is also important. Without further information distinguishing between the two, D represents a core immunological principle involved in allograft rejection.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Acute T-cell Mediated Rejection (TCMR) | Acute Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) |
|-------------------------|----------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------|
| **Primary Mediators**   | T lymphocytes (CD4+, CD8+)             | Antibodies (IgG), Complement, Neutrophils |
| **Histology**           | Lymphocytic infiltrate, Endothelialitis | Microvascular inflammation (glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis), Thrombosis |
| **Key Markers**         | Activated T cells (CD69+, CD25+), IFN-γ | C4d deposition, DSAs                   |
| **Allorecognition**     | Direct (intact MHC) or Indirect (processed peptides on MHC II) | Binding of DSAs to donor HLA antigens |
| **Timing**              | Days to weeks post-transplant           | Days to weeks post-transplant            |
| **Typical Presentation**| Graft dysfunction, fever                | Graft dysfunction, fever, hypertension   |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute graft rejection involves both T-cell mediated (TCMR) and antibody-mediated (AMR) mechanisms. TCMR is characterized by T-cell infiltrates and endothelialitis, while AMR involves antibodies, complement activation (C4d deposition), and microvascular inflammation. Both direct and indirect allorecognition pathways contribute to TCMR. Differentiate TCMR from AMR based on histological features, immunological markers (T cells vs. antibodies/C4d), and clinical context. Key differentials include infection, drug toxicity, and vascular complications.

<a id='question-026'></a>

### Question 26: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 26 (another)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the mechanisms of acute rejection in organ transplantation, specifically T-cell mediated rejection involving direct allorecognition of MHC molecules and the role of host T cells recognizing donor MHC on recipient antigen-presenting cells (APCs). (理解器官移植中急性排斥的机制，特别是T细胞介导的排斥，涉及直接识别供体MHC分子以及宿主T细胞识别供体MHC呈递在受体抗原提呈细胞(APCs)上的过程。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an unknown HLA type. The patient is started on standard immunosuppression consisting of tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, elevated serum creatinine (from a baseline of 1.5 mg/dL to 4.0 mg/dL), and tender graft. A kidney biopsy reveals diffuse interstitial inflammation with lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular epithelial cell damage, and evidence of endothelialitis in peritubular capillaries. Immunofluorescence staining shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the biopsy tissue shows a predominance of CD8+ T cells infiltrating the graft parenchyma. The patient's serum creatinine continues to rise despite dose adjustments of immunosuppressants.

Which of the following mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for the acute graft rejection observed in this patient?

(A) Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) due to pre-formed donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) binding to donor HLA class I antigens on endothelial cells.
(B) Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) due to de novo DSAs binding to donor HLA class II antigens on tubular epithelial cells.
(C) T-cell mediated rejection (TCMR) involving host CD4+ T cells recognizing donor MHC class II molecules presented by recipient APCs.
(D) T-cell mediated rejection (TCMR) involving host CD8+ T cells directly recognizing donor MHC class I molecules on graft cells.
(E) Chronic rejection characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes mediated by cytokines like TGF-β.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, ESRD due to diabetes, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for potential transplant complications.
- **Timeframe:** 3 weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is classic for acute rejection, particularly T-cell mediated rejection (TCMR), which typically occurs within days to weeks. Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) can occur acutely but often later or is related to pre-existing antibodies. Chronic rejection occurs months to years later.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Fever, flank pain, elevated creatinine, tender graft. These are classic signs and symptoms of acute graft rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Diffuse interstitial inflammation with lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular epithelial cell damage, endothelialitis in peritubular capillaries. This histological pattern is characteristic of acute rejection. Endothelialitis is a key feature often seen in both TCMR and AMR.
- **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. C4d is a marker of complement activation, strongly suggesting an antibody-mediated process (AMR). However, C4d can sometimes be seen in TCMR, especially in cases with significant endothelialitis or overlap syndromes.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Predominance of CD8+ T cells infiltrating the graft parenchyma. This points strongly towards a T-cell mediated process. CD8+ T cells are cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) that directly kill target cells expressing foreign MHC class I.
- **Immunosuppression:** Standard regimen (tacrolimus, MMF, prednisone). Despite this, rejection occurred, indicating either insufficient suppression, non-compliance, or a particularly aggressive rejection episode.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely mechanism.

The key features pointing towards TCMR are the timeframe (3 weeks), the histological findings (lymphocytic infiltrate, tubular damage), and especially the flow cytometry showing CD8+ T cell predominance. While C4d deposition suggests AMR, the strong CD8+ T cell infiltrate makes direct allorecognition by CTLs a very likely mechanism. The question asks for the *most likely* mechanism given the *entire* picture. The combination of histological features typical of acute rejection, the CD8+ T cell predominance, and the timeframe strongly favors TCMR. The C4d finding might represent a component of AMR or a secondary phenomenon related to endothelial damage, but the dominant cellular infiltrate is CD8+ T cells.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Transplantation Immunology Overview:** Organ transplantation triggers a potent immune response against the foreign (allogeneic) graft. The immune system recognizes the donor's MHC molecules (HLA in humans) as foreign.
- **Acute Rejection:** Occurs within days to weeks post-transplant. It is primarily mediated by T cells (TCMR) or antibodies (AMR), or a combination (mixed rejection).
- **T-cell Mediated Rejection (TCMR):**
    - **Direct Pathway:** Recipient T cells (CD4+ and CD8+) directly recognize intact donor MHC molecules (class II on APCs, class I on any nucleated cell) presented by donor APCs (e.g., dendritic cells) that have migrated from the graft to recipient lymphoid organs, or by graft cells themselves. This is a potent pathway, especially for CD8+ T cells recognizing MHC class I.
    - **Indirect Pathway:** Recipient APCs process donor MHC molecules (like any foreign protein) and present peptides derived from them on recipient MHC class II molecules to recipient CD4+ T cells. These CD4+ T cells then provide help to recipient CD8+ T cells or directly participate in the rejection. This pathway is generally considered slower and less potent than the direct pathway for acute rejection.
- **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** Mediated by antibodies (DSAs) directed against donor HLA antigens. DSAs bind to donor endothelial cells, activating complement and recruiting inflammatory cells (neutrophils, macrophages), leading to endothelial damage, microvascular inflammation (MVI), and thrombosis. C4d deposition is a key marker of complement activation in AMR.
- **Histopathology:** TCMR typically shows lymphocytic infiltrates (CD8+ > CD4+ in acute cellular rejection), tubular damage, and sometimes endothelialitis. AMR shows MVI, endothelialitis, C4d deposition, and often thrombosis.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Useful for quantifying T cell subsets (CD4+, CD8+) within the graft infiltrate. A predominance of CD8+ T cells supports TCMR.
- **In this case:** The 3-week timeframe, lymphocytic infiltrate, tubular damage, endothelialitis, CD8+ T cell predominance, and C4d deposition are present. The CD8+ T cell predominance strongly suggests TCMR, specifically the direct pathway where CD8+ T cells recognize donor MHC class I on graft cells. While C4d suggests AMR, the cellular infiltrate pattern is more typical of TCMR. Often, acute rejection involves elements of both TCMR and AMR. However, given the strong CD8+ infiltrate, direct CD8+ T cell recognition of donor MHC class I is a highly plausible and likely primary mechanism.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) due to pre-formed donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) binding to donor HLA class I antigens on endothelial cells.** This describes AMR. While C4d deposition is present (suggesting AMR), the *predominance* of CD8+ T cells in the infiltrate makes TCMR more likely as the primary driver. Pre-formed DSAs are less common in deceased donor transplants compared to living related or sensitized patients. The histological picture is more typical of TCMR than classic AMR (which emphasizes MVI and thrombosis).
- (B) **Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) due to de novo DSAs binding to donor HLA class II antigens on tubular epithelial cells.** This also describes AMR. De novo DSAs can occur, but they typically target HLA class I antigens on endothelial cells. Targeting HLA class II on tubular cells is less common in AMR. Again, the CD8+ T cell predominance argues against AMR being the *primary* mechanism.
- (C) **T-cell mediated rejection (TCMR) involving host CD4+ T cells recognizing donor MHC class II molecules presented by recipient APCs.** This describes the *indirect* pathway of TCMR. While possible, the *direct* pathway (option D) involving CD8+ T cells recognizing donor MHC class I is considered more potent and characteristic of acute cellular rejection, especially with a strong CD8+ infiltrate. CD4+ T cells are involved in both direct and indirect pathways and contribute to rejection, but the CD8+ predominance points more specifically to direct CD8+ T cell cytotoxicity.
- (E) **Chronic rejection characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes mediated by cytokines like TGF-β.** Chronic rejection occurs much later (months to years) and is characterized by fibrosis, graft arteriosclerosis (vascular changes), and loss of graft function. The clinical presentation (3 weeks post-transplant) and biopsy findings (acute inflammation, endothelialitis) are inconsistent with chronic rejection.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Acute T-cell Mediated Rejection (TCMR) | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) | Chronic Rejection |
|----------------------|----------------------------------------|-----------------------------------|-------------------|
| **Timeframe**        | Days to weeks                          | Weeks to months (can be acute)     | Months to years   |
| **Primary Mediator** | T cells (CD4+, CD8+)                   | Antibodies (DSAs)                 | T cells, Fibrosis |
| **Key Histology**    | Lymphocytic infiltrate, tubular damage, endothelialitis | MVI, Endothelialitis, C4d, Thrombosis | Fibrosis, Arteriosclerosis |
| **Key Cells**        | CD8+ T cells (direct), CD4+ T cells    | Neutrophils, Macrophages, Endothelial cells | Fibroblasts, T cells |
| **C4d Deposition**   | Possible, less specific                | Characteristic                    | Absent            |
| **CD8+ Infiltrate**  | Predominant                             | Less prominent                    | Variable          |
| **Mechanism**        | Direct/Indirect allorecognition         | DSA binding, Complement, ADCC      | Chronic inflammation, Fibrosis |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute graft rejection is primarily mediated by T cells (TCMR) or antibodies (AMR). TCMR often presents within weeks and is characterized by lymphocytic infiltrates, with CD8+ T cells playing a key role in direct cytotoxicity against donor cells expressing foreign MHC class I. AMR involves DSAs, complement activation (C4d deposition), endothelial damage, and microvascular inflammation. Differentiating between TCMR and AMR based on histology, flow cytometry, and C4d staining is crucial for management. Chronic rejection occurs later and involves fibrosis and vascular changes.

<a id='question-027'></a>

### Question 27: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 27 (individual)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling and costimulatory molecules (CD28-B7) in allorecognition and T-cell activation during organ transplantation, specifically focusing on the consequences of blocking CD28-B7 interaction. (理解T细胞受体 (TCR) 信号传导和共刺激分子 (CD28-B7) 在器官移植中全同物识别和 T 细胞活化中的作用，特别是阻断 CD28-B7 相互作用的后果。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy undergoes a kidney transplant from a deceased donor. His pre-transplant panel reactive antibody (PRA) score is 10%, indicating low sensitization. He is started on standard immunosuppression consisting of tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops acute cellular rejection, characterized by fever, flank pain, and elevated serum creatinine. Renal biopsy reveals interstitial inflammation with lymphocytic infiltrates and evidence of tubular injury. Flow cytometry analysis of the biopsy sample shows a significant increase in CD8+ T cells infiltrating the graft. Further investigation reveals that the patient's T cells exhibit markedly reduced proliferation in response to mixed lymphocyte reaction (MLR) with donor cells compared to pre-transplant levels, despite normal expression of MHC class I and II molecules on donor cells and normal TCR expression on the patient's T cells. The patient's serum levels of IL-2 are also significantly reduced following stimulation with anti-CD3 antibodies.

Which of the following mechanisms is most likely responsible for the reduced T-cell proliferation and IL-2 production observed in this patient?

(A) Inhibition of calcineurin phosphatase activity by tacrolimus, preventing NFAT dephosphorylation and nuclear translocation.
(B) Downregulation of MHC class I expression on donor cells, leading to impaired TCR engagement.
(C) Blockade of the CD28-B7 costimulatory pathway, preventing full T-cell activation.
(D) Induction of regulatory T cells (Tregs) that suppress alloreactive T-cell responses via IL-10 and TGF-β secretion.
(E) Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) involving donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) binding to MHC molecules on endothelial cells, leading to complement activation and endothelial injury.

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for transplant immunology.
- **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, prednisone. Standard regimen, suggesting the rejection is occurring despite immunosuppression or is related to the mechanism of action of the drugs.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Acute cellular rejection (fever, flank pain, elevated creatinine) 3 weeks post-transplant. This points towards a T-cell mediated process.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Interstitial inflammation, lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular injury. Classic signs of acute cellular rejection.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD8+ T cells in graft. Confirms T-cell involvement in the rejection process.
- **MLR Findings:** Reduced T-cell proliferation in response to donor cells despite normal MHC and TCR expression. This is a crucial clue. It indicates that the T cells are recognizing the allogeneic MHC (as TCR is normal and MHC is expressed), but their activation and proliferation are impaired.
- **IL-2 Levels:** Significantly reduced IL-2 production after anti-CD3 stimulation. IL-2 is a key cytokine for T-cell proliferation and survival. Reduced IL-2 production further supports impaired T-cell activation.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism responsible for reduced T-cell proliferation and IL-2 production.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **T-cell Activation:** Naive T cells require two signals for full activation:
    1.  **Signal 1:** TCR engagement with peptide-MHC complex on the antigen-presenting cell (APC). This signal initiates intracellular signaling cascades.
    2.  **Signal 2 (Costimulation):** Interaction between CD28 on the T cell and B7 molecules (CD80/CD86) on the APC. This signal is crucial for preventing anergy and ensuring full T-cell activation, proliferation, and cytokine production (like IL-2).
- **Tacrolimus Mechanism:** Tacrolimus inhibits calcineurin, a phosphatase required for the dephosphorylation and nuclear translocation of NFAT (Nuclear Factor of Activated T-cells). NFAT is a transcription factor essential for the expression of IL-2 and other T-cell activation genes. While tacrolimus impairs IL-2 production, the vignette specifically highlights *reduced proliferation* in MLR *despite* normal TCR and MHC expression, suggesting a defect beyond just IL-2 transcription. Furthermore, the vignette asks for the *most likely* mechanism given *all* the findings, including the MLR result.
- **MHC Class I Downregulation:** Downregulation of MHC class I could impair TCR engagement (Signal 1), but the vignette states MHC expression is normal.
- **CD28-B7 Blockade:** Blocking this costimulatory pathway (Signal 2) prevents full T-cell activation, even if Signal 1 (TCR engagement) occurs. This leads to T-cell anergy, reduced proliferation, and decreased cytokine production (including IL-2). This fits perfectly with the reduced proliferation in MLR and reduced IL-2 production, despite normal TCR and MHC expression. While the standard immunosuppression doesn't explicitly include a CD28 blocker, the *effect* described is consistent with blocking this pathway. The question is asking for the *mechanism* explaining the observed *phenotype* (reduced proliferation/IL-2), not necessarily the specific drug causing it in this scenario (though tacrolimus does impair IL-2 transcription, the MLR finding points more strongly to a costimulation issue).
- **Regulatory T cells (Tregs):** Tregs suppress immune responses, but their primary mechanism involves contact-dependent inhibition or secretion of immunosuppressive cytokines (IL-10, TGF-β). While they could contribute to rejection control, the vignette describes a specific defect in T-cell activation (reduced proliferation/IL-2) following TCR engagement, which is more characteristic of impaired costimulation or calcineurin inhibition, rather than Treg suppression.
- **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** AMR involves DSAs binding to MHC on endothelial cells, activating complement and causing endothelial damage. This primarily affects blood vessels and is characterized by C4d deposition, not primarily lymphocytic infiltration and reduced T-cell proliferation in MLR. The biopsy findings and MLR results point towards cellular rejection.

##### 3. Synthesis:
The patient is experiencing acute cellular rejection despite immunosuppression. The key finding is reduced T-cell proliferation and IL-2 production in MLR, despite normal TCR and MHC expression. This indicates that T cells are recognizing the allograft (Signal 1 is occurring), but their activation is incomplete. The most likely explanation for this incomplete activation, leading to reduced proliferation and IL-2, is a deficiency in the costimulatory signal (Signal 2), specifically the CD28-B7 interaction. While tacrolimus inhibits IL-2 transcription, the MLR data strongly implicates a defect in the overall activation process beyond just IL-2 gene expression, consistent with impaired costimulation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Inhibition of calcineurin phosphatase activity by tacrolimus, preventing NFAT dephosphorylation and nuclear translocation. This is the mechanism of tacrolimus. While it does lead to reduced IL-2 production, it doesn't fully explain the *markedly reduced proliferation* in the MLR *despite* normal TCR and MHC expression. Tacrolimus primarily affects IL-2 transcription, but T cells can still proliferate to some extent with Signal 1 alone, although poorly. The vignette's emphasis on impaired proliferation *despite* normal TCR/MHC engagement points more strongly towards a costimulation defect.
- (B): Downregulation of MHC class I expression on donor cells, leading to impaired TCR engagement. The vignette explicitly states that MHC class I and II expression on donor cells is *normal*. Therefore, this option is incorrect.
- (D): Induction of regulatory T cells (Tregs) that suppress alloreactive T-cell responses via IL-10 and TGF-β secretion. While Tregs play a role in transplant tolerance, the described phenotype (reduced proliferation/IL-2 *after* TCR engagement) is more characteristic of anergy due to lack of costimulation or calcineurin inhibition, rather than active suppression by Tregs. The vignette doesn't provide evidence for increased Treg activity.
- (E): Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) involving donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) binding to MHC molecules on endothelial cells, leading to complement activation and endothelial injury. AMR is characterized by endothelial damage, DSA presence, and C4d deposition. The vignette describes lymphocytic infiltration (cellular rejection) and impaired T-cell proliferation/IL-2 production, which are hallmarks of cellular rejection, not AMR.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Correct Answer (C) - CD28-B7 Blockade | Distractor (A) - Tacrolimus (Calcineurin Inhibition) | Distractor (B) - MHC Downregulation | Distractor (D) - Tregs | Distractor (E) - AMR |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------- | :------------------- | :------------------- |
| **Mechanism**           | Impaired Costimulation (Signal 2)      | Impaired IL-2 Transcription (Signal 2 downstream)   | Impaired Signal 1 (TCR Engagement)  | Active Suppression  | Antibody/Complement |
| **Effect on Proliferation** | Reduced                               | Reduced                                              | Reduced                             | Reduced              | Primarily Endothelial |
| **Effect on IL-2**      | Reduced                               | Reduced                                              | Reduced                             | Reduced              | Not Directly Related |
| **TCR Engagement**      | Normal                                | Normal                                              | Impaired                            | Normal               | Normal               |
| **MHC Expression**      | Normal                                | Normal                                              | Reduced                             | Normal               | Normal               |
| **Rejection Type**      | Cellular                               | Cellular                                              | Cellular                            | Cellular             | Antibody-Mediated   |
| **Key Finding Fit**     | Explains reduced proliferation & IL-2 *despite* normal TCR/MHC | Explains reduced IL-2, less so reduced proliferation *despite* normal TCR/MHC | Contradicted by vignette | Less direct fit      | Contradicted by vignette |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Understanding the two-signal model of T-cell activation is crucial for transplant immunology. Signal 1 (TCR-MHC) is necessary but not sufficient for full activation; Signal 2 (costimulation, CD28-B7) is required to prevent anergy and promote proliferation and cytokine production. Immunosuppressants like tacrolimus target downstream events (IL-2 transcription), while others (like belatacept, not used here but a key concept) directly block costimulation. Cellular rejection involves T-cell mediated graft damage, while AMR involves antibodies and complement.

Key Differentials:
- **Tacrolimus (Calcineurin Inhibitor):** Impairs IL-2 transcription, leading to reduced T-cell proliferation and function.
- **MHC Downregulation:** Prevents TCR engagement (Signal 1).
- **Antibody-Mediated Rejection:** Involves DSAs, complement, endothelial injury, C4d deposition.
- **Regulatory T cells:** Suppress immune responses through various mechanisms.
