<a id='question-028'></a>

### Question 28: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 28 (recipient)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying acute rejection in organ transplantation, specifically the role of T cells (CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes and CD4+ helper T cells) and antibodies in recognizing and attacking foreign MHC molecules on the graft, leading to graft damage. (理解器官移植中急性排斥的免疫机制，特别是T细胞（CD8+细胞毒性T淋巴细胞和CD4+辅助T细胞）和抗体识别并攻击移植排斥上异体MHC分子，导致移植排斥损伤的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease secondary to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an unknown HLA type. The patient is started on standard immunosuppression consisting of tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals diffuse interstitial edema, inflammatory cell infiltrates primarily composed of lymphocytes, and evidence of tubular injury. Immunofluorescence microscopy shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the peripheral blood shows a significant increase in CD8+ T cells expressing activation markers (CD69, CD25). Serum analysis reveals the presence of donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) against HLA class I antigens.

Which of the following immunological mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for this patient's acute kidney transplant rejection?

(A) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells recognizing graft cells coated with DSAs.
(B) Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) mediated by DSAs binding to graft endothelial cells and activating the classical complement pathway.
(C) Direct allorecognition of graft MHC class I molecules by recipient CD8+ T cells, leading to cytotoxic T lymphocyte (CTL)-mediated graft injury.
(D) Formation of immune complexes containing DSAs and graft antigens, leading to deposition in the glomeruli and causing glomerulonephritis.
(E) Activation of recipient B cells by graft MHC class II molecules, leading to the production of DSAs that block complement activation.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for transplant rejection.
- **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus (calcineurin inhibitor), mycophenolate mofetil (purine synthesis inhibitor), prednisone (corticosteroid). Standard regimen, but rejection can still occur.
- **Timeline:** 3 weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is typical for acute cellular rejection (T-cell mediated) or antibody-mediated rejection (AMR).
- **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated creatinine. Classic signs of acute graft dysfunction/rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Diffuse interstitial edema, lymphocyte infiltrates (primarily lymphocytes), tubular injury. Suggests cellular rejection.
- **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. A hallmark of antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), indicating complement activation.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD8+ T cells with activation markers (CD69, CD25). Points towards T-cell activation and involvement in rejection.
- **Serology:** Donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) against HLA class I antigens. Confirms the presence of antibodies targeting the graft.

The combination of biopsy findings (lymphocyte infiltrates, tubular injury), flow cytometry (activated CD8+ T cells), and serology (DSAs) indicates a mixed acute rejection episode, involving both cellular (T-cell mediated) and humoral (antibody-mediated) components. However, the question asks for the *most likely* mechanism. The presence of activated CD8+ T cells and lymphocyte infiltrates strongly points to a significant T-cell mediated component. CD8+ T cells directly recognize foreign MHC class I molecules on graft cells, leading to CTL-mediated killing. While DSAs and C4d deposition indicate AMR, the prominent T-cell activation makes direct allorecognition by CD8+ T cells a primary mechanism.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Transplant Rejection:** An immune response directed against the transplanted organ (graft).
- **Acute Rejection:** Occurs within days to months post-transplant. Can be T-cell mediated (cellular rejection) or antibody-mediated (humoral rejection).
- **T-cell Mediated Rejection:**
    - **Mechanism:** Recipient T cells (CD4+ and CD8+) recognize foreign MHC molecules (alloantigens) on the graft.
    - **CD4+ T cells:** Recognize MHC class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells (APCs) in the graft. They differentiate into helper T cells (Th1, Th2, Th17) and secrete cytokines (e.g., IFN-γ, TNF-α) that activate macrophages and recruit other inflammatory cells, contributing to graft damage.
    - **CD8+ T cells:** Recognize MHC class I molecules directly on graft cells (parenchymal cells, endothelial cells). Upon recognition, they become cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) and kill the target graft cells via perforin/granzyme release or Fas/FasL interaction.
    - **Histopathology:** Infiltrates of lymphocytes (predominantly T cells), endothelialitis, parenchymal cell damage.
- **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):**
    - **Mechanism:** Pre-formed or de novo DSAs bind to graft endothelial cells.
    - **Consequences:**
        - **Complement Activation:** Binding of DSAs (especially IgG) to endothelial cells activates the classical complement pathway, leading to C3b deposition, C5 convertase formation, and generation of C5a and C5b-9 (MAC). This causes endothelial injury, inflammation, and thrombosis (microangiopathy). C4d is a stable breakdown product of C4b, deposited during complement activation, and is a key marker for AMR.
        - **Cell-Mediated Cytotoxicity:** DSAs can bind to Fc receptors on effector cells like NK cells, macrophages, and neutrophils, leading to ADCC.
    - **Histopathology:** Endothelialitis, microvascular inflammation (glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis), C4d deposition, thrombosis.
- **Mixed Rejection:** Simultaneous presence of cellular and humoral rejection mechanisms.

In this case, the presence of activated CD8+ T cells and lymphocyte infiltrates points strongly to T-cell mediated rejection. The C4d deposition and DSAs indicate AMR. However, direct allorecognition by CD8+ T cells is a fundamental mechanism of acute cellular rejection, often considered the primary driver when T-cell activation markers are elevated and lymphocyte infiltrates are prominent.

##### 3. Option Analysis
- **(A) ADCC:** While DSAs are present, ADCC is *a* mechanism of AMR, not necessarily the *most likely* primary mechanism given the strong evidence of T-cell activation. AMR often involves both CDC and ADCC.
- **(B) CDC:** Complement-dependent cytotoxicity is another key mechanism of AMR, driven by DSAs activating complement. The C4d deposition supports this. However, the question asks for the *most likely* mechanism overall, and the prominent T-cell findings suggest a significant cellular component.
- **(C) Direct allorecognition by CD8+ T cells:** This is the hallmark of acute cellular rejection. Activated CD8+ T cells directly kill graft cells expressing foreign MHC class I. This aligns well with the increased activated CD8+ T cells and lymphocyte infiltrates seen in the biopsy.
- **(D) Immune complex deposition:** Immune complexes (antigen-antibody complexes) typically deposit in glomeruli or other vascular beds, causing glomerulonephritis or vasculitis. While DSAs are involved, the primary mechanism of AMR involves DSAs binding *directly* to graft endothelium, activating complement or triggering ADCC, rather than forming circulating immune complexes that deposit elsewhere.
- **(E) B cell activation leading to blocking antibodies:** DSAs are produced by B cells, but in the context of acute rejection, these DSAs are typically *pathogenic* (activating complement or mediating ADCC), not blocking antibodies. Blocking antibodies would theoretically prevent rejection by inhibiting T-cell activation or graft cell function, which is not the scenario described.

Given the strong evidence for both T-cell activation (increased activated CD8+ T cells, lymphocyte infiltrates) and antibody-mediated injury (DSAs, C4d deposition), this is likely a mixed rejection. However, direct allorecognition by CD8+ T cells is a central mechanism of acute cellular rejection, which is strongly suggested by the clinical and pathological findings.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) is a mechanism of antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) where NK cells or other effector cells recognize graft cells coated with DSAs via their Fc receptors. While DSAs are present, the prominent T-cell activation (increased activated CD8+ T cells) makes direct T-cell cytotoxicity a more central mechanism in this mixed rejection picture. ADCC is plausible but less likely to be the *most* prominent mechanism compared to direct CTL killing.
- (B): Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) is another key mechanism of AMR where DSAs bind to graft endothelial cells and activate the classical complement pathway, leading to cell lysis. The presence of C4d deposition strongly supports complement activation. However, similar to ADCC, the significant T-cell activation points towards a major cellular component. CDC is a major contributor to AMR, but direct T-cell killing is the defining feature of cellular rejection.
- (D): Immune complex formation typically involves soluble antigens and antibodies forming complexes that deposit in tissues like glomeruli. In transplant rejection, DSAs primarily bind directly to fixed antigens (MHC molecules) on graft endothelial cells, triggering complement or ADCC, rather than forming circulating immune complexes that deposit systemically. While some immune complex formation might occur, it's not the primary mechanism of acute rejection.
- (E): DSAs are typically pathogenic in acute rejection, activating complement or mediating ADCC, leading to graft injury. Blocking antibodies, which would bind to MHC molecules without causing complement activation or ADCC, are theoretically protective and are sometimes explored as therapeutic strategies, but they are not the cause of acute rejection. The presence of C4d deposition confirms that the DSAs are activating complement, ruling out blocking antibodies.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Acute Cellular Rejection (T-cell mediated) | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) | Immune Complex Disease |
|-------------------------|-------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------|------------------------|
| **Primary Mediator**    | T cells (CD4+, CD8+)                      | Antibodies (DSAs)                 | Immune Complexes      |
| **Target Antigen**      | Graft MHC molecules (alloantigens)         | Graft MHC molecules (alloantigens) | Soluble antigens      |
| **Key Mechanism**       | Direct CTL killing, cytokine release       | CDC, ADCC                         | Deposition in tissues |
| **Histopathology**      | Lymphocyte infiltrates, endothelialitis    | Endothelialitis, C4d deposition   | Glomerulonephritis    |
| **Key Markers**         | Activated T cells (CD69, CD25)            | DSAs, C4d                         | Complement levels     |
| **Example**             | Acute T-cell mediated rejection            | Acute AMR                          | Lupus nephritis        |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute kidney transplant rejection can be T-cell mediated (cellular rejection), antibody-mediated (humoral rejection), or mixed. T-cell mediated rejection involves direct killing by CD8+ CTLs recognizing foreign MHC class I or CD4+ T cell help. Antibody-mediated rejection involves DSAs activating complement (CDC) or mediating ADCC. Key distinguishing features include biopsy findings (lymphocyte infiltrates vs. endothelialitis/C4d), flow cytometry (T-cell activation markers), and serology (DSAs). This case presents features of both, but the prominent T-cell activation makes direct allorecognition by CD8+ T cells a central mechanism. Differentiate AMR from cellular rejection based on C4d deposition, DSAs, and specific histological features.

<a id='question-029'></a>

### Question 029: Transfusion Reaction
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Acute hemolytic transfusion reaction (AHTR) mediated by pre-formed antibodies (IgM) against ABO blood group antigens, leading to complement activation and intravascular hemolysis. (急性溶血性输血反应 (AHTR) 由预先形成的抗体 (IgM) 介导，针对 ABO 血型抗原，导致补体激活和血管内溶血。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and coronary artery disease is admitted to the hospital for elective coronary artery bypass grafting (CABG). Preoperatively, his blood type is determined to be A positive (A+). During surgery, he receives two units of packed red blood cells (PRBCs) for significant blood loss. Approximately 30 minutes after the start of the second unit transfusion, the patient develops chills, fever (39°C), flank pain, and dyspnea. His blood pressure drops to 85/50 mmHg, and he becomes oliguric. Laboratory studies reveal hemoglobinuria, elevated indirect bilirubin, decreased haptoglobin, and a positive direct antiglobulin test (DAT, Coombs test). Urinalysis shows hemoglobin. A blood sample drawn during the reaction is sent for compatibility testing, which reveals that the transfused PRBCs were AB positive (AB+).

What is the most likely immunological mechanism responsible for this patient's acute reaction?

(A) Activation of T helper cells leading to cytokine release syndrome.
(B) Complement-mediated intravascular hemolysis due to pre-formed anti-A antibodies binding to the transfused AB+ red blood cells.
(C) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) by NK cells against the transfused red blood cells.
(D) Formation of new antibodies against minor blood group antigens on the transfused red blood cells, leading to extravascular hemolysis.
(E) Activation of mast cells and basophils by IgE antibodies against allergens present in the transfused blood product.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male, A+ blood type, undergoing CABG. This establishes the baseline and potential need for transfusion.
- **Transfusion:** Received two units of PRBCs. The second unit was AB+. This is the critical incompatibility.
- **Timing:** Reaction occurred ~30 minutes after starting the second unit. This rapid onset is characteristic of acute hemolytic transfusion reactions (AHTR).
- **Symptoms:** Chills, fever, flank pain, dyspnea, hypotension, oliguria. These are classic signs of AHTR, indicating systemic effects of hemolysis and potential kidney injury.
- **Lab Findings:** Hemoglobinuria (hemoglobin in urine), elevated indirect bilirubin (product of heme breakdown), decreased haptoglobin (binds free hemoglobin, consumed during hemolysis), positive DAT (indicates antibodies coating RBCs), oliguria (kidney injury). These confirm intravascular hemolysis.
- **Compatibility Testing:** Confirms the transfused blood (AB+) was incompatible with the recipient's blood (A+). AB+ RBCs express both A and B antigens. A recipient with anti-B antibodies (which A+ individuals naturally have) will react strongly to B antigens.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *immunological mechanism*.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **ABO Incompatibility:** The patient is A+ (has A antigen, anti-B antibodies) and received AB+ blood (has A and B antigens). The patient's pre-formed anti-B antibodies (IgM class) recognize the B antigens on the transfused AB+ RBCs.
- **Mechanism of AHTR:**
    - **Antibody Binding:** Pre-formed IgM anti-B antibodies in the recipient's plasma bind to the B antigens on the transfused AB+ RBCs.
    - **Complement Activation:** IgM is a potent activator of the classical complement pathway. Binding of IgM to the RBC surface initiates the complement cascade (C1q, C1r, C1s, C4, C2, C3 convertase, C5 convertase).
    - **Membrane Attack Complex (MAC) Formation:** The complement cascade leads to the formation of the MAC (C5b-9), which inserts into the RBC membrane, creating pores.
    - **Intravascular Hemolysis:** The MAC disrupts the RBC membrane integrity, leading to rapid lysis of the transfused RBCs *within* the blood vessels (intravascular hemolysis).
    - **Systemic Effects:** Release of intracellular contents (hemoglobin, potassium, ADP) into the plasma causes:
        - Hemoglobinuria (free Hb filtered by kidneys).
        - Elevated indirect bilirubin (heme catabolism).
        - Decreased haptoglobin (binds free Hb).
        - Hypotension (vasodilation, possibly due to ADP).
        - Oliguria/Acute Kidney Injury (Hb precipitation in renal tubules, complement activation in kidneys).
        - Fever, chills (cytokine release).
    - **DAT:** The antibodies (IgM) coating the transfused RBCs are detected by the direct antiglobulin test (DAT).

##### 3. Kaplan Preclinical Immunology and Microbiology Review 2023 & First Aid 2025 Correlation:
    - **Kaplan:** Discusses ABO incompatibility as the most common cause of AHTR. Emphasizes the role of pre-formed IgM antibodies, rapid onset, complement activation, intravascular hemolysis, and systemic symptoms (fever, chills, hypotension, renal failure).
    - **First Aid:** Describes AHTR as a Type II hypersensitivity reaction mediated by pre-formed antibodies (usually IgM) against ABO antigens, leading to complement activation and intravascular hemolysis.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Activation of T helper cells leading to cytokine release syndrome.** This describes a delayed transfusion reaction, often seen with HLA-matched but non-identical transfusions (e.g., platelet transfusions in multiparous women) or certain immunosuppressants (e.g., anti-thymocyte globulin). It involves T-cell activation and cytokine release, not pre-formed antibodies against RBC antigens and complement-mediated hemolysis. The timing (acute, minutes) and mechanism (antibody-mediated hemolysis) are wrong.
- (C) **Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) by NK cells against the transfused red blood cells.** ADCC involves IgG antibodies coating target cells, which are then recognized and killed by NK cells via Fc receptors (CD16). While ADCC can occur in transfusion reactions (e.g., against minor antigens like Kell or Duffy), it typically causes *extravascular* hemolysis (spleen) and is less common and usually less severe than AHTR. AHTR is primarily mediated by IgM and complement.
- (D) **Formation of new antibodies against minor blood group antigens on the transfused red blood cells, leading to extravascular hemolysis.** This describes a *delayed* hemolytic transfusion reaction (DHTR), occurring days to weeks after transfusion. It involves the recipient forming new antibodies (usually IgG) against minor RBC antigens (e.g., Kell, Kidd, Duffy) present on the transfused cells. This leads to *extravascular* hemolysis in the spleen and liver, typically presenting with a mild fever, jaundice, and a positive indirect antiglobulin test (IAT), not the acute, severe intravascular hemolysis seen here.
- (E) **Activation of mast cells and basophils by IgE antibodies against allergens present in the transfused blood product.** This describes an allergic transfusion reaction (urticarial or anaphylactic). It is mediated by IgE antibodies reacting with allergens in the donor plasma or leukocytes. Symptoms range from hives (urticaria) to severe anaphylaxis (bronchospasm, hypotension). It does not involve hemolysis or the specific lab findings (hemoglobinuria, positive DAT) seen in this case.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Acute Hemolytic Transfusion Reaction (AHTR) | Delayed Hemolytic Transfusion Reaction (DHTR) | Allergic Transfusion Reaction | Febrile Non-Hemolytic Transfusion Reaction (FNHTR) | Transfusion-Related Acute Lung Injury (TRALI) | Cytokine Release Syndrome (CRS) |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------ | :--------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------- |
| **Timing**              | Minutes                                     | Days to Weeks                                | Minutes                      | Minutes to Hours                                  | Hours                                          | Hours to Days                  |
| **Antibody Type**       | Pre-formed IgM (usually anti-A or anti-B)   | Newly formed IgG (against minor antigens)    | IgE                          | None (or pre-formed anti-leukocyte/cytokine)      | Anti-HLA/HNA antibodies                        | T-cell activation             |
| **Hemolysis Type**      | Intravascular                               | Extravascular                                | No hemolysis                 | No hemolysis                                      | No hemolysis                                   | No hemolysis                  |
| **Key Symptoms**        | Fever, chills, hypotension, flank pain, oliguria | Mild fever, jaundice, anemia                  | Hives, itching, anaphylaxis   | Fever, chills                                     | Dyspnea, hypoxia, pulmonary edema              | Fever, chills, hypotension, rash |
| **Key Labs**            | Hemoglobinuria, +DAT, ↓Haptoglobin          | +IAT, ↑Indirect Bilirubin, ↓Haptoglobin       | None specific                | None specific                                     | None specific                                   | Cytokine elevation            |
| **Mechanism**           | IgM binds RBCs -> Complement -> Intravascular Hemolysis | IgG binds RBCs -> Extravascular Hemolysis (Spleen) | IgE binds mast cells/basophils | Leukocyte antibodies/cytokines | Donor antibodies react with recipient leukocytes | T-cell activation/cytokine release |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
AHTR is a classic example of a Type II hypersensitivity reaction mediated by pre-formed IgM antibodies against ABO blood group antigens, leading to rapid complement activation and intravascular hemolysis. Differentiate it from DHTR (delayed, IgG, extravascular), allergic reactions (IgE, no hemolysis), FNHTR (fever, no hemolysis), TRALI (lung injury), and CRS (T-cell mediated). The rapid onset and signs of intravascular hemolysis (hemoglobinuria, hypotension, positive DAT) are key features.

<a id='question-030'></a>

### Question 030: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 30 (special)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying acute rejection in solid organ transplantation, specifically the role of T cells (CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes and CD4+ helper T cells) and antibodies (pre-formed or de novo) in mediating graft damage, and the distinction between hyperacute, acute, and chronic rejection types. (理解实体器官移植中急性排斥的免疫机制，特别是T细胞（CD8+细胞毒性T淋巴细胞和CD4+辅助T细胞）和抗体（预先存在的或新产生的）在介导移植物损伤中的作用，以及超急性、急性、慢性排斥类型的区别。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an unknown ABO blood type and negative crossmatch. He is immediately started on immunosuppressive therapy consisting of tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals diffuse interstitial edema, inflammatory cell infiltrates primarily composed of lymphocytes (CD3+, CD8+), and evidence of tubular epithelial cell necrosis. Immunofluorescence microscopy shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Serum analysis shows elevated levels of donor-specific antibodies (DSA) against HLA class I antigens.

Which of the following immunological mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for this patient's acute kidney graft rejection?

(A) Antibody-mediated complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) targeting donor endothelial cells, leading to microvascular injury.
(B) CD4+ T helper cell-mediated activation of macrophages, resulting in fibrosis and chronic graft dysfunction.
(C) CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocyte (CTL)-mediated direct killing of donor tubular epithelial cells.
(D) Pre-formed maternal antibodies against minor histocompatibility antigens in the graft, causing hyperacute rejection.
(E) Neutrophil-mediated inflammation triggered by ischemia-reperfusion injury during the transplantation procedure.

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, ESRD due to diabetes, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for transplant rejection.
- **Transplant Details:** Deceased donor, unknown ABO type (though crossmatch was negative, ABO mismatch can still occur or be missed), negative crossmatch (initially suggests no pre-formed DSA).
- **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus (calcineurin inhibitor), mycophenolate mofetil (purine synthesis inhibitor), prednisone (corticosteroid). Standard triple therapy, but rejection can still occur.
- **Timing:** Three weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is characteristic of acute rejection, particularly T-cell mediated rejection (TCMR) or antibody-mediated rejection (ABMR).
- **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated creatinine. Classic signs of acute graft dysfunction/rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings:**
    - Diffuse interstitial edema, lymphocyte infiltrates (CD3+, CD8+): Suggests T-cell mediated inflammation. The presence of CD8+ cells points towards direct cytotoxicity.
    - Tubular epithelial cell necrosis: Consistent with damage caused by cytotoxic T cells or antibody-mediated injury.
    - C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries: A marker of complement activation, often seen in ABMR, but can also be present in TCMR.
- **Serum Analysis:** Elevated donor-specific antibodies (DSA) against HLA class I antigens. This is a key finding indicating ABMR. However, the biopsy findings (prominent CD8+ infiltrate, tubular necrosis) are also highly suggestive of TCMR. The combination suggests a mixed picture, but the question asks for the *most likely* mechanism given *all* findings. The prominent CD8+ infiltrate and tubular necrosis strongly implicate direct CTL killing. The C4d deposition and DSA indicate an antibody component, but the cellular infiltrate pattern is classic for TCMR. The question asks for the *most likely* mechanism responsible for the *observed* pathology, which includes significant tubular damage and CD8+ infiltration.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Transplant Rejection Types:**
    - **Hyperacute:** Occurs minutes to hours post-transplant. Pre-formed antibodies (e.g., ABO, anti-HLA) bind to donor endothelium, activating complement (CDC) and causing thrombosis and graft necrosis. (Option D describes this, but the timing is wrong).
    - **Acute:** Occurs days to months post-transplant. Mediated by T cells (TCMR) or antibodies (ABMR).
        - **TCMR:** Primarily mediated by CD4+ T helper cells (recruiting macrophages, activating B cells) and CD8+ CTLs (directly killing graft cells). Biopsy shows lymphocytic infiltrates, often with interface hepatitis (liver) or tubular injury (kidney).
        - **ABMR:** Mediated by donor-specific antibodies (DSA) binding to donor endothelium, activating complement (CDC, C3d deposition) or Fc receptors on effector cells (NK cells, macrophages), leading to endothelial injury, microvascular inflammation (glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis), and thrombosis. C4d deposition is a key marker.
    - **Chronic:** Occurs months to years post-transplant. Characterized by fibrosis, vascular changes (graft arteriosclerosis), and progressive graft dysfunction. Involves both cellular and humoral immunity.
- **Mechanism in Vignette:** The patient presents at 3 weeks with signs of acute rejection. The biopsy shows CD8+ lymphocyte infiltrates and tubular necrosis, classic features of TCMR. The presence of DSA and C4d deposition indicates an ABMR component. However, the *most prominent* histological feature described, directly correlating with the CD8+ infiltrate, is tubular epithelial cell necrosis, which is the hallmark of CTL-mediated killing. Therefore, CD8+ CTL-mediated direct killing is the most likely *primary* mechanism explaining the observed pathology.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Antibody-mediated complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) targeting donor endothelial cells. This describes a key mechanism in ABMR. While DSA and C4d are present, suggesting ABMR, the *primary* histological finding described is tubular necrosis and CD8+ infiltrate, which are more characteristic of TCMR. ABMR typically causes endothelialitis and microvascular injury (glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis) more prominently than direct tubular necrosis, although it can occur. The question asks for the *most likely* mechanism given the *entire* picture, and the cellular infiltrate pattern points strongly towards TCMR.
- (B): CD4+ T helper cell-mediated activation of macrophages, resulting in fibrosis and chronic graft dysfunction. CD4+ T cells are involved in both acute and chronic rejection. While they contribute to acute rejection (e.g., by helping CTLs), the direct tubular necrosis is primarily attributed to CD8+ CTLs. Fibrosis is characteristic of *chronic* rejection, not the acute presentation described here.
- (D): Pre-formed maternal antibodies against minor histocompatibility antigens in the graft, causing hyperacute rejection. This describes hyperacute rejection, which occurs within minutes to hours. The patient's presentation is at 3 weeks post-transplant. Also, maternal antibodies are relevant in fetal-maternal transplantation, not typically in adult kidney transplants.
- (E): Neutrophil-mediated inflammation triggered by ischemia-reperfusion injury during the transplantation procedure. Ischemia-reperfusion injury occurs immediately post-transplant and causes acute tubular necrosis (ATN), characterized by neutrophil infiltration and tubular damage. While ATN can occur, the presentation at 3 weeks, the specific lymphocyte infiltrate (CD3+, CD8+), and the presence of DSA/C4d point towards immunological rejection, not just ATN.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Acute T-Cell Mediated Rejection (TCMR) | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (ABMR) | Hyperacute Rejection | Ischemia-Reperfusion Injury |
|----------------------|----------------------------------------|------------------------------------|----------------------|-----------------------------|
| **Timing**          | Days to months                         | Days to months                    | Minutes to hours     | Immediately post-op         |
| **Mediator**        | T cells (CD4+, CD8+)                   | Donor-Specific Antibodies (DSA)   | Pre-formed Abs       | Neutrophils, ischemia       |
| **Key Cells**       | Lymphocytes (CD3+, CD8+)                | Endothelial cells, NK cells, Macrophages | Endothelium         | Neutrophils                 |
| **Key Pathology**   | Interstitial infiltrates, tubular injury, interface hepatitis | Endothelialitis, microvascular injury, C4d deposition | Thrombosis, necrosis        | ATN, neutrophil infiltrates |
| **Biopsy Findings** | CD8+ infiltrate, tubular necrosis      | Glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis, C4d+ | Endothelial swelling, thrombosis | Tubular necrosis, neutrophils |
| **DSA/C4d**         | Usually negative or low                | Positive                           | Not applicable       | Not applicable              |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute kidney transplant rejection can be T-cell mediated (TCMR) or antibody-mediated (ABMR), or a combination. TCMR is characterized by lymphocytic infiltrates (especially CD8+ cells) and direct cell killing (e.g., tubular necrosis). ABMR is characterized by DSA, C4d deposition, and endothelial injury. Differentiating between TCMR and ABMR is crucial for management. Hyperacute rejection occurs immediately due to pre-formed antibodies. Ischemia-reperfusion injury causes ATN early post-transplant. The key is to correlate the clinical presentation, timing, biopsy findings, and serology.

<a id='question-031'></a>

### Question 031: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 31 (transplantation)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying acute rejection in solid organ transplantation, specifically the role of T lymphocytes (CD8+ cytotoxic T cells and CD4+ helper T cells) and antibodies in recognizing and attacking allogeneic antigens presented by MHC molecules on the graft endothelium (T细胞介导的急性排斥反应机制).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an A blood type, while the recipient is blood type B. The patient is started on standard immunosuppressive therapy including a calcineurin inhibitor (cyclosporine), an antiproliferative agent (mycophenolate mofetil), and corticosteroids (prednisone). Three weeks post-transplant, the patient develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals diffuse interstitial edema, inflammatory cell infiltrates primarily composed of lymphocytes, and endothelialitis characterized by lymphocytic infiltration into the endothelial cells of peritubular capillaries. Immunofluorescence staining shows deposition of C4d in the peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the peripheral blood shows a significant increase in CD8+ T cells.

Which of the following immunological mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for this patient's acute kidney graft rejection?

(A) Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) due to pre-formed anti-donor ABO antibodies.
(B) Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) due to de novo donor-specific antibodies (DSA) targeting MHC class II antigens.
(C) T-cell mediated rejection (TCMR) primarily involving CD4+ T helper cells recognizing donor MHC class II antigens.
(D) T-cell mediated rejection (TCMR) primarily involving CD8+ cytotoxic T cells recognizing donor MHC class I antigens.
(E) Chronic rejection characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes mediated by non-immune mechanisms.

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient.
*   **ABO Incompatibility:** Donor is A, recipient is B. This is a major incompatibility that can lead to hyperacute or acute antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) if pre-formed antibodies exist.
*   **Immunosuppression:** Standard triple therapy (calcineurin inhibitor, antiproliferative, steroids) is used, suggesting an attempt to prevent rejection.
*   **Timing:** Rejection occurs 3 weeks post-transplant, typical for acute rejection (either T-cell mediated rejection - TCMR, or AMR).
*   **Clinical Presentation:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated creatinine – classic signs of acute graft dysfunction/rejection.
*   **Renal Biopsy Findings:**
    *   Diffuse interstitial edema and lymphocytic infiltrates: Common in both TCMR and AMR.
    *   Endothelialitis (lymphocytic infiltration into endothelial cells): A hallmark feature of acute TCMR, particularly involving CD8+ T cells.
    *   C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries: Indicates complement activation, strongly suggestive of AMR. However, C4d can also be seen in some forms of TCMR, especially those involving CD4+ T cells recognizing MHC class II.
*   **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD8+ T cells in peripheral blood: Suggests a cellular immune response is active.

The combination of clinical signs, biopsy findings (especially endothelialitis), and increased CD8+ T cells points strongly towards T-cell mediated rejection. The ABO incompatibility raises suspicion for AMR, but the prominent endothelialitis and increased CD8+ T cells are more characteristic of TCMR. While C4d deposition is typically associated with AMR, it can sometimes be seen in TCMR, particularly when CD4+ T cells recognize MHC class II on endothelial cells, leading to complement activation. The question asks for the *most likely* mechanism. The endothelialitis and increased CD8+ T cells are strong indicators of TCMR, specifically involving CD8+ cells attacking MHC class I on graft endothelial cells.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **Transplantation Immunology Overview:** Solid organ transplantation involves introducing foreign (allogeneic) tissue, which triggers an immune response. The primary goal of immunosuppression is to prevent this rejection. Rejection can be classified as hyperacute, acute, or chronic, and further subdivided into T-cell mediated rejection (TCMR) and antibody-mediated rejection (AMR).
*   **T-Cell Mediated Rejection (TCMR):**
    *   **Mechanism:** Primarily driven by recipient T lymphocytes recognizing donor MHC molecules (alloantigens) presented on graft cells.
    *   **Cell Types:**
        *   **CD8+ Cytotoxic T Lymphocytes (CTLs):** Recognize donor MHC class I molecules on graft cells (e.g., endothelial cells, tubular epithelial cells) via their T-cell receptor (TCR). Upon activation, CTLs directly kill the target cells by releasing cytotoxic granules (perforin, granzymes) or inducing apoptosis via Fas/FasL interaction. Endothelialitis (lymphocytes infiltrating endothelial cells) is a characteristic histological feature of CD8+ mediated TCMR.
        *   **CD4+ Helper T Lymphocytes:** Recognize donor MHC class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells (APCs) within the graft (e.g., dendritic cells, macrophages, B cells) or on graft endothelial cells. Activated CD4+ T cells release cytokines (e.g., IFN-γ, TNF-α) that recruit and activate other immune cells (macrophages, CTLs, NK cells), promote inflammation, and contribute to graft damage.
    *   **Histopathology:** Infiltrates of lymphocytes (primarily T cells), endothelialitis, interstitial edema, tubular injury.
*   **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):**
    *   **Mechanism:** Mediated by antibodies (primarily IgG) directed against donor antigens, particularly HLA (MHC) molecules. These antibodies can be pre-formed (e.g., due to prior sensitization from blood transfusions, pregnancy, or previous transplants) or de novo (developed after transplantation).
    *   **Pathogenesis:** Antibodies bind to donor HLA antigens on graft endothelial cells, activating complement (classical pathway) and/or binding to Fc receptors on effector cells (NK cells, macrophages, neutrophils). This leads to endothelial cell injury, inflammation, thrombosis (microvascular inflammation - MVI), and ultimately graft dysfunction.
    *   **Histopathology:** Microvascular inflammation (MVI), characterized by inflammatory cell infiltrates (neutrophils, macrophages) within the walls of small blood vessels, endothelial swelling, and thrombosis. C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries is a strong indicator of complement activation in AMR.
*   **ABO Incompatibility:** Pre-formed anti-A or anti-B antibodies (IgM, sometimes IgG) in the recipient can rapidly bind to donor endothelial cells, activating complement and causing hyperacute rejection (within minutes to hours) or acute AMR (days to weeks).
*   **C4d Deposition:** A breakdown product of the complement C4 protein, deposited in tissues where the classical complement pathway has been activated. It is a sensitive marker for AMR but can also be seen in some cases of TCMR, particularly those involving CD4+ T cells recognizing MHC class II.

In this case, the prominent endothelialitis and increased CD8+ T cells strongly suggest TCMR, specifically mediated by CD8+ CTLs targeting MHC class I on graft endothelial cells. While the ABO incompatibility and C4d deposition raise the possibility of AMR, the histological findings are more typical of TCMR.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) due to pre-formed anti-donor ABO antibodies.** While ABO incompatibility exists (Donor A, Recipient B), hyperacute rejection (occurring within minutes/hours) is typically mediated by pre-formed IgM antibodies causing rapid thrombosis. Acute AMR can occur days/weeks later, often mediated by IgG anti-ABO antibodies. However, the *hallmark* histological finding for AMR is microvascular inflammation (MVI), not the prominent endothelialitis described. While ABO incompatibility can cause AMR, the specific biopsy findings (endothelialitis) and increased CD8+ T cells point more strongly towards TCMR.
- (B): **Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) due to de novo donor-specific antibodies (DSA) targeting MHC class II antigens.** De novo DSAs can develop post-transplant and cause AMR. MHC class II antigens are targets for DSAs. AMR is characterized by MVI and C4d deposition. While C4d is present, the key finding of endothelialitis is more characteristic of TCMR. Furthermore, DSAs targeting MHC class II would typically lead to MVI, not the described endothelialitis.
- (C): **T-cell mediated rejection (TCMR) primarily involving CD4+ T helper cells recognizing donor MHC class II antigens.** CD4+ T cells are crucial in TCMR, orchestrating the inflammatory response. They recognize MHC class II on APCs and endothelial cells. While CD4+ T cells contribute to endothelialitis, the description of lymphocytic infiltration *into* endothelial cells (endothelialitis) and the increased peripheral CD8+ T cells are more strongly suggestive of CD8+ CTL-mediated damage. CD4+ T cell mediated TCMR often presents with more interstitial inflammation and less prominent endothelialitis compared to CD8+ mediated rejection.
- (E): **Chronic rejection characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes mediated by non-immune mechanisms.** Chronic rejection typically occurs months to years post-transplant. Histologically, it is characterized by transplant glomerulopathy (in kidney transplants), interstitial fibrosis, and tubular atrophy. The presentation here is acute (3 weeks post-transplant) with signs of acute inflammation and cellular infiltration, not chronic fibrosis. Furthermore, chronic rejection involves complex immunological and non-immunological mechanisms, but the acute presentation rules this out.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | TCMR (CD8+ Dominant)                                  | AMR (ABO/DSA)                                       | Chronic Rejection                                  |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------- |
| **Timing**              | Days to weeks                                         | Minutes/hours (Hyperacute), Days/weeks (Acute)       | Months to years                                    |
| **Primary Mediators**   | CD8+ CTLs, CD4+ T cells                               | IgG antibodies (pre-formed or de novo), Complement | T cells, B cells, Fibroblasts, Non-immune factors |
| **Key Histology**       | Endothelialitis (lymphocytes *in* endothelial cells) | Microvascular Inflammation (MVI), Endothelial swelling | Interstitial fibrosis, Tubular atrophy, Glomerulopathy |
| **C4d Deposition**       | Possible, less common/prominent                       | Common, hallmark feature                             | Variable                                           |
| **Peripheral Blood**    | Increased T cells (esp. CD8+)                         | May have DSAs                                       | Often normal or non-specific                       |
| **ABO Incompatibility** | Not directly causative                                | Can cause Hyperacute or Acute AMR                   | Not directly causative                                |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute rejection in transplantation is broadly divided into TCMR and AMR. TCMR involves T cells recognizing donor MHC molecules, with CD8+ T cells causing endothelialitis and direct cell killing (MHC class I targets), and CD4+ T cells orchestrating inflammation (MHC class II targets). AMR involves antibodies against donor HLA, leading to complement activation and endothelial injury (MVI, C4d deposition). Distinguishing between TCMR and AMR based on clinical presentation and, crucially, biopsy findings (endothelialitis vs. MVI) is essential for diagnosis and management. ABO incompatibility can cause AMR, but the specific histological features guide the diagnosis.

<a id='question-032'></a>

### Question 032: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 32 (frequently)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying graft rejection, specifically the role of T-cell activation, antigen presentation, and the targets of immunosuppressive therapy in the context of acute cellular rejection. (理解移植排斥的免疫机制，特别是T细胞活化、抗原呈递以及免疫抑制剂的作用靶点，尤其是在细胞排斥的背景下。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an unknown HLA type. He is started on standard immunosuppression consisting of tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals diffuse interstitial inflammation with lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular epithelial cell damage, and endothelialitis. Immunofluorescence staining shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes shows a significant increase in activated CD4+ and CD8+ T cells. Which of the following mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for this patient's acute graft dysfunction?

(A) Antibody-mediated rejection targeting donor-specific HLA antigens on endothelial cells.
(B) Complement-dependent cytotoxicity mediated by pre-formed anti-HLA antibodies.
(C) T-cell mediated cytotoxicity against donor MHC class I molecules presented on tubular epithelial cells.
(D) Direct allorecognition of intact donor MHC class II molecules by recipient T cells.
(E) Macrophage-mediated inflammation driven by recipient antibodies binding to donor complement components.

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, ESRD due to diabetes, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for potential graft rejection.
*   **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus (calcineurin inhibitor, inhibits T-cell activation), mycophenolate mofetil (inhibits purine synthesis, affects lymphocyte proliferation), prednisone (corticosteroid, broad anti-inflammatory/immunosuppressive). Despite this, rejection occurs.
*   **Timing:** 3 weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is typical for acute rejection, particularly T-cell mediated rejection (TCMR) or antibody-mediated rejection (AMR).
*   **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated creatinine. These are classic signs of acute graft dysfunction/rejection.
*   **Biopsy Findings:** Diffuse interstitial inflammation with lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular epithelial cell damage, endothelialitis. This pattern is characteristic of acute cellular rejection (TCMR). Endothelialitis (inflammation of endothelial cells) can be seen in both TCMR and AMR.
*   **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. C4d is a marker of complement activation, often associated with AMR, but can also be seen in TCMR, especially in cases with significant endothelialitis or overlap syndromes. However, the predominant histological picture points towards TCMR.
*   **Flow Cytometry:** Increased activated CD4+ and CD8+ T cells. This strongly supports a T-cell mediated process.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely mechanism responsible for acute graft dysfunction.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **Transplantation Immunology Overview:** Graft rejection is an immune response against foreign MHC (HLA in humans) antigens on the donor tissue. It can be classified as hyperacute, acute, or chronic. Acute rejection is further divided into T-cell mediated rejection (TCMR) and antibody-mediated rejection (AMR).
*   **TCMR (Cellular Rejection):** Primarily mediated by recipient T cells recognizing donor MHC molecules.
    *   **Direct Pathway:** Recipient T cells directly recognize intact donor MHC molecules (MHC class I and MHC class II) on donor antigen-presenting cells (APCs) within the graft. This is the predominant pathway for acute rejection. MHC class II is expressed on APCs (dendritic cells, macrophages, B cells), while MHC class I is expressed on almost all nucleated cells. Recognition of MHC class II by CD4+ T cells and MHC class I by CD8+ T cells leads to T-cell activation, cytokine release (e.g., IFN-γ, TNF-α), and cytotoxic T lymphocyte (CTL) activity, causing direct damage to graft cells (tubular epithelial cells, endothelial cells).
    *   **Indirect Pathway:** Recipient APCs process donor MHC molecules (shed from graft cells or presented by donor APCs that migrate to recipient lymphoid organs) and present peptides derived from these donor MHC molecules on their own MHC class II molecules to recipient CD4+ T cells. This pathway is more important in chronic rejection and sensitization.
*   **AMR (Antibody-Mediated Rejection):** Mediated by pre-formed or de novo antibodies against donor HLA antigens, particularly HLA class I and class II antigens on endothelial cells.
    *   Antibodies bind to donor endothelial cells, activating complement (leading to C4d deposition) and recruiting inflammatory cells (neutrophils, macrophages).
    *   Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) can also occur.
    *   Endothelialitis is a key feature.
    *   Often associated with higher levels of immunosuppression or non-adherence.
*   **Mechanism in the Vignette:** The biopsy shows lymphocytic infiltrates (T cells), tubular damage, and endothelialitis. Flow cytometry confirms increased activated T cells. While C4d deposition is present (suggesting complement activation, often linked to AMR), the overall histological picture (lymphocytic infiltrate, tubular damage) and the strong evidence of T-cell activation point primarily towards TCMR. The direct pathway of allorecognition is the most common mechanism for acute TCMR.
*   **Option Analysis:**
    *   (A) AMR targeting endothelial cells: Possible, given endothelialitis and C4d, but the predominant histological findings (lymphocytic infiltrate, tubular damage) and increased T cells favor TCMR.
    *   (B) Complement-dependent cytotoxicity by pre-formed antibodies: This is a mechanism of AMR, not TCMR.
    *   (C) T-cell mediated cytotoxicity against donor MHC class I on tubular epithelial cells: This is part of TCMR, but the direct pathway (D) is the primary mechanism for initiating acute rejection, involving recognition of MHC molecules on APCs, leading to activation of both CD4+ and CD8+ T cells which then attack various graft cells, including tubules and endothelium. Option D describes the initial recognition event more comprehensively.
    *   (D) Direct allorecognition of intact donor MHC class II molecules by recipient T cells: This is the hallmark mechanism of acute TCMR, explaining the T-cell activation and subsequent graft damage seen in the vignette. CD4+ T cells recognize MHC class II on donor APCs, and CD8+ T cells recognize MHC class I on donor cells (or indirectly presented).
    *   (E) Macrophage-mediated inflammation driven by recipient antibodies binding to donor complement components: This describes a component of AMR, where antibodies bind complement on endothelial cells, activating macrophages.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Antibody-mediated rejection targeting donor-specific HLA antigens on endothelial cells. This describes AMR. While endothelialitis and C4d deposition can occur in AMR, the prominent lymphocytic infiltrate and increased activated T cells strongly favor TCMR as the primary mechanism in this case. AMR often presents with more pronounced vascular changes (glomerulitis, thrombosis) and may have fewer lymphocytes in the interstitium compared to TCMR.
- (B) Complement-dependent cytotoxicity mediated by pre-formed anti-HLA antibodies. This is a specific mechanism of AMR, involving antibodies binding to donor HLA antigens and activating complement, leading to cell lysis. This is not the primary mechanism of TCMR, which is T-cell driven.
- (C) T-cell mediated cytotoxicity against donor MHC class I molecules presented on tubular epithelial cells. While CD8+ T cells do kill target cells presenting MHC class I, this option focuses only on MHC class I and tubular cells. The direct pathway (D) involves the initial recognition of intact MHC molecules (both class I and class II) on APCs, which is the fundamental trigger for acute TCMR, leading to activation of both CD4+ and CD8+ T cells that then attack various graft cells. Option D describes the initiating event more broadly and accurately for acute TCMR.
- (E) Macrophage-mediated inflammation driven by recipient antibodies binding to donor complement components. This is another aspect of AMR, where antibody-complement complexes activate macrophages, contributing to inflammation and damage. This mechanism is not central to TCMR.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Acute T-cell Mediated Rejection (TCMR) | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------- |
| **Primary Mediator**    | T lymphocytes (CD4+, CD8+)              | Antibodies                         |
| **Key Histology**       | Lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular damage | Endothelialitis, C4d deposition, vascular changes |
| **MHC Recognition**     | Direct (intact MHC I & II) or Indirect | Donor HLA antigens (esp. on endothelium) |
| **Complement Activation** | Can occur, but less central            | Central (C4d deposition)           |
| **T-cell Activation**   | Hallmark                               | Less direct                        |
| **Typical Timing**      | Days to weeks post-transplant            | Can occur early or late            |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute graft rejection is primarily mediated by T cells recognizing foreign MHC molecules (TCMR) or by antibodies targeting donor HLA antigens (AMR). TCMR is characterized by lymphocytic infiltrates and T-cell activation, often initiated by the direct pathway of allorecognition. AMR involves endothelialitis, C4d deposition, and complement activation. Differentiating between TCMR and AMR is crucial for management. Key differentials include infection, drug toxicity, and recurrence of original disease.

<a id='question-033'></a>

### Question 033: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 33 (practiced)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying acute rejection in organ transplantation, specifically the role of T cells (CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes and CD4+ helper T cells) and antibodies in recognizing and attacking allogeneic antigens on the graft endothelium, leading to endothelial damage and graft dysfunction. (理解器官移植中急性排斥的免疫机制，特别是T细胞（CD8+细胞毒性T淋巴细胞和CD4+辅助T细胞）和抗体在识别和攻击移植物内皮抗原上的作用，导致内皮损伤和移植物功能障碍。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an unknown HLA type. The patient is started on standard immunosuppression consisting of tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals diffuse interstitial edema, inflammatory cell infiltrates primarily composed of lymphocytes, and evidence of endothelialitis with fibrinoid necrosis of peritubular capillaries. Immunofluorescence microscopy shows deposition of C4d in the peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the peripheral blood shows a significant increase in CD8+ T cells expressing HLA-DR.

Which of the following immunological mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for this patient's acute kidney transplant rejection?

(A) Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) due to pre-formed anti-HLA antibodies binding to donor endothelial cells, leading to complement activation and endothelial injury.
(B) Chronic rejection characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes, typically occurring months to years after transplantation.
(C) Acute cellular rejection (ACR) mediated primarily by recipient T cells recognizing donor HLA antigens presented on graft endothelial cells, leading to direct cytotoxicity and cytokine release.
(D) Hyperacute rejection occurring within minutes to hours post-transplant due to pre-existing antibodies against donor ABO blood group antigens or HLA antigens.
(E) Graft-versus-host disease (GVHD) where donor T cells present in the graft attack recipient tissues.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease (ESRD) from diabetic nephropathy, received kidney transplant. This sets the stage for transplant rejection.
- **Timing:** Three weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is characteristic of acute rejection, specifically acute cellular rejection (ACR) or antibody-mediated rejection (AMR). Hyperacute rejection occurs much earlier (minutes/hours), and chronic rejection occurs much later (months/years).
- **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated creatinine. These are classic signs of acute graft dysfunction, strongly suggesting rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Diffuse interstitial edema, lymphocyte infiltrates, endothelialitis, fibrinoid necrosis of peritubular capillaries. These histological features are hallmarks of acute rejection. Endothelialitis (inflammation of the endothelium) and fibrinoid necrosis are particularly indicative of cellular rejection, although they can also be seen in AMR.
- **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. C4d is a stable breakdown product of the classical complement pathway (C3 convertase cleavage of C4). Its presence indicates complement activation, which can occur in both ACR (due to T-cell mediated endothelial damage) and AMR (due to antibody binding). However, in the context of cellular rejection, it often reflects T-cell mediated endothelial injury activating complement.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD8+ T cells expressing HLA-DR. HLA-DR is a MHC class II molecule typically expressed on antigen-presenting cells (APCs). Its upregulation on CD8+ T cells indicates their activation, suggesting they are recognizing alloantigens presented by graft cells (likely endothelial cells or APCs within the graft). CD8+ T cells are key mediators of direct cytotoxicity in ACR.
- **Immunosuppression:** Patient is on tacrolimus (calcineurin inhibitor), mycophenolate mofetil (purine synthesis inhibitor), and prednisone (corticosteroid). Despite this, rejection occurred, indicating the immune response overwhelmed the immunosuppression.

Combining these clues: The timing (3 weeks), symptoms of graft dysfunction, histological findings (lymphocyte infiltrates, endothelialitis, fibrinoid necrosis), complement deposition (C4d), and activated CD8+ T cells strongly point towards an acute rejection episode. The prominent cellular infiltrate and activated CD8+ T cells specifically implicate acute cellular rejection (ACR) as the primary mechanism.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** This is the most common type of acute rejection. It is primarily mediated by recipient T lymphocytes (both CD4+ helper T cells and CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes) that recognize donor HLA antigens presented on graft cells.
    - **Mechanism:** Recipient T cells recognize donor HLA class I (presented by almost all nucleated cells, including endothelial cells) and HLA class II (presented by APCs like dendritic cells, macrophages, and B cells) molecules on the graft endothelium and parenchyma.
    - **CD8+ T cells:** Recognize donor HLA class I via their T cell receptor (TCR) and CD8 co-receptor. Upon activation, they directly kill graft cells (especially endothelial cells) via perforin/granzyme release and Fas/FasL interaction.
    - **CD4+ T cells:** Recognize donor HLA class II. They help activate CD8+ T cells and macrophages, and release cytokines (like IFN-γ, TNF-α) that contribute to inflammation and endothelial damage.
    - **Endothelialitis:** Inflammation of the graft endothelium is a key feature, often leading to fibrinoid necrosis and thrombosis.
    - **C4d Deposition:** Complement activation can occur secondary to T-cell mediated endothelial damage, leading to C4d deposition. While C4d is a marker often associated with AMR, it can also be present in ACR, especially when endothelial damage is significant.
    - **Flow Cytometry:** Activated T cells (like CD8+ T cells) upregulate activation markers such as HLA-DR, CD69, and CD25.

- **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** Mediated by recipient antibodies (pre-formed or de novo) against donor HLA antigens or non-HLA antigens (e.g., MICA). Antibodies bind to endothelial cells, activating complement and recruiting inflammatory cells (neutrophils, macrophages). Histology often shows microvascular inflammation (glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis), thrombosis, and C4d deposition.
- **Chronic Rejection:** Occurs months to years post-transplant. Characterized by gradual loss of graft function due to fibrosis, vascular changes (graft arteriosclerosis), and tubular atrophy. It involves both cellular and humoral immune responses.
- **Hyperacute Rejection:** Occurs within minutes to hours. Caused by pre-formed antibodies (e.g., anti-ABO, anti-HLA) that bind to graft endothelium, leading to rapid complement activation, thrombosis, and graft necrosis.
- **Graft-versus-Host Disease (GVHD):** Occurs when donor T cells (present in the graft, especially from related donors or non-irradiated blood products) recognize recipient tissues as foreign and attack them. This is rare in solid organ transplants but more common in hematopoietic stem cell transplants.

##### 3. Linking Vignette to Correct Mechanism (C)
The vignette describes an acute process (3 weeks post-transplant) with signs of graft dysfunction. The biopsy shows lymphocytic infiltrates, endothelialitis, and fibrinoid necrosis, consistent with acute rejection. The presence of activated CD8+ T cells expressing HLA-DR strongly supports a T-cell mediated process. While C4d deposition is present (often associated with AMR), it can also occur in severe ACR. The overall picture, particularly the prominent cellular infiltrate and activated cytotoxic T cells, points most strongly to Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR):** While C4d deposition is present, the primary histological findings (lymphocytic infiltrate, endothelialitis, fibrinoid necrosis) and the prominent activated CD8+ T cells are more characteristic of ACR. AMR typically shows more prominent microvascular inflammation (glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis) and thrombosis, often with less interstitial infiltrate. The vignette doesn't explicitly mention pre-formed antibodies or typical AMR histology.
- (B) **Chronic rejection:** This occurs much later (months to years) and is characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes (graft arteriosclerosis), not the acute inflammatory infiltrate and endothelialitis seen here.
- (D) **Hyperacute rejection:** This occurs within minutes to hours due to pre-formed antibodies, leading to immediate graft thrombosis and necrosis. The patient's presentation at 3 weeks rules this out.
- (E) **Graft-versus-host disease (GVHD):** This involves donor T cells attacking recipient tissues. It is rare in solid organ transplants (more common in bone marrow transplants) and would typically present with skin rash, diarrhea, and liver dysfunction, not primarily acute kidney graft failure with the described biopsy findings.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR) | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) | Hyperacute Rejection | Chronic Rejection | GVHD (Solid Organ) |
|----------------------|---------------------------------|-----------------------------------|----------------------|-------------------|--------------------|
| **Timing**           | Days to months (typically weeks) | Days to months                    | Minutes to hours     | Months to years   | Variable (rare)   |
| **Mediator**         | T cells (CD8+, CD4+)             | Antibodies                        | Pre-formed Antibodies| T cells, Abs      | Donor T cells     |
| **Histology**        | Lymphocytic infiltrate, Endothelialitis, Fibrinoid necrosis | Microvascular inflammation, Thrombosis, Endothelialitis | Thrombosis, Necrosis | Fibrosis, Vascular changes | Skin/GI/Liver involvement |
| **C4d Deposition**   | Often present (secondary)        | Characteristic                    | Present              | Often present     | Not applicable    |
| **Key Cells**        | CD8+ T cells, CD4+ T cells       | Neutrophils, Macrophages, NK cells | Neutrophils         | Fibroblasts, T cells | Donor T cells     |
| **Vignette Match**   | **Strong Match**                 | Possible, but less likely          | No                  | No                | No                |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute rejection in transplantation is broadly divided into ACR and AMR. ACR is T-cell mediated, typically occurring within the first few months, characterized by lymphocytic infiltrates and endothelialitis. AMR is antibody-mediated, often associated with C4d deposition and microvascular inflammation. Differentiate based on timing, histology, and specific immunological markers. Hyperacute rejection is immediate, and chronic rejection is late-onset with fibrosis. GVHD is rare in solid organ transplants.

<a id='question-034'></a>

### Question 034: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 34 (today,)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying acute rejection in organ transplantation, specifically the role of T cells (CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes and CD4+ helper T cells) and antibodies (specifically, the role of pre-formed antibodies against donor HLA antigens) in hyperacute and acute cellular rejection. (理解器官移植中急性排斥的免疫机制，特别是T细胞（CD8+细胞毒性T淋巴细胞和CD4+辅助T细胞）和抗体（特别是针对供体HLA抗原的预存抗体）在超急性排斥和细胞性急性排斥中的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 58-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor. His pre-transplant panel reactive antibody (PRA) screen showed a high level of antibodies against HLA-A2 and HLA-B7 antigens. The patient is started on standard immunosuppression consisting of tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three days post-transplant, the patient develops acute onset of fever, flank pain, oliguria, and hypertension. Urinalysis reveals hematuria and proteinuria. A kidney biopsy is performed, showing diffuse thrombosis of glomerular capillaries, fibrinoid necrosis of afferent arterioles, and infiltration of neutrophils and macrophages into the interstitium. Immunofluorescence microscopy reveals strong C4d deposition along the peritubular capillaries. Serum creatinine rises rapidly from a baseline of 2.5 mg/dL to 6.0 mg/dL within 24 hours.

Which of the following immunological mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for this patient's acute kidney graft dysfunction?

(A) Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) due to pre-formed donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) targeting HLA antigens.
(B) Acute cellular rejection (ACR) mediated primarily by CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes recognizing donor MHC class I molecules.
(C) Chronic rejection characterized by fibrosis and glomerulosclerosis, typically occurring months to years post-transplant.
(D) Calcuneurin inhibitor toxicity (tacrolimus), leading to vasoconstriction and delayed graft function.
(E) Cytomegalovirus (CMV) infection causing CMV-associated graft dysfunction.

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 58-year-old male, ESRD due to diabetes, receiving kidney transplant. This sets the stage for potential rejection.
*   **Pre-transplant PRA:** High PRA against HLA-A2 and HLA-B7. This indicates pre-existing antibodies against common HLA antigens, increasing the risk of antibody-mediated rejection (AMR).
*   **Time Course:** Symptoms (fever, flank pain, oliguria, hypertension) develop acutely within 3 days post-transplant. This rapid onset is characteristic of hyperacute or acute AMR.
*   **Clinical Findings:** Oliguria, hypertension, rising creatinine (from 2.5 to 6.0 mg/dL in 24 hours) indicate severe, acute graft dysfunction.
*   **Biopsy Findings:**
    *   Glomerular capillary thrombosis and fibrinoid necrosis of afferent arterioles: Classic signs of microvascular injury characteristic of AMR.
    *   Neutrophil and macrophage infiltration: Indicates inflammation and tissue damage.
    *   Strong C4d deposition along peritubular capillaries: C4d is a stable breakdown product of the classical complement pathway. Its deposition indicates complement activation, a key feature of antibody-mediated injury.
*   **Immunosuppression:** Standard regimen (tacrolimus, mycophenolate, prednisone) is in place, but the rapid onset and severity suggest the pre-formed antibodies overwhelmed the initial immunosuppression or were not effectively targeted.

These clues collectively point strongly towards acute antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), likely hyperacute given the very early onset and severe presentation, driven by pre-formed DSAs.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** Occurs when recipient antibodies (pre-formed DSAs or de novo DSAs) bind to donor HLA antigens expressed on the graft endothelium. This binding activates the complement cascade (classical pathway) and recruits inflammatory cells (neutrophils, macrophages).
    *   **Mechanism:** DSA binding -> Complement activation (C3b deposition, C5a generation) -> Endothelial cell activation -> Leukocyte recruitment -> Endothelial injury -> Microvascular thrombosis (glomeruli, arterioles) -> Ischemia and graft dysfunction.
    *   **C4d Deposition:** A key histological marker for complement activation in AMR. It deposits in peritubular capillaries and glomeruli.
    *   **Hyperacute Rejection:** Occurs within minutes to hours post-transplant, caused by pre-formed DSAs (e.g., from prior transplants, pregnancies, or blood transfusions). Characterized by rapid thrombosis and necrosis.
    *   **Acute AMR:** Occurs days to weeks post-transplant, caused by pre-formed or de novo DSAs. Presents similarly to hyperacute but may develop slightly later.
*   **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** Mediated primarily by T cells.
    *   **CD8+ T cells:** Recognize donor MHC class I molecules on graft cells, leading to direct cytotoxicity (perforin/granzyme release, Fas/FasL interaction) and graft cell death.
    *   **CD4+ T cells:** Recognize donor MHC class II molecules (on APCs) or indirectly recognize donor antigens presented by recipient APCs. They release cytokines (IFN-γ, TNF-α) that activate macrophages and promote inflammation, leading to tubulitis (inflammation of tubules).
    *   **Histology:** ACR typically shows tubulitis (CD4+ T cells infiltrating tubules) and interstitial inflammation, sometimes with endothelialitis (inflammation of small vessels). C4d deposition is typically absent or minimal.
*   **Chronic Rejection:** Occurs months to years post-transplant. Characterized by progressive fibrosis, glomerulosclerosis, and vascular changes (intimal arteritis). Both cellular and humoral immunity contribute.
*   **Calcuneurin Inhibitor Toxicity:** Tacrolimus inhibits calcineurin, blocking T cell activation. Toxicity can cause vasoconstriction (especially afferent arterioles), leading to delayed graft function or acute kidney injury, but typically doesn't cause the specific histological findings (thrombosis, fibrinoid necrosis, C4d deposition) seen here.
*   **CMV Infection:** Can cause graft dysfunction, often presenting with fever, malaise, and rising creatinine. Histology may show viral inclusions in endothelial or epithelial cells, interstitial inflammation, and sometimes hemorrhagic necrosis. While possible, the specific biopsy findings (thrombosis, fibrinoid necrosis, C4d) are much more characteristic of AMR.

The patient's presentation (rapid onset, high PRA, biopsy findings of thrombosis, fibrinoid necrosis, and C4d deposition) is pathognomonic for acute antibody-mediated rejection driven by pre-formed DSAs.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Correct.** This option accurately describes the mechanism consistent with the clinical presentation, pre-transplant PRA, and biopsy findings (thrombosis, fibrinoid necrosis, C4d deposition) indicating antibody-mediated injury due to pre-formed DSAs.
- (B): **Incorrect.** Acute cellular rejection is primarily T-cell mediated. Histology would typically show tubulitis and interstitial inflammation, not the prominent microvascular thrombosis, fibrinoid necrosis, and C4d deposition seen in this case. While T cells play a role in rejection, the dominant features here point to antibodies.
- (C): **Incorrect.** Chronic rejection is a late complication (months to years) characterized by fibrosis and glomerulosclerosis. This patient presents with acute (3 days) graft dysfunction with features of acute injury, not chronic scarring.
- (D): **Incorrect.** Tacrolimus toxicity can cause vasoconstriction and delayed graft function, but it does not typically cause the specific histological findings of microvascular thrombosis, fibrinoid necrosis, and C4d deposition seen in this biopsy. These findings are hallmarks of antibody-mediated injury.
- (E): **Incorrect.** CMV infection can cause graft dysfunction, but the characteristic histological findings are viral inclusions, interstitial inflammation, and sometimes hemorrhagic necrosis. The prominent microvascular thrombosis, fibrinoid necrosis, and C4d deposition are not typical features of CMV infection.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) | Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR) | Chronic Rejection | Tacrolimus Toxicity | CMV Infection |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :----------------------------- | :---------------- | :------------------ | :----------- |
| **Time Course**         | Minutes to days/weeks             | Days to weeks                 | Months to years   | Variable            | Variable     |
| **Primary Mediator**    | Antibodies (DSAs)                 | T cells (CD8+, CD4+)           | T cells, Abs      | Drug effect         | Virus        |
| **Key Histology**       | Thrombosis, fibrinoid necrosis, C4d+ | Tubulitis, interstitial infiltrate | Fibrosis, glomerulosclerosis | Vasoconstriction | Viral inclusions |
| **Pre-transplant PRA**  | Often high                        | Variable                      | N/A               | N/A                 | N/A          |
| **C4d Deposition**      | Characteristic                    | Absent/Minimal                 | Variable          | Absent              | Absent       |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) is characterized by rapid graft dysfunction due to pre-formed or de novo DSAs binding to donor HLA antigens, activating complement, and causing microvascular injury. Key histological features include glomerular thrombosis, fibrinoid necrosis, and C4d deposition. Differentiating AMR from ACR based on clinical presentation and biopsy findings is crucial for appropriate management. Remember that high pre-transplant PRA increases the risk of AMR.
