<a id='question-042'></a>

### Question 042: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 42 (immune)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying acute rejection in solid organ transplantation, specifically the role of T cells (CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes and CD4+ helper T cells) and antibodies in recognizing and destroying allogeneic graft tissue. (理解实体器官移植中急性排斥的免疫机制，特别是T细胞（CD8+细胞毒性T淋巴细胞和CD4+辅助T细胞）和抗体在识别和破坏异体移植组织中的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an unknown HLA type. The patient is started on standard immunosuppression consisting of tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and graft tenderness. Serum creatinine rises from a baseline of 1.2 mg/dL to 4.5 mg/dL. A renal biopsy reveals diffuse interstitial inflammation with infiltration of lymphocytes and eosinophils, tubular epithelial cell damage, and evidence of endothelialitis in peritubular capillaries. Immunofluorescence staining shows deposition of C4d in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes shows a significant increase in activated CD8+ T cells.

Which of the following immunological mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for this patient's acute graft rejection?

(A) Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) due to pre-formed donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) binding to HLA class I antigens on endothelial cells, leading to complement activation and endothelial injury.
(B) Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) due to de novo DSAs binding to HLA class II antigens on tubular epithelial cells, leading to complement-independent antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC).
(C) T cell-mediated rejection (TCMR) primarily driven by CD4+ helper T cells recognizing donor MHC class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells (APCs) in the graft, leading to cytokine release and recruitment of other inflammatory cells.
(D) T cell-mediated rejection (TCMR) primarily driven by CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) recognizing donor MHC class I molecules on graft parenchymal cells (tubular epithelial cells), leading to direct cell lysis.
(E) Chronic rejection characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes (graft arteriosclerosis) mediated by alloantibodies against non-HLA antigens and sustained T cell activation.

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for transplant rejection.
*   **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus (calcineurin inhibitor), mycophenolate mofetil (purine synthesis inhibitor), prednisone (corticosteroid). Standard regimen, but rejection can still occur.
*   **Timing:** 3 weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is typical for acute rejection, either T cell-mediated (TCMR) or antibody-mediated (AMR).
*   **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, graft tenderness. Classic signs of acute graft dysfunction/rejection.
*   **Laboratory Findings:** Rising creatinine (4.5 mg/dL from 1.2 mg/dL). Indicates acute kidney injury/graft failure.
*   **Renal Biopsy:** Diffuse interstitial inflammation, lymphocyte and eosinophil infiltration, tubular epithelial cell damage, endothelialitis in peritubular capillaries. These findings are characteristic of acute rejection. Endothelialitis is a key feature often seen in both AMR and TCMR.
*   **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. C4d is a stable breakdown product of the complement C4 complex, indicating recent complement activation. While often associated with AMR, it can also be seen in TCMR, particularly in cases with significant endothelialitis.
*   **Flow Cytometry:** Increased activated CD8+ T cells in peripheral blood. This strongly suggests a significant role for CD8+ T cells in the rejection process.

The combination of clinical signs, biopsy findings (especially endothelialitis and tubular damage), C4d deposition, and increased activated CD8+ T cells points towards an acute rejection episode involving both cellular and potentially humoral components. However, the prominent increase in activated CD8+ T cells and the direct damage to tubular epithelial cells strongly implicate CD8+ T cell-mediated cytotoxicity as a major mechanism.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **Transplant Rejection Types:**
    *   **Acute Rejection:** Occurs within days to months post-transplant. Can be T cell-mediated (TCMR) or antibody-mediated (AMR).
        *   **TCMR:** Primarily mediated by recipient T cells recognizing donor MHC molecules (alloantigens).
            *   **CD4+ T cells:** Recognize MHC class II on APCs (dendritic cells, macrophages, B cells) in the graft. Release cytokines (IFN-γ, TNF-α) that activate macrophages, recruit other inflammatory cells, and directly damage graft cells.
            *   **CD8+ T cells:** Recognize MHC class I on graft parenchymal cells (e.g., tubular epithelial cells). Directly lyse target cells via perforin/granzyme pathway or Fas/FasL interaction.
        *   **AMR:** Mediated by antibodies (DSAs) against donor HLA antigens.
            *   **Mechanism:** DSAs bind to HLA antigens on endothelial cells, activating complement (classical pathway) leading to endothelial injury (endothelialitis), inflammation, and thrombosis. DSAs can also bind to Fc receptors on effector cells (NK cells, macrophages) causing ADCC.
            *   **Diagnosis:** Often associated with C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries (though not specific), presence of DSAs, and characteristic biopsy findings (glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis, transplant vasculitis).
    *   **Chronic Rejection:** Occurs months to years post-transplant. Characterized by fibrosis, graft arteriosclerosis (vascular changes), and progressive loss of graft function. Involves both T cells and antibodies against both HLA and non-HLA antigens.

*   **Key Players:**
    *   **MHC Class I:** Expressed on all nucleated cells. Presents endogenous antigens (e.g., viral peptides, self-peptides) to CD8+ T cells.
    *   **MHC Class II:** Expressed primarily on professional APCs (dendritic cells, macrophages, B cells). Presents exogenous antigens (e.g., bacterial peptides, self-peptides) to CD4+ T cells.
    *   **CD8+ T cells (CTLs):** Recognize MHC class I-peptide complexes. Kill target cells expressing the specific allo-MHC class I.
    *   **CD4+ T cells (Helper T cells):** Recognize MHC class II-peptide complexes. Secrete cytokines that orchestrate the immune response, activate other immune cells (macrophages, B cells, CTLs), and can directly damage cells.
    *   **Antibodies (DSAs):** Bind specifically to donor HLA antigens. Can activate complement or mediate ADCC.
    *   **C4d:** A stable complement activation product found in tissues where complement has been activated.

*   **Applying to the Vignette:** The biopsy shows tubular epithelial cell damage and endothelialitis. The increased activated CD8+ T cells strongly suggest a role for CTLs. CD8+ T cells directly target and lyse parenchymal cells (like tubular epithelial cells) expressing donor MHC class I. While endothelialitis and C4d deposition can be seen in AMR, they can also occur in severe TCMR. The prominent finding of activated CD8+ T cells makes CD8+-mediated cytotoxicity a highly likely primary mechanism.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) due to pre-formed donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) binding to HLA class I antigens on endothelial cells, leading to complement activation and endothelial injury.
    *   **Why Incorrect:** While endothelialitis and C4d deposition are features of AMR, the vignette emphasizes increased *activated CD8+ T cells* and *tubular epithelial cell damage*. AMR primarily targets endothelial cells, causing vasculitis and glomerulitis, although tubular damage can occur secondarily. Pre-formed DSAs are less common than de novo DSAs, and the primary driver suggested by the flow cytometry is T cells, not antibodies. The biopsy findings are more consistent with TCMR, although AMR cannot be entirely ruled out without DSA testing.
- (B): Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) due to de novo DSAs binding to HLA class II antigens on tubular epithelial cells, leading to complement-independent antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC).
    *   **Why Incorrect:** HLA class II expression on tubular epithelial cells is generally low under normal conditions, although it can be upregulated during inflammation or injury. AMR typically targets HLA class I on endothelial cells via complement activation. While ADCC can occur, the primary mechanism of AMR involves complement activation leading to endothelial damage. Furthermore, the strong evidence for activated CD8+ T cells points away from AMR being the *primary* mechanism.
- (C): T cell-mediated rejection (TCMR) primarily driven by CD4+ helper T cells recognizing donor MHC class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells (APCs) in the graft, leading to cytokine release and recruitment of other inflammatory cells.
    *   **Why Incorrect:** CD4+ T cells are crucial in TCMR, orchestrating the response. However, the vignette specifically highlights the increase in *activated CD8+ T cells* and the damage to *tubular epithelial cells*. CD8+ T cells are the primary mediators of direct cytotoxicity against parenchymal cells expressing MHC class I. While CD4+ T cells contribute, the most direct explanation for the observed cellular damage and the specific lymphocyte population increase is CD8+ T cell activity.
- (E): Chronic rejection characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes (graft arteriosclerosis) mediated by alloantibodies against non-HLA antigens and sustained T cell activation.
    *   **Why Incorrect:** Chronic rejection occurs much later (months to years) and is characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes (graft arteriosclerosis), not the acute inflammatory infiltrate and tubular damage seen here. The timeframe (3 weeks) is clearly acute rejection.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Acute TCMR (CD8+ Dominant) | Acute AMR                     | Chronic Rejection              |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------- | :---------------------------- | :---------------------------- |
| **Timing**              | Days to Months             | Days to Months                | Months to Years               |
| **Primary Mediators**   | CD8+ T cells (CTLs)        | DSAs (Antibodies)             | T cells, Antibodies          |
| **Target Cells**        | Parenchymal (e.g., Tubules) | Endothelial Cells             | Endothelial & Parenchymal Cells |
| **Key Biopsy Findings** | Tubular damage, Endothelialitis, Lymphocytic infiltrate | Endothelialitis, Glomerulitis, Vasculitis, C4d deposition | Fibrosis, Graft arteriosclerosis |
| **Flow Cytometry**      | Increased activated CD8+ T cells | May show B cell activation | Variable                      |
| **C4d Deposition**      | Possible, especially with endothelialitis | Common in peritubular capillaries | Less common                   |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute rejection in transplantation can be T cell-mediated (TCMR) or antibody-mediated (AMR). TCMR involves CD4+ and CD8+ T cells. CD8+ T cells directly kill graft parenchymal cells expressing donor MHC class I. AMR involves DSAs targeting donor HLA antigens, primarily on endothelial cells, leading to complement activation and endothelial injury. Key clues differentiating TCMR and AMR include the predominant cell type involved (T cells vs. antibodies), target cells (parenchyma vs. endothelium), and specific biopsy findings (tubular damage vs. vasculitis/glomerulitis). C4d deposition is more typical of AMR but can be seen in TCMR. Increased activated CD8+ T cells strongly suggest a significant role for CD8+-mediated cytotoxicity in TCMR.

<a id='question-043'></a>

### Question 043: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 43 (system)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of specific cytokines (specifically IL-2) in T cell proliferation and function, and how their dysregulation contributes to graft rejection or immunosuppression in the context of transplantation. (理解细胞因子（特别是IL-2）在T细胞增殖和功能中的作用，以及其失调如何导致移植排斥或免疫抑制。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor. His pre-transplant panel reactive antibody (PRA) score was 10%, indicating low sensitization. He is started on standard immunosuppression consisting of tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, and elevated serum creatinine (from a baseline of 1.2 mg/dL to 3.5 mg/dL). A renal biopsy reveals acute tubular necrosis and interstitial inflammation with lymphocytic infiltrates. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes shows a significant decrease in the percentage of CD4+ T cells expressing the high-affinity IL-2 receptor (CD25). The patient's serum IL-2 levels are markedly decreased.

Which of the following is the most likely mechanism contributing to this patient's acute kidney transplant rejection?

(A) Inhibition of calcineurin leading to decreased NFAT activation and reduced IL-2 transcription.
(B) Antibody-mediated complement activation causing endothelial injury and graft dysfunction.
(C) Impaired antigen presentation by donor antigen-presenting cells (APCs) leading to T cell anergy.
(D) Defective T cell receptor (TCR) signaling due to mutations in downstream signaling molecules.
(E) Increased production of regulatory T cells (Tregs) suppressing effector T cell responses.

#### Correct Answer: (A)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 45-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for potential transplant rejection.
- **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, prednisone. This indicates an attempt to prevent rejection, but rejection can still occur.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Fever, flank pain, elevated creatinine 3 weeks post-transplant. These are classic signs and symptoms of acute rejection.
- **Renal Biopsy:** Acute tubular necrosis and interstitial inflammation with lymphocytic infiltrates. This confirms acute cellular rejection.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Decreased CD4+ T cells expressing CD25 (high-affinity IL-2 receptor). This is a crucial clue. CD25 is upregulated on activated T cells and is essential for high-affinity IL-2 binding.
- **Serum IL-2 Levels:** Markedly decreased. This correlates with the decreased CD25 expression, suggesting impaired IL-2 signaling or production in response to T cell activation.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism contributing to acute rejection, given the clinical and immunological findings.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **T Cell Activation and IL-2:** T cell activation requires two signals: TCR engagement with MHC-peptide complex (Signal 1) and co-stimulation (Signal 2, e.g., CD28-B7). Signal 1 leads to activation of intracellular signaling cascades, including the calcineurin pathway.
- **Calcineurin Pathway:** Activated TCR signaling leads to increased intracellular calcium, which activates calcineurin. Calcineurin dephosphorylates NFAT (Nuclear Factor of Activated T cells). Dephosphorylated NFAT translocates to the nucleus and acts as a transcription factor, inducing the expression of genes crucial for T cell proliferation and function, most notably IL-2.
- **IL-2 and T Cell Proliferation:** IL-2 binds to its receptor, which exists in low-affinity (IL-2Rβγ, CD122/CD132) and high-affinity (IL-2Rαβγ, CD25/CD122/CD132) forms. The high-affinity receptor (CD25) is upregulated upon T cell activation. Binding of IL-2 to its receptor triggers downstream signaling pathways (e.g., JAK-STAT) that promote T cell proliferation, differentiation, and survival.
- **Tacrolimus Mechanism:** Tacrolimus is a calcineurin inhibitor. It binds to FKBP12 (FK506 binding protein 12), and this complex inhibits calcineurin. By inhibiting calcineurin, tacrolimus prevents the dephosphorylation and nuclear translocation of NFAT. This blocks the transcription of IL-2 and other T cell activation genes.
- **Connecting the Clues:** The patient is on tacrolimus. Tacrolimus inhibits calcineurin, preventing NFAT activation and thus reducing IL-2 transcription. The decreased IL-2 levels and decreased CD25 expression on T cells (indicating reduced activation and/or impaired response to IL-2) are consistent with the effects of tacrolimus. However, the patient is experiencing acute rejection. This suggests that despite immunosuppression, the rejection process is occurring. The question asks for the *mechanism contributing to rejection*. While tacrolimus *should* prevent rejection by inhibiting IL-2, the *failure* of this mechanism (or insufficient suppression) allows rejection to proceed. The underlying mechanism of T cell activation and rejection involves IL-2 production and signaling. The decreased IL-2 levels and CD25 expression point towards a disruption in this pathway, which is the target of tacrolimus. Therefore, the inhibition of calcineurin leading to decreased IL-2 transcription is the fundamental immunological mechanism underlying T cell activation and rejection, and its disruption by tacrolimus is the intended therapeutic effect. The rejection itself is occurring *despite* this inhibition, but the pathway described in (A) is the core process involved.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Inhibition of calcineurin leading to decreased NFAT activation and reduced IL-2 transcription.** This describes the mechanism of action of tacrolimus, which the patient is taking. While tacrolimus aims to *prevent* rejection by inhibiting IL-2 production, the underlying process of T cell activation and rejection *relies* on IL-2 production initiated by calcineurin/NFAT. The decreased IL-2 levels observed in the patient are a direct consequence of this pathway being inhibited by tacrolimus. The rejection is occurring despite this inhibition, but the pathway itself is central to the process. This option correctly identifies the key pathway involved in T cell activation and the target of the patient's immunosuppression.
- (B): **Antibody-mediated complement activation causing endothelial injury and graft dysfunction.** This describes antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), also known as humoral rejection. While AMR can occur, the vignette describes lymphocytic infiltrates on biopsy, which is more characteristic of acute cellular rejection (ACR). Furthermore, the flow cytometry and IL-2 data point towards a T cell-mediated process. AMR involves donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) binding to HLA antigens on endothelial cells, activating complement and leading to endothelial damage. This is not supported by the provided data.
- (C): **Impaired antigen presentation by donor antigen-presenting cells (APCs) leading to T cell anergy.** While impaired antigen presentation can lead to T cell tolerance or anergy, this is not the primary mechanism of acute rejection. In fact, rejection requires efficient antigen presentation by donor APCs (or recipient APCs presenting donor antigens) to recipient T cells. Anergy is a state of T cell unresponsiveness, which is the opposite of the activated state seen in rejection.
- (D): **Defective T cell receptor (TCR) signaling due to mutations in downstream signaling molecules.** This describes primary immunodeficiencies affecting T cell function. While such defects can predispose to infections or autoimmunity, they are not the cause of acute transplant rejection in an immunocompetent individual receiving a transplant. Rejection is an adaptive immune response to foreign antigens.
- (E): **Increased production of regulatory T cells (Tregs) suppressing effector T cell responses.** Tregs are crucial for maintaining immune tolerance and preventing rejection. Increased Treg activity would *suppress* rejection, not cause it. The vignette describes signs of active rejection, not immune suppression by Tregs.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR) | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) | Tacrolimus Mechanism | Treg Function |
|-------------------------|---------------------------------|------------------------------------|----------------------|---------------|
| **Primary Cells**       | T lymphocytes (CD4+, CD8+)      | Antibodies, Complement, Endothelium | T lymphocytes        | Tregs         |
| **Biopsy Findings**     | Lymphocytic infiltrates, Tubulitis | Endothelialitis, C4d deposition   | N/A                  | N/A           |
| **Key Cytokines**       | IL-2, IFN-γ, TNF-α              | TNF-α, IL-1β, Complement factors   | IL-2 (inhibited)     | IL-10, TGF-β  |
| **Mechanism**           | T cell recognition of alloantigens | DSA binding to endothelium        | Calcineurin inhibition | Suppression  |
| **IL-2 Levels**         | High                            | Variable                          | Low                  | N/A           |
| **CD25 Expression**     | High (on activated T cells)     | N/A                               | Low (on T cells)     | N/A           |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The vignette describes acute cellular rejection in a kidney transplant recipient. The key immunological finding is decreased IL-2 production and signaling, evidenced by low serum IL-2 and reduced CD25 expression on T cells. This points to the critical role of the IL-2 pathway (TCR signaling -> Calcineurin -> NFAT -> IL-2 transcription -> IL-2R signaling -> T cell proliferation) in T cell activation and rejection. The patient is on tacrolimus, a calcineurin inhibitor that blocks this pathway. While the drug aims to prevent rejection, the underlying mechanism of rejection involves this very pathway. Understanding the role of IL-2 and the mechanism of calcineurin inhibitors is crucial for understanding transplant immunology. Differentiate ACR (T cell mediated, IL-2 dependent) from AMR (antibody mediated, complement dependent).

<a id='question-044'></a>

### Question 044: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 44 (elaborately)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying acute graft rejection, specifically T-cell mediated rejection (cellular rejection) involving direct allorecognition of MHC molecules on graft cells by recipient T cells, and the role of recipient antibodies against donor HLA antigens in hyperacute rejection. (理解移植排斥反应的免疫机制，特别是细胞介导的急性排斥反应，涉及受体T细胞直接识别移植组织细胞上的MHC分子，以及受体抗体对抗体介导的超急性排斥反应。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with HLA-A*02:01/B*07:02 and HLA-DR*03:01 alleles. The recipient's pre-transplant serum contains antibodies against HLA-A*02:01 and HLA-DR*03:01. The patient is started on standard immunosuppression consisting of tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three days post-transplant, the patient develops acute onset of fever, flank pain, oliguria, and hypertension. Urinalysis shows hematuria and proteinuria. A renal biopsy reveals diffuse fibrinoid necrosis of glomerular capillaries, endothelial swelling, and neutrophil infiltration. Immunofluorescence microscopy shows strong linear deposition of C4d along the glomerular basement membrane and peritubular capillaries. Serum creatinine rises rapidly from a baseline of 2.5 mg/dL to 6.0 mg/dL within 24 hours.

Which of the following immunological mechanisms is most likely responsible for this patient's acute kidney graft failure?

(A) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by recipient NK cells against donor HLA-DR molecules.
(B) T-cell mediated rejection involving direct allorecognition of donor MHC class I molecules by recipient CD8+ T cells.
(C) Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) mediated by pre-formed recipient antibodies binding to donor HLA class I and class II antigens.
(D) Antibody-mediated rejection involving Fc receptor-mediated inflammation triggered by recipient IgG antibodies binding to donor HLA class I antigens.
(E) Chronic antibody-mediated rejection characterized by transplant glomerulopathy and C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries only.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient.
*   **HLA Mismatch:** Donor HLA-A*02:01/B*07:02/DR*03:01; Recipient has pre-formed antibodies against HLA-A*02:01 and HLA-DR*03:01. This indicates pre-existing sensitization, likely from prior blood transfusions, pregnancy, or previous transplants.
*   **Immunosuppression:** Standard regimen (tacrolimus, mycophenolate, prednisone).
*   **Clinical Presentation:** Acute onset (3 days post-transplant) of fever, flank pain, oliguria, hypertension, hematuria, proteinuria, rapidly rising creatinine (2.5 -> 6.0 mg/dL). This is a classic presentation of acute graft failure.
*   **Biopsy Findings:** Fibrinoid necrosis of glomerular capillaries, endothelial swelling, neutrophil infiltration. These are characteristic features of hyperacute or acute antibody-mediated rejection.
*   **Immunofluorescence:** Strong linear deposition of C4d along glomerular basement membrane (GBM) and peritubular capillaries. C4d is a stable breakdown product of the complement C4 complex and is a marker of complement activation. Its deposition indicates antibody-mediated complement activation.
*   **Timing:** The acute onset within days of transplantation, coupled with pre-formed antibodies and biopsy findings, strongly points towards hyperacute or acute antibody-mediated rejection.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **Hyperacute Rejection:** Occurs within minutes to hours post-transplant. Caused by pre-formed recipient antibodies (usually IgG) against donor ABO blood group antigens or HLA antigens. Antibodies bind to endothelial cells of the graft vasculature, activating complement (classical pathway) and leading to endothelial damage, thrombosis, ischemia, and rapid graft failure. Characterized by diffuse thrombosis, fibrinoid necrosis, and neutrophil infiltration. C4d deposition is typically seen.
*   **Acute Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** Occurs days to weeks post-transplant. Caused by de novo donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) or pre-formed DSAs. Similar mechanisms to hyperacute rejection (complement activation, endothelial injury, inflammation). Biopsy shows similar features (glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis, C4d deposition).
*   **Chronic Antibody-Mediated Rejection:** Occurs months to years post-transplant. Characterized by transplant glomerulopathy (double contours of GBM), peritubular capillary basement membrane thickening, interstitial fibrosis, and tubular atrophy. C4d deposition is often present but may be patchy.
*   **T-Cell Mediated Rejection (Cellular Rejection):** Can be acute or chronic. Acute occurs days to weeks post-transplant. Involves direct allorecognition (recipient T cells recognize intact donor MHC molecules on graft cells) or indirect allorecognition (recipient APCs process donor MHC molecules and present them to T cells). CD8+ T cells directly kill graft cells expressing foreign MHC class I. CD4+ T cells orchestrate the response by activating macrophages and B cells. Biopsy shows interstitial inflammation (lymphocytes, macrophages, eosinophils), tubular injury, and sometimes endothelialitis. C4d deposition is typically absent.
*   **Mechanism of Complement-Dependent Cytotoxicity (CDC):** Antibodies (IgG or IgM) bind to target cells (e.g., endothelial cells). The C1 complex binds to the Fc portion of the antibody. This activates the classical complement pathway, leading to the formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC, C5b-9), which inserts into the cell membrane, causing lysis.
*   **Mechanism of Antibody-Dependent Cell-Mediated Cytotoxicity (ADCC):** Antibodies bind to target cells. NK cells or other cytotoxic cells (e.g., macrophages, neutrophils) express Fc receptors (e.g., CD16 on NK cells). Binding of the Fc portion of the antibody to the Fc receptor on the effector cell triggers the release of cytotoxic granules (perforin, granzymes) or reactive oxygen species, leading to target cell death.
*   **Mechanism of Fc Receptor-Mediated Inflammation:** Antibodies bind to target cells. Phagocytes (macrophages, neutrophils) bind to the Fc portion of the antibody via Fc receptors (e.g., FcγR). This triggers phagocytosis, release of inflammatory mediators, and tissue damage.

In this case, the patient has pre-formed antibodies against donor HLA antigens, acute onset graft failure within days, biopsy findings of fibrinoid necrosis and endothelial swelling, and strong C4d deposition. This constellation strongly indicates acute antibody-mediated rejection, specifically driven by complement activation (CDC) due to pre-formed DSAs.

##### 3. Linking the Vignette to the Correct Answer (C)
The patient has pre-formed antibodies against donor HLA class I (A*02:01) and class II (DR*03:01) antigens. These antibodies bind to the donor endothelial cells in the transplanted kidney. The binding of IgG antibodies activates the classical complement pathway (C1q binding to Fc region). This leads to the formation of C3 convertase and C5 convertase, ultimately generating the MAC (C5b-9). The MAC inserts into the endothelial cell membranes, causing lysis and endothelial damage, leading to fibrinoid necrosis, thrombosis, and rapid graft failure. The deposition of C4d confirms complement activation. Therefore, complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) mediated by pre-formed recipient antibodies is the most likely mechanism.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by recipient NK cells against donor HLA-DR molecules. While ADCC can contribute to AMR, the primary mechanism in hyperacute/acute AMR with pre-formed antibodies and strong C4d deposition is typically CDC. ADCC involves NK cells recognizing antibody-coated cells via Fc receptors (CD16), leading to cytotoxicity. This is less likely to be the *primary* mechanism given the strong evidence for complement activation (C4d deposition, fibrinoid necrosis).
- (B): T-cell mediated rejection involving direct allorecognition of donor MHC class I molecules by recipient CD8+ T cells. This is a form of cellular rejection. It typically occurs later (days to weeks) than hyperacute rejection and does not involve pre-formed antibodies or significant C4d deposition. Biopsy findings would primarily show interstitial inflammation and tubular injury, not fibrinoid necrosis.
- (D): Antibody-mediated rejection involving Fc receptor-mediated inflammation triggered by recipient IgG antibodies binding to donor HLA class I antigens. Fc receptor-mediated inflammation (e.g., via FcγR on macrophages/neutrophils) is another mechanism contributing to AMR, often alongside CDC and ADCC. However, the prominent fibrinoid necrosis and strong C4d deposition are hallmarks of complement activation (CDC) being the dominant pathway in this acute, severe presentation with pre-formed antibodies. While Fc receptor involvement might occur, CDC is the most direct explanation for the observed pathology.
- (E): Chronic antibody-mediated rejection characterized by transplant glomerulopathy and C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries only. This occurs much later (months to years) and has distinct histological features (transplant glomerulopathy, chronic interstitial fibrosis). While C4d deposition can be seen, it's often patchy and associated with chronic changes, not the acute fibrinoid necrosis seen here. The vignette describes an acute process occurring within days.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Hyperacute/Acute AMR (Correct Answer - C) | Acute Cellular Rejection (B) | ADCC (A)                     | Fc Receptor Inflammation (D) | Chronic AMR (E)                 |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------------------------- | :--------------------------- | :--------------------------- | :--------------------------- | :------------------------------ |
| **Timing**              | Minutes to Days                          | Days to Weeks                | Days to Weeks                | Days to Weeks                | Months to Years                 |
| **Cause**               | Pre-formed/De Novo DSAs                  | T-cell Allorecognition       | DSAs + NK cells              | DSAs + Phagocytes            | DSAs                           |
| **Antibodies**          | Yes (Pre-formed or De Novo)               | No                           | Yes                          | Yes                          | Yes                             |
| **T-cells**             | No (Primary Mechanism)                    | Yes                          | No (Primary Mechanism)        | No (Primary Mechanism)        | No (Primary Mechanism)          |
| **Complement Activation** | Yes (Strong C4d)                         | No                           | Variable                     | Variable                     | Yes (Often Patchy)              |
| **Key Biopsy Findings** | Fibrinoid Necrosis, Endothelialitis, C4d+ | Interstitial Inflammation, Tubulitis | Variable                     | Variable                     | Transplant Glomerulopathy, Fibrosis |
| **Primary Mechanism**   | CDC                                       | Direct/Indirect T-cell Killing | NK cell cytotoxicity         | Phagocyte Activation         | Chronic Inflammation/Fibrosis   |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute graft failure within days of transplantation, especially in a patient with pre-formed antibodies against donor HLA antigens, accompanied by biopsy findings of fibrinoid necrosis and strong C4d deposition, is characteristic of hyperacute or acute antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) driven primarily by complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC). Differentiate this from acute cellular rejection (T-cell mediated, later onset, interstitial inflammation, no C4d) and chronic AMR (later onset, transplant glomerulopathy, fibrosis). Understand the roles of CDC, ADCC, and Fc receptor-mediated inflammation in AMR.

<a id='question-045'></a>

### Question 45: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 45 (evolved)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of T-cell activation, costimulatory signals (B7-CD28), and the mechanism of action of immunosuppressive drugs like cyclosporine in preventing graft rejection. (理解T细胞活化、共刺激信号（B7-CD28）以及环孢素等免疫抑制剂在预防移植物排斥中的作用机制。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor. His pre-transplant blood type is A+, and the donor's is O+. He is started on a standard immunosuppressive regimen including cyclosporine, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, and decreased urine output. A renal biopsy reveals acute tubular necrosis and interstitial inflammation with T-cell infiltrates. Serum creatinine rises from a baseline of 1.8 mg/dL to 4.5 mg/dL. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes shows a normal CD4+ T-cell count but a significantly decreased percentage of activated T cells (CD69+).

Which of the following mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for the observed acute rejection episode despite the patient being on immunosuppressive therapy?

(A) Antibody-mediated rejection due to pre-formed anti-donor HLA antibodies.
(B) Failure of cyclosporine to inhibit calcineurin, leading to continued IL-2 production and T-cell activation.
(C) Development of resistance to mycophenolate mofetil, allowing proliferation of B cells and antibody production.
(D) Inadequate suppression of costimulatory signals (B7-CD28 interaction) by the current immunosuppressive regimen.
(E) Prednisone-induced immunosuppression being insufficient to prevent the activation of NK cells against the graft.

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, ESRD (diabetic nephropathy), kidney transplant recipient (deceased donor). This sets the stage for potential graft rejection.
- **Blood Type Mismatch:** Recipient A+, Donor O+. This is an ABO-compatible transplant (O is the universal donor), so pre-formed anti-A antibodies are not the primary concern here, although they can still play a role in some cases.
- **Immunosuppression:** Cyclosporine, mycophenolate mofetil, prednisone. This is a standard triple therapy regimen designed to prevent rejection.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, rising creatinine (1.8 -> 4.5 mg/dL) three weeks post-transplant. These are classic signs and symptoms of acute graft rejection.
- **Renal Biopsy:** Acute tubular necrosis, interstitial inflammation with T-cell infiltrates. This confirms acute cellular rejection.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Normal CD4+ count, decreased activated T cells (CD69+). This suggests that the immunosuppression is partially effective in preventing full T-cell activation, but not completely. The rejection is occurring despite immunosuppression.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism responsible for the acute rejection *despite* immunosuppression.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **T-cell Activation:** T-cell activation requires two signals:
    1.  **Signal 1:** T-cell receptor (TCR) binding to peptide-MHC complex on the antigen-presenting cell (APC).
    2.  **Signal 2 (Costimulation):** Interaction between CD28 on the T cell and B7 molecules (CD80/CD86) on the APC.
- **Cyclosporine Mechanism:** Cyclosporine inhibits calcineurin, a phosphatase required for the activation of NFAT (Nuclear Factor of Activated T-cells). NFAT is crucial for the transcription of IL-2 and other cytokines necessary for T-cell proliferation and differentiation. By inhibiting IL-2 production, cyclosporine primarily blocks T-cell proliferation (Signal 1 is still delivered, but the consequence is blunted).
- **Mycophenolate Mofetil Mechanism:** Inhibits inosine monophosphate dehydrogenase (IMPDH), an enzyme crucial for *de novo* purine synthesis, primarily affecting rapidly proliferating lymphocytes (both T and B cells).
- **Prednisone Mechanism:** A corticosteroid that has broad immunosuppressive effects, including inhibiting cytokine production (like IL-2, TNF-α, IFN-γ) and reducing lymphocyte proliferation and migration.
- **Acute Cellular Rejection:** Primarily mediated by recipient T cells recognizing donor MHC molecules (alloantigens) on the graft. This involves both Signal 1 (TCR-MHC) and Signal 2 (CD28-B7).
- **Focus 45 (evolved):** This section of Kaplan emphasizes the importance of costimulation (B7-CD28) in T-cell activation and how blocking this pathway can prevent rejection. While cyclosporine blocks IL-2 production (downstream of Signal 1), it does not directly inhibit the initial T-cell activation signals or the costimulatory signal.
- **The Problem:** The patient is experiencing acute cellular rejection despite being on cyclosporine, mycophenolate, and prednisone. The decreased CD69+ cells suggest some suppression is occurring, but it's insufficient. This implies that the T-cells are still receiving sufficient signals to initiate the rejection process. Since cyclosporine primarily targets IL-2 production (downstream of initial activation), the initial activation signals, including costimulation (B7-CD28), might not be adequately blocked. Blocking costimulation (e.g., with belatacept, which binds CD80/CD86) provides an additional layer of immunosuppression. The standard triple therapy might not always completely block the costimulatory signal, allowing for subclinical or even overt rejection in some cases.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) is characterized by donor-specific antibodies (DSA) binding to the graft endothelium, activating complement and recruiting inflammatory cells. While ABO incompatibility can lead to pre-formed antibodies (anti-A in an O donor recipient), this patient is A+ receiving from O+ (ABO compatible). AMR typically occurs later than 3 weeks, although hyperacute rejection can occur immediately with pre-formed DSAs. The biopsy findings (T-cell infiltrates) are more characteristic of cellular rejection. The vignette doesn't provide evidence for DSAs or complement activation.
- (B): Cyclosporine *does* inhibit calcineurin, which *does* lead to decreased IL-2 production and T-cell activation. If cyclosporine were failing to inhibit calcineurin, we would expect *more* T-cell activation and rejection, not necessarily the observed partial suppression (decreased CD69+ cells). The question asks why rejection is occurring *despite* therapy, implying the drug is working to some extent, but perhaps not completely blocking all necessary signals for rejection. Failure of cyclosporine would lead to *uninhibited* T-cell activation.
- (C): Mycophenolate mofetil primarily targets lymphocyte proliferation (both T and B cells). While resistance can occur, it's less common than issues with costimulation. Furthermore, the primary pathology described is T-cell mediated (cellular rejection), not antibody-mediated (which would involve B cells). While B cells can contribute to rejection, the core issue here is T-cell activation.
- (E): Prednisone is a broad immunosuppressant, but its primary mechanism isn't specifically targeting NK cells. While NK cells can play a role in rejection, especially in the absence of MHC matching, the biopsy clearly shows T-cell infiltrates, pointing to a T-cell mediated process. Prednisone's effect on T-cells is significant, but the question focuses on the specific signals required for T-cell activation that might be inadequately blocked.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Correct Answer (D) - Inadequate Costimulation Blockade | Option (A) - Antibody-Mediated Rejection | Option (B) - Cyclosporine Failure | Option (C) - Mycophenolate Resistance | Option (E) - Prednisone & NK Cells |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------ | :-------------------------------------- | :------------------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :-------------------------------- |
| **Primary Mechanism**   | Insufficient blocking of B7-CD28 interaction           | DSA binding graft endothelium, complement | Cyclosporine not inhibiting calcineurin | Mycophenolate not inhibiting IMPDH | Prednisone insufficient for NK cell suppression |
| **Cell Type**           | T cells                                                 | Antibodies, complement, inflammatory cells | T cells                          | B cells (primarily), T cells        | NK cells                          |
| **Biopsy Findings**     | T-cell infiltrates                                       | Endothelialitis, C4d deposition         | T-cell infiltrates              | Variable, often T-cell infiltrates  | Variable                          |
| **Timing**              | Weeks to months                                          | Can be hyperacute, acute, or chronic    | Acute                           | Variable                             | Variable                          |
| **Relevance to Vignette** | Explains rejection despite standard triple therapy      | ABO compatible, biopsy shows T cells     | Cyclosporine is working partially | Mycophenolate is working partially  | Biopsy shows T cells, not NK cells |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute graft rejection requires T-cell activation, which involves both Signal 1 (TCR-MHC) and Signal 2 (CD28-B7 costimulation). Standard immunosuppression (like cyclosporine) primarily targets downstream events (IL-2 production) or lymphocyte proliferation, but may not completely block the initial costimulatory signal. This incomplete blockade can lead to rejection despite therapy. Differential diagnoses include antibody-mediated rejection, drug failure/resistance, or other immune cell involvement, but the vignette points towards incomplete T-cell suppression via costimulation.

<a id='question-046'></a>

### Question 046: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 46 (recognize)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying graft rejection, specifically the roles of T cells (CD4+ helper T cells and CD8+ cytotoxic T cells) and antibodies in recognizing foreign MHC molecules and initiating rejection pathways. (理解移植排斥的免疫机制，特别是T细胞（CD4+辅助T细胞和CD8+细胞毒性T细胞）和抗体在识别外源MHC分子并启动排斥途径中的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor who was ABO-compatible but HLA-mismatched. The patient is started on standard immunosuppressive therapy including a calcineurin inhibitor (cyclosporine), an antimetabolite (mycophenolate mofetil), and corticosteroids (prednisone). Three weeks post-transplant, the patient develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals acute tubular necrosis and interstitial inflammation with infiltration of mononuclear cells. Immunofluorescence staining shows deposition of C4d in the peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the peripheral blood shows a significant increase in CD4+ T cells expressing CD25 (IL-2 receptor alpha chain) and CD69 (an early T cell activation marker). Serum analysis reveals elevated levels of donor-specific antibodies (DSA) against HLA class I antigens.

Which of the following immunological mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for this patient's acute graft rejection?

(A) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells recognizing antibody-coated graft endothelial cells.
(B) Direct allorecognition by recipient CD8+ T cells recognizing intact donor MHC class I molecules on graft antigen-presenting cells (APCs).
(C) Indirect allorecognition by recipient CD4+ T cells recognizing processed donor MHC class II peptides presented by recipient APCs.
(D) Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) mediated by donor-specific antibodies binding to donor MHC class I molecules on graft endothelial cells, leading to membrane attack complex (MAC) formation.
(E) T-cell independent antibody-mediated rejection involving IgM antibodies binding to carbohydrate antigens on the graft endothelium.

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant. This sets the stage for transplantation immunology.
*   **Graft Type:** Deceased donor kidney, ABO-compatible but HLA-mismatched. HLA mismatch is a major driver of allorecognition and rejection.
*   **Immunosuppression:** Standard triple therapy (calcineurin inhibitor, antimetabolite, steroids). Despite immunosuppression, rejection can still occur.
*   **Clinical Presentation:** 3 weeks post-transplant, fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated creatinine. These are classic signs of acute graft rejection.
*   **Biopsy Findings:** Acute tubular necrosis, interstitial inflammation with mononuclear cells. This indicates cellular infiltration and tissue damage.
*   **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. C4d is a stable breakdown product of the classical complement pathway, indicating complement activation, often associated with antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), but can also be seen in T-cell mediated rejection (TCMR) with antibody involvement.
*   **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD4+ T cells with CD25 and CD69 expression. CD69 is an early activation marker, and CD25 (IL-2Rα) is upregulated on activated T cells, suggesting T cell activation.
*   **Serology:** Elevated donor-specific antibodies (DSA) against HLA class I antigens. This confirms the presence of antibodies targeting the graft.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely immunological mechanism. The combination of HLA mismatch, clinical signs of rejection, biopsy findings, T cell activation markers, and DSAs points towards a complex rejection process involving both cellular and humoral immunity.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **Transplantation Immunology Basics:** Graft rejection is an immune response against foreign MHC molecules (alloantigens) on the transplanted organ. The immune system recognizes these as non-self.
*   **Types of Rejection:**
    *   **Hyperacute Rejection:** Occurs within minutes to hours, mediated by pre-formed antibodies (e.g., ABO incompatibility). Characterized by endothelial damage, thrombosis, and graft necrosis. Not relevant here as the patient is ABO-compatible.
    *   **Acute Rejection:** Occurs days to weeks post-transplant. Can be T-cell mediated (TCMR) or antibody-mediated (AMR), or a combination.
        *   **TCMR:** Primarily mediated by T cells.
            *   **Direct Pathway:** Recipient T cells (CD4+ and CD8+) recognize intact foreign MHC molecules (donor MHC class I and II) presented directly on donor APCs (e.g., dendritic cells) within the graft. This requires MHC matching for CD8+ T cells (recognizing MHC I) and is less dependent on matching for CD4+ T cells (recognizing MHC II).
            *   **Indirect Pathway:** Recipient APCs process donor MHC molecules (taken up from the graft or circulating donor cells) and present peptides derived from these donor MHC molecules on their own MHC class II molecules to recipient CD4+ T cells. This pathway is similar to how the immune system responds to foreign proteins from pathogens.
        *   **AMR:** Mediated by antibodies (DSAs) against donor HLA antigens. Antibodies can activate complement (CDC) or recruit effector cells (ADCC, NK cells). C4d deposition is a hallmark of complement activation in AMR.
    *   **Chronic Rejection:** Occurs months to years post-transplant. Characterized by fibrosis and gradual loss of graft function. Involves both cellular and humoral immunity.
*   **Specifics of the Case:**
    *   The patient has HLA mismatch, providing alloantigens for immune recognition.
    *   The presence of DSAs against HLA class I indicates an antibody response.
    *   C4d deposition suggests complement activation, often associated with AMR.
    *   Increased activated T cells (CD25+, CD69+) indicate a cellular immune response.
    *   The combination of DSAs, C4d deposition, and T cell activation points towards a mixed acute rejection episode, likely involving both AMR and TCMR components.
*   **Evaluating the Options:**
    *   **(A) ADCC:** While DSAs can mediate ADCC, the primary mechanism of *acute* rejection involving DSAs and HLA class I mismatch often involves direct T cell recognition or complement activation. ADCC is a significant mechanism in AMR, but direct T cell recognition is a fundamental pathway in acute cellular rejection.
    *   **(B) Direct allorecognition by recipient CD8+ T cells:** This is a key mechanism in acute TCMR. CD8+ T cells directly recognize donor MHC class I molecules on donor APCs within the graft, leading to cytotoxic killing of graft cells. This pathway is particularly important for HLA class I mismatches, which are present in this patient and targeted by DSAs. The presence of activated T cells supports this.
    *   **(C) Indirect allorecognition by recipient CD4+ T cells:** This pathway is also important in TCMR, especially for HLA class II mismatches. While CD4+ T cells are activated (CD25+, CD69+), the primary target of the DSAs is HLA class I, making direct recognition by CD8+ T cells a very likely contributor, especially given the clinical picture of acute rejection.
    *   **(D) CDC:** This is a major mechanism of AMR, where DSAs bind to donor MHC class I (or II) and activate the classical complement pathway, leading to MAC formation and endothelial damage. The C4d deposition strongly supports complement activation. However, the question asks for the *most likely* mechanism, and the vignette highlights both DSAs and T cell activation. While CDC is involved, direct T cell recognition is a fundamental pathway for acute cellular rejection triggered by HLA mismatch.
    *   **(E) T-cell independent antibody-mediated rejection:** This is incorrect. Antibody production against graft antigens is typically T-cell dependent, requiring T cell help for B cell activation and antibody class switching. Also, the primary targets in acute rejection are usually MHC molecules, not simple carbohydrate antigens.

*   **Conclusion:** The vignette presents a complex picture of acute rejection with both cellular (activated T cells) and humoral (DSAs, C4d) components. Both direct T cell recognition (B) and complement-dependent cytotoxicity (D) are plausible mechanisms involving the HLA class I mismatch targeted by DSAs. However, direct allorecognition by CD8+ T cells is a fundamental pathway for acute cellular rejection driven by MHC class I mismatch and is strongly supported by the presence of activated T cells. While CDC (D) is also likely occurring due to DSAs and C4d, direct T cell recognition (B) represents the primary cellular immune response to the foreign MHC molecules. In the context of acute rejection driven by HLA mismatch, direct T cell recognition is considered a central mechanism.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) is a mechanism of AMR where NK cells or other effector cells bind to antibody-coated target cells via Fc receptors, leading to target cell lysis. While DSAs can mediate ADCC, it's often considered secondary to CDC or direct T cell killing in acute rejection. The vignette emphasizes T cell activation alongside DSAs, making direct T cell recognition (B) or CDC (D) more central mechanisms. ADCC is plausible but perhaps less likely to be the *most* prominent mechanism compared to direct T cell cytotoxicity against MHC I expressing cells.
- (B): Direct allorecognition by recipient CD8+ T cells recognizing intact donor MHC class I molecules on graft APCs is a classic mechanism of acute cellular rejection, especially relevant given the HLA class I mismatch and the presence of activated T cells. CD8+ T cells are potent killers of cells presenting foreign MHC I. This pathway directly explains the cellular infiltration and graft damage.
- (C): Indirect allorecognition involves recipient APCs presenting donor MHC peptides on MHC II to recipient CD4+ T cells. This pathway is important, particularly for CD4+ T cell help in the immune response, but direct recognition (B) is often considered the primary driver of acute cytotoxic rejection by CD8+ T cells against MHC I mismatches. The vignette highlights activated CD4+ T cells, but the DSAs target MHC I, making direct CD8+ T cell recognition highly relevant.
- (D): Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) is a key mechanism of AMR, mediated by DSAs activating complement on graft endothelial cells. The C4d deposition strongly supports this pathway. Given the presence of DSAs against HLA class I and C4d, CDC is definitely occurring. However, the question asks for the *most likely* mechanism, and the vignette also includes strong evidence of T cell activation (CD25+, CD69+). Direct T cell recognition (B) is a fundamental cellular mechanism driving acute rejection. Often, acute rejection involves both TCMR and AMR components. Choosing between (B) and (D) is difficult, but direct T cell recognition is a primary cellular pathway.
- (E): T-cell independent antibody-mediated rejection is incorrect. Antibody responses against complex antigens like MHC molecules are typically T-cell dependent. Furthermore, the primary targets in acute rejection are MHC molecules, not typically simple carbohydrate antigens targeted in T-independent responses.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Direct Allorecognition (B)                                  | Complement-Dependent Cytotoxicity (D)                           | Antibody-Dependent Cell-Mediated Cytotoxicity (A)                 | Indirect Allorecognition (C)                                     | T-cell Independent Ab Rejection (E)                           |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Mediator**            | CD8+ T cells                                                | Antibodies (DSAs) + Complement                                  | Antibodies (DSAs) + NK cells/other effector cells                | CD4+ T cells                                                    | Antibodies (IgM)                                                |
| **Target**              | Intact Donor MHC Class I on APCs                             | Donor MHC Class I/II on endothelial cells                        | Antibody-coated graft cells (endothelial, etc.)                   | Donor MHC peptides on Recipient MHC Class II on APCs              | Carbohydrate antigens on graft endothelium                      |
| **Mechanism**           | T cell receptor recognizes MHC I directly                     | Antibody binds MHC, activates complement cascade (MAC)           | Antibody binds target cell, effector cell binds Ab via Fc receptor | T cell receptor recognizes peptide-MHC II complex                | Antibody binds antigen, activates complement or phagocytosis     |
| **Key Findings**        | Activated CD8+ T cells, HLA Class I mismatch                 | DSAs, C4d deposition                                            | DSAs, NK cell infiltration                                        | Activated CD4+ T cells, HLA Class II mismatch                   | IgM antibodies, no T cell involvement                            |
| **Relevance to Vignette** | HLA Class I mismatch, activated T cells (CD8+)              | HLA Class I mismatch, DSAs, C4d deposition                       | HLA Class I mismatch, DSAs                                        | HLA Class II mismatch, activated CD4+ T cells                    | Not consistent with MHC targeting or T cell activation          |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute graft rejection involves both T-cell mediated (TCMR) and antibody-mediated (AMR) mechanisms. TCMR involves direct recognition of intact donor MHC molecules by recipient T cells (CD8+ for MHC I, CD4+ for MHC II) or indirect recognition of processed donor MHC peptides presented by recipient APCs. AMR involves DSAs activating complement (CDC) or recruiting effector cells (ADCC). The presence of HLA mismatch, DSAs, C4d deposition, and activated T cells often indicates a mixed rejection episode. Direct T cell recognition is a fundamental pathway for acute cellular rejection driven by MHC mismatches. Key differentials include hyperacute rejection (pre-formed antibodies, immediate), chronic rejection (fibrosis, late), and rejection due to infection.

<a id='question-047'></a>

### Question 047: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 47 (differences)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological basis of transplant rejection, specifically the role of differences in MHC molecules (HLA in humans) between donor and recipient in triggering T-cell mediated rejection. (理解移植排斥的免疫学基础，特别是供体和受体之间MHC分子差异如何触发T细胞介导的排斥反应。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor. The donor is ABO blood type A and HLA-A*02:01/B*07:02/DRB1*15:01 positive. The recipient is ABO blood type A and HLA-A*02:01/B*08:01/DRB1*15:01 positive. The transplant procedure is uneventful. Three weeks post-transplant, the patient develops fever, flank pain, elevated serum creatinine (from baseline 1.2 mg/dL to 4.5 mg/dL), and proteinuria. A renal biopsy reveals acute tubular necrosis and interstitial inflammation with infiltration of T lymphocytes. Immunofluorescence staining shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. The patient's serum contains antibodies against donor HLA-B*07:02.

Which of the following immunological mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for this patient's acute kidney transplant rejection?

(A) Antibody-mediated rejection due to pre-formed anti-HLA antibodies binding to donor endothelial cells, activating complement and causing endothelial injury.
(B) Antibody-mediated rejection due to de novo anti-HLA antibodies binding to donor endothelial cells, activating complement and causing endothelial injury.
(C) T-cell mediated rejection due to direct allorecognition of donor MHC class I molecules on recipient antigen-presenting cells (APCs).
(D) T-cell mediated rejection due to indirect allorecognition of donor MHC class II molecules presented by recipient APCs.
(E) T-cell mediated rejection due to direct allorecognition of donor MHC class II molecules on donor APCs migrating to recipient lymphoid organs.

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, ESRD due to diabetic nephropathy, receives kidney transplant.
*   **HLA Mismatch:** Donor: A, HLA-A*02:01/B*07:02/DRB1*15:01. Recipient: A, HLA-A*02:01/B*08:01/DRB1*15:01. The key mismatch is HLA-B*07:02 (donor) vs. HLA-B*08:01 (recipient).
*   **Clinical Presentation:** 3 weeks post-transplant, fever, flank pain, elevated creatinine, proteinuria – classic signs of acute rejection.
*   **Biopsy Findings:** Acute tubular necrosis, interstitial inflammation with T lymphocytes, C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries – indicative of acute rejection, specifically involving both cellular and humoral components.
*   **Serology:** Serum contains antibodies against donor HLA-B*07:02. This is the crucial piece of information.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely immunological mechanism.

The presence of pre-existing antibodies against a donor HLA antigen (HLA-B*07:02 in this case) is the hallmark of antibody-mediated rejection (AMR). The C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries further supports AMR, as C4d is a stable breakdown product of the classical complement pathway, which is activated by antibody binding to HLA antigens. The timing (3 weeks) is consistent with AMR, which can occur hyperacutely (minutes to hours, due to pre-formed antibodies), acutely (days to weeks), or chronically. The clinical signs (fever, flank pain, rising creatinine) and biopsy findings (tubular necrosis, inflammation, C4d) are all consistent with AMR.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **Transplant Rejection Types:** Rejection can be broadly classified as T-cell mediated (cellular) or antibody-mediated (humoral).
*   **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** Occurs when recipient antibodies bind to donor HLA antigens expressed on endothelial cells of the graft vasculature. This binding activates the classical complement pathway (leading to C4d deposition) and recruits inflammatory cells (neutrophils, macrophages), causing endothelial injury, microvascular inflammation (MVI), thrombosis, and ultimately graft dysfunction.
    *   **Mechanism:** Recipient antibodies (pre-formed or de novo) recognize donor HLA antigens (usually Class I, but can be Class II).
    *   **Consequences:** Complement activation (C3/C4 consumption, C4d deposition), endothelial cell activation, inflammation, thrombosis, graft injury.
    *   **Timing:** Can be hyperacute (pre-formed antibodies, minutes/hours), acute (days/weeks), or chronic (months/years).
*   **T-cell Mediated Rejection (TCMR):** Occurs when recipient T cells recognize donor HLA antigens presented by donor APCs or recipient APCs presenting donor antigens.
    *   **Direct Pathway:** Recipient T cells directly recognize intact donor MHC molecules (Class I and II) on donor APCs (e.g., dendritic cells) that have migrated to recipient lymphoid organs or are present in the graft. This is a potent form of rejection.
    *   **Indirect Pathway:** Recipient APCs process donor MHC molecules (from shed graft cells or donor APCs) and present peptides derived from these molecules on their own MHC Class II molecules to recipient T cells. This is generally considered less potent than the direct pathway.
*   **Kaplan Preclinical Immunology and Microbiology Review 2023:** Details the mechanisms of transplant rejection, emphasizing the role of HLA mismatches and the distinction between AMR and TCMR. It highlights the importance of C4d deposition as a marker for AMR.
*   **First Aid 2025:** Summarizes transplant immunology, including the roles of HLA matching, T-cell and antibody-mediated rejection pathways, and immunosuppressive drugs.

In this case, the patient has *pre-existing* antibodies against donor HLA-B*07:02. This is indicated by the finding of these antibodies in the serum *after* the transplant but *before* the onset of rejection symptoms, suggesting they were present beforehand (or developed very rapidly, but the question implies they were present). The presence of C4d deposition confirms complement activation, a key feature of AMR. Therefore, the mechanism is antibody-mediated rejection due to pre-formed antibodies.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Antibody-mediated rejection due to pre-formed anti-HLA antibodies binding to donor endothelial cells, activating complement and causing endothelial injury.** This option correctly identifies AMR and the role of pre-formed antibodies, complement activation, and endothelial injury. However, the vignette states the antibodies are present *after* the transplant, but the clinical presentation suggests they were likely present *before* or developed very early. While technically possible they developed rapidly post-transplant, the most common scenario for AMR with pre-existing antibodies is hyperacute rejection (minutes to hours), which doesn't fit the 3-week timeframe. Option (B) is more precise because it acknowledges the *de novo* development of antibodies, which is also a common cause of acute AMR, fitting the 3-week timeframe better than hyperacute rejection caused by pre-formed antibodies. The vignette doesn't explicitly state the antibodies were pre-formed *before* the transplant, only that they are present *now* against the donor HLA. Therefore, while (A) describes a mechanism of AMR, (B) better fits the clinical context of acute rejection occurring weeks post-transplant, potentially due to antibodies developing shortly after transplantation or being present at low levels before transplantation and boosting after. *Correction:* Re-reading the vignette, the presence of antibodies *at 3 weeks* strongly suggests they were either pre-formed or developed very rapidly post-transplant. The question asks for the *most likely* mechanism. Given the 3-week timeframe and the presence of antibodies against a specific donor HLA, AMR is the correct category. The distinction between pre-formed and de novo is important, but both lead to AMR. Option (A) describes AMR due to pre-formed antibodies, which can cause hyperacute or acute rejection. Option (B) describes AMR due to de novo antibodies, typically causing acute rejection. The vignette doesn't definitively state if the antibodies were pre-formed or de novo. However, the presence of C4d deposition strongly points towards antibody-mediated injury. Let's re-evaluate the options based on the most common causes of rejection at 3 weeks. Acute AMR often involves de novo antibodies or boosting of pre-existing antibodies. Hyperacute rejection (pre-formed antibodies) is usually much faster. Therefore, (B) might be slightly more likely than (A) in this specific 3-week timeframe, although (A) is also plausible if the antibodies were pre-formed but didn't cause immediate hyperacute rejection. Let's reconsider the wording. The vignette says "The patient's serum contains antibodies against donor HLA-B*07:02." It doesn't say "pre-formed antibodies". This phrasing leans towards antibodies developing *after* the transplant, fitting the definition of *de novo* antibodies, although they could have been present at very low levels before. Given the 3-week timeframe, acute AMR due to de novo antibodies (B) is a very common scenario.
- (B): **Antibody-mediated rejection due to de novo anti-HLA antibodies binding to donor endothelial cells, activating complement and causing endothelial injury.** This option describes AMR caused by antibodies that develop *after* the transplant. This fits the clinical presentation of acute rejection occurring 3 weeks post-transplant and is a common cause of AMR in this timeframe. The presence of C4d deposition strongly supports this mechanism. This is the most likely mechanism given the information.
- (C): **T-cell mediated rejection due to direct allorecognition of donor MHC class I molecules on recipient antigen-presenting cells (APCs).** This describes a mechanism of TCMR. While T cells are present in the biopsy, the presence of donor-specific antibodies and C4d deposition points strongly towards AMR as the primary mechanism. TCMR typically doesn't involve donor-specific antibodies or C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries.
- (D): **T-cell mediated rejection due to indirect allorecognition of donor MHC class II molecules presented by recipient APCs.** This is another mechanism of TCMR. Similar to (C), it doesn't explain the presence of donor-specific antibodies or C4d deposition.
- (E): **T-cell mediated rejection due to direct allorecognition of donor MHC class II molecules on donor APCs migrating to recipient lymphoid organs.** This is also a mechanism of TCMR. Again, it doesn't fit the clinical picture dominated by antibody-mediated injury (presence of DSA and C4d).

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR)                                  | T-cell Mediated Rejection (TCMR)                                     |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Mediators**   | Antibodies (DSA)                                                    | T lymphocytes (CD8+ cytotoxic T cells, CD4+ helper T cells)          |
| **Target Antigens**     | Donor HLA Class I and/or Class II molecules on graft endothelium/cells | Donor HLA Class I and Class II molecules presented by APCs          |
| **Key Pathological Features** | Endothelial injury, microvascular inflammation (MVI), C4d deposition, thrombosis | Interstitial inflammation, tubular atrophy, T-cell infiltrates        |
| **Diagnostic Markers**  | Donor-specific antibodies (DSA), C4d deposition, C3/C4 consumption | Biopsy findings (inflammation pattern), sometimes flow cytometry     |
| **Timing**              | Hyperacute (minutes), Acute (days/weeks), Chronic (months/years)     | Acute (days/weeks), Chronic (months/years)                            |
| **Vignette Findings**   | DSA present, C4d deposition                                         | T-cell infiltrates present, but DSA and C4d are key findings here |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The presence of donor-specific antibodies (DSA) and C4d deposition in a kidney transplant recipient experiencing acute graft dysfunction strongly indicates antibody-mediated rejection (AMR). AMR can be caused by pre-formed antibodies (leading to hyperacute or acute rejection) or de novo antibodies (typically causing acute or chronic rejection). T-cell mediated rejection (TCMR) is characterized by T-cell infiltrates and lacks DSA and C4d deposition. Key differentials include acute TCMR, infection, drug toxicity, and thrombotic microangiopathy.
