<a id='question-048'></a>

### Question 048: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 48 (antigens)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of ABO blood group antigens as major histocompatibility complex (MHC) class I-like molecules in transplant rejection, specifically hyperacute rejection mediated by pre-formed antibodies against donor ABO antigens. (理解ABO血型抗原在移植排斥反应中的作用，特别是作为MHC I类分子，介导由预存抗体引起的超急性排斥反应。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy is scheduled for a kidney transplant. His blood type is A+. The donor kidney is from a deceased donor with blood type B+. The crossmatch test is positive for cytotoxic antibodies. The transplant surgery proceeds. Within minutes of reperfusion of the donor kidney, the patient develops severe hypotension, disseminated intravascular coagulation (DIC), and acute renal failure. Renal biopsy shows diffuse thrombosis of glomerular capillaries and peritubular capillaries, along with extensive interstitial hemorrhage and necrosis. Immunofluorescence staining reveals strong deposition of C3d along the endothelium of the graft vessels.

Which of the following immunological mechanisms is most likely responsible for this patient's immediate post-transplant complications?

(A) T-cell mediated acute cellular rejection involving donor MHC class I and class II antigens.
(B) Antibody-mediated rejection targeting donor HLA class I and class II antigens, occurring days to weeks post-transplant.
(C) Hyperacute rejection mediated by pre-formed anti-A antibodies binding to donor B blood group antigens on endothelial cells.
(D) Chronic rejection characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes occurring months to years post-transplant.
(E) Graft-versus-host disease (GVHD) where donor T cells attack the recipient's tissues.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male, ESRD (diabetic nephropathy), blood type A+. This sets the stage for a kidney transplant.
- **Donor Profile:** Deceased donor, blood type B+. This is ABO incompatible (Recipient A, Donor B).
- **Crossmatch:** Positive for cytotoxic antibodies. This indicates the presence of pre-formed antibodies in the recipient's serum that react with donor cells, strongly suggesting ABO incompatibility or sensitization to other HLA antigens.
- **Timing:** Complications occur "within minutes of reperfusion". This extremely rapid onset points towards a hyperacute rejection mechanism.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Severe hypotension, DIC, acute renal failure. These are classic signs of systemic vascular damage and thrombosis, characteristic of hyperacute rejection.
- **Pathology:** Glomerular and peritubular capillary thrombosis, interstitial hemorrhage, necrosis. This confirms vascular damage and tissue destruction within the graft.
- **Immunofluorescence:** Strong C3d deposition along graft endothelium. This indicates complement activation triggered by antibody binding to the endothelium, a hallmark of antibody-mediated rejection, particularly hyperacute rejection.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the immunological mechanism responsible.

The combination of ABO incompatibility (A recipient, B donor), a positive crossmatch, immediate onset of severe complications upon reperfusion, vascular thrombosis, and complement deposition strongly points to hyperacute rejection mediated by pre-formed antibodies against donor ABO blood group antigens. ABO antigens are potent immunogens and are expressed on endothelial cells, making them prime targets for pre-existing antibodies.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **ABO Blood Group Antigens:** These are carbohydrate antigens expressed on red blood cells and other tissues, including endothelial cells. They are structurally similar to MHC class I molecules and can bind to certain T-cell receptors, although their primary immunological role is as targets for antibodies (isohemagglutinins).
- **Hyperacute Rejection:** This is a form of antibody-mediated rejection that occurs within minutes to hours after transplantation. It is caused by pre-formed antibodies in the recipient's serum that recognize antigens on the donor organ's endothelial cells. The most common cause is ABO incompatibility, where the recipient has pre-existing anti-A or anti-B antibodies (IgM and IgG) that bind to A or B antigens on the donor endothelium.
- **Mechanism:**
    1.  **Antibody Binding:** Pre-formed recipient antibodies (e.g., anti-B in an A recipient receiving a B donor organ) bind to ABO antigens on the donor endothelial cells.
    2.  **Complement Activation:** This antibody binding activates the classical complement pathway, leading to the deposition of C3b and C3d on the endothelium.
    3.  **Endothelial Activation:** Complement activation and antibody binding trigger endothelial cell activation, leading to the release of procoagulant factors (like tissue factor) and inflammatory mediators.
    4.  **Thrombosis and Inflammation:** Widespread intravascular coagulation (DIC) occurs due to endothelial damage and procoagulant activation. Neutrophils and other inflammatory cells are recruited, causing further endothelial damage and tissue necrosis.
    5.  **Graft Failure:** The resulting thrombosis and inflammation lead to rapid graft ischemia and failure.
- **ABO Antigens as MHC Class I-like Molecules:** ABO antigens are structurally similar to MHC class I molecules and can present peptides to T cells. However, in the context of hyperacute rejection, the primary mechanism is antibody-mediated complement activation and endothelial damage, not T-cell recognition.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **T-cell mediated acute cellular rejection:** This typically occurs days to weeks post-transplant and involves T cells (CD8+ cytotoxic T cells and CD4+ helper T cells) recognizing donor MHC class I and class II antigens. It causes interstitial inflammation and tubular damage, not immediate thrombosis and DIC. The timing and pathology described are inconsistent with acute cellular rejection.
- (B): **Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) targeting HLA:** This also occurs days to weeks post-transplant (or later) and involves antibodies against donor HLA class I and class II antigens. It causes endothelial injury, C4d deposition (a marker of complement activation), and graft dysfunction. While it is antibody-mediated, the *timing* (minutes) and the specific target (ABO antigens, not HLA) make this incorrect. Hyperacute rejection is a specific, very rapid form of AMR.
- (D): **Chronic rejection:** This occurs months to years post-transplant and is characterized by gradual fibrosis, vascular changes (e.g., transplant glomerulopathy, chronic active antibody-mediated rejection), and progressive graft dysfunction. The timeline is completely wrong for the described scenario.
- (E): **Graft-versus-host disease (GVHD):** This occurs when immunocompetent donor T cells (present in bone marrow or liver transplants, rarely in solid organ transplants unless the recipient is severely immunocompromised) recognize recipient antigens as foreign and attack recipient tissues. It typically affects the skin, liver, and gastrointestinal tract. It is not a rejection of the graft *by* the recipient's immune system, and the clinical presentation is different.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Hyperacute Rejection (Correct Answer) | Acute Cellular Rejection (A) | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (B) | Chronic Rejection (D) | Graft-vs-Host Disease (E) |
| :------------------ | :------------------------------------- | :--------------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :------------------- | :------------------------ |
| **Timing**          | Minutes to hours                      | Days to weeks                | Days to weeks (or later)          | Months to years      | Weeks to months           |
| **Mechanism**       | Pre-formed Abs vs. ABO/HLA antigens    | T cells vs. HLA antigens     | Abs vs. HLA antigens               | T cells & Abs        | Donor T cells vs. Recipient |
| **Primary Target**  | ABO antigens (endothelium)             | HLA class I/II (parenchyma)  | HLA class I/II (endothelium)       | Vessels, parenchyma  | Recipient tissues         |
| **Key Pathology**   | Thrombosis, DIC, necrosis              | Interstitial inflammation   | Endothelial injury, C4d deposition | Fibrosis, vasculopathy | Skin, GI, liver rash      |
| **Complement**      | Strong C3d deposition                  | Minimal                     | C4d deposition                    | Variable             | N/A                      |
| **Crossmatch**      | Positive                               | Usually negative             | Can be positive                    | N/A                 | N/A                      |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Hyperacute rejection is a rapid, catastrophic form of transplant rejection mediated by pre-formed antibodies, most commonly against ABO blood group antigens. Recognize the key features: ABO incompatibility, positive crossmatch, immediate onset (minutes), thrombosis, DIC, and complement deposition. Differentiate it from other forms of rejection based on timing, mechanism (cellular vs. humoral), and target antigens.

<a id='question-049'></a>

### Question 049: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 49 (reflect)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of T cell receptor (TCR) signaling and costimulatory molecules (CD28-B7 interaction) in T cell activation, specifically in the context of allorecognition during organ transplantation and the mechanism of action of immunosuppressive drugs like cyclosporine A and CTLA4-Ig (belatacept). (理解T细胞受体 (TCR) 信号传导和共刺激分子 (CD28-B7 相互作用) 在 T 细胞活化中的作用，特别是在器官移植过程中的异体识别以及环孢素 A 和 CTLA4-Ig (belatacept) 等免疫抑制药物的作用机制。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an unknown HLA type. He is started on standard immunosuppressive therapy, including cyclosporine A, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops acute rejection, characterized by fever, flank pain, elevated serum creatinine (from 1.8 mg/dL pre-transplant to 4.5 mg/dL), and biopsy findings showing interstitial edema, lymphocytic infiltration, and endothelialitis. His maintenance immunosuppression is intensified with anti-thymocyte globulin (ATG). Despite this, he experiences recurrent acute rejection episodes over the next year. His nephrologist decides to switch his maintenance immunosuppression regimen to include belatacept in addition to mycophenolate mofetil and low-dose prednisone, discontinuing cyclosporine A. Belatacept is a fusion protein that binds to CD80 and CD86 on antigen-presenting cells (APCs). Which of the following mechanisms BEST explains the therapeutic effect of belatacept in preventing T cell-mediated rejection in this patient?

(A) Belatacept inhibits the calcineurin pathway, preventing the transcription of IL-2 and other cytokines necessary for T cell proliferation.
(B) Belatacept blocks the interaction between CD28 on T cells and CD80/CD86 on APCs, thereby inhibiting the costimulatory signal required for full T cell activation.
(C) Belatacept directly inhibits the binding of the T cell receptor (TCR) to the peptide-MHC complex on the surface of APCs.
(D) Belatacept promotes the differentiation of regulatory T cells (Tregs), which suppress the activity of alloreactive effector T cells.
(E) Belatacept inhibits the production of antibodies by B cells, preventing antibody-mediated rejection.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the context for transplantation immunology.
- **Initial Immunosuppression:** Cyclosporine A (calcineurin inhibitor), mycophenolate mofetil (inhibits purine synthesis, affecting lymphocyte proliferation), prednisone (corticosteroid). Standard regimen for preventing rejection.
- **Acute Rejection:** Occurs despite initial therapy, indicating insufficient immunosuppression or strong alloreactivity. Symptoms (fever, flank pain, elevated creatinine) and biopsy findings (lymphocytic infiltration, endothelialitis) are classic signs.
- **Treatment Intensification:** ATG (anti-thymocyte globulin) is used to deplete T cells, a common strategy for acute rejection.
- **Recurrent Rejection:** Highlights the need for a different long-term maintenance strategy.
- **Switch to Belatacept:** The core of the question. Belatacept replaces cyclosporine A.
- **Mechanism of Belatacept:** Described as a fusion protein binding to CD80 and CD86 on APCs. This is the key pharmacological clue.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *mechanism* of belatacept's therapeutic effect in preventing T cell-mediated rejection.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **T Cell Activation:** Naive T cells require two signals for full activation:
    1.  **Signal 1:** TCR binding to peptide-MHC complex on APCs. This signal initiates intracellular signaling cascades.
    2.  **Signal 2 (Costimulation):** Interaction between CD28 on the T cell and B7 molecules (CD80/CD86) on the APC. This signal is crucial for preventing anergy or tolerance.
- **Allorecognition:** In transplantation, recipient T cells recognize donor MHC molecules (alloantigens) as foreign. This triggers T cell activation, leading to rejection.
- **Belatacept Mechanism:** Belatacept is a fusion protein containing the extracellular domain of CD80 fused to the Fc portion of human IgG1. It binds to CD80 (B7-1) and CD86 (B7-2) on APCs with higher affinity than CD28. By occupying CD80/CD86, belatacept prevents the binding of CD28, thus blocking the crucial costimulatory signal (Signal 2).
- **Consequence of Blocking Costimulation:** Without costimulation, T cells that recognize alloantigens may become anergic (unresponsive) or undergo apoptosis, rather than proliferating and mediating rejection. Belatacept specifically targets the costimulatory pathway, leaving the TCR-MHC interaction (Signal 1) intact.
- **Cyclosporine A Mechanism:** Inhibits calcineurin, a phosphatase required for activating NFAT (Nuclear Factor of Activated T cells). NFAT is a transcription factor essential for the expression of IL-2 and other cytokines (like IFN-γ, TNF-α) needed for T cell proliferation and effector function. Blocking calcineurin prevents Signal 1 transduction downstream effects related to cytokine production.
- **Contrast:** Belatacept blocks Signal 2 (costimulation), while cyclosporine A blocks downstream effects of Signal 1 (cytokine production).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): This describes the mechanism of action of cyclosporine A (and tacrolimus), which inhibits calcineurin and prevents IL-2 transcription. The patient *stopped* taking cyclosporine A, and belatacept works differently. This is incorrect.
- (C): This describes the initial signal (Signal 1) for T cell activation. While TCR binding is necessary, belatacept does not directly inhibit this interaction. Blocking Signal 1 would lead to anergy or deletion, but belatacept targets Signal 2. This is incorrect.
- (D): While some immunosuppressants (like IL-2 inhibitors or certain therapies) might influence Treg function, the primary mechanism of belatacept is not Treg promotion. Belatacept directly interferes with the costimulatory pathway required for effector T cell activation. This is incorrect.
- (E): Belatacept primarily targets T cell-mediated rejection by blocking costimulation. While B cells are involved in rejection (antibody-mediated rejection), belatacept's main action is on the T cell/APC interaction required for T cell activation. It does not directly inhibit B cell antibody production. This is incorrect.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Belatacept (CTLA4-Ig)                                  | Cyclosporine A (Calcineurin Inhibitor)                 | Anti-thymocyte Globulin (ATG)                     |
| :------------------ | :----------------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------ |
| **Target**          | CD80/CD86 on APCs                                      | Calcineurin (Phosphatase)                               | T cells (depletion)                                |
| **Mechanism**       | Blocks CD28 costimulation (Signal 2)                   | Inhibits IL-2 transcription (downstream of Signal 1) | Depletes T cells                                  |
| **Effect**          | Prevents T cell activation/proliferation              | Prevents T cell proliferation/cytokine production     | Reduces overall T cell number                      |
| **Primary Role**    | T cell-mediated rejection                              | T cell-mediated rejection                              | Acute rejection treatment/prevention              |
| **Side Effects**    | Increased risk of infection, malignancy, thrombosis    | Nephrotoxicity, hypertension, neurotoxicity, hirsutism | Serum sickness, infusion reactions, cytopenias    |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Belatacept is a costimulation blocker that inhibits T cell activation by preventing the CD28-CD80/CD86 interaction (Signal 2). This differs from calcineurin inhibitors (like cyclosporine A) which block Signal 1 downstream effects (IL-2 production) and T cell depleting agents (like ATG) which reduce the number of effector cells. Understanding the specific targets and mechanisms of different immunosuppressants is crucial for managing transplant rejection. Key differentials include calcineurin inhibitors (cyclosporine, tacrolimus), mTOR inhibitors (sirolimus, everolimus), antimetabolites (mycophenolate, azathioprine), and T cell depleting agents (ATG, alemtuzumab).

<a id='question-050'></a>

### Question 050: Transplantation: Part 1 Focus 50 (invasion)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding opportunistic infections in immunocompromised transplant recipients, specifically the pathogenesis of *Aspergillus* invasion and the role of neutrophils in fungal clearance. (理解移植受者机会性感染，特别是 *曲霉菌* 侵袭的病理机制以及中性粒细胞在真菌清除中的作用)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male, status post-liver transplant 6 months ago for alcoholic cirrhosis, presents to the emergency department with a 3-day history of worsening fever, chills, and pleuritic chest pain. He is currently on maintenance immunosuppression with tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Physical examination reveals a temperature of 39.2°C, tachypnea (respiratory rate 28/min), and localized crackles in the right lower lung field. A chest X-ray shows a poorly defined, wedge-shaped opacity in the right lower lobe. Sputum analysis reveals septate hyphae branching at acute angles (45 degrees) on Gram stain. Bronchoalveolar lavage (BAL) fluid culture grows filamentous fungi within 7 days at 37°C. The patient's absolute neutrophil count (ANC) is 250 cells/µL.

Which of the following mechanisms is MOST likely impaired in this patient, contributing to his susceptibility to this infection?

(A) Impaired T-cell mediated cytotoxicity against infected cells.
(B) Defective phagosome-lysosome fusion in macrophages.
(C) Reduced production of reactive oxygen species (ROS) by neutrophils.
(D) Decreased complement activation leading to impaired opsonization.
(E) Impaired antibody production against fungal cell wall components.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, 6 months post-liver transplant, on immunosuppression (tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, prednisone). This establishes a high-risk patient for opportunistic infections due to immunosuppression.
- **Symptoms:** Fever, chills, pleuritic chest pain, tachypnea. Suggestive of pneumonia.
- **Physical Exam:** Fever (39.2°C), tachypnea (RR 28), crackles in right lower lung. Consistent with pneumonia.
- **Imaging:** Chest X-ray shows a wedge-shaped opacity. Classic finding for pulmonary infarction or, in an immunocompromised host, invasive fungal infection, particularly *Aspergillus*.
- **Microbiology:** Sputum Gram stain shows septate hyphae branching at 45 degrees. Characteristic morphology of *Aspergillus* species. BAL culture grows filamentous fungi at 37°C. Confirms fungal growth, likely *Aspergillus*.
- **Immunology:** Absolute neutrophil count (ANC) is 250 cells/µL. This indicates severe neutropenia (normal ANC > 1500 cells/µL). Neutrophils are crucial for host defense against invasive fungal infections like *Aspergillus*.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the impaired mechanism contributing to susceptibility. The combination of immunosuppression (especially neutropenia) and *Aspergillus* infection points towards a defect in neutrophil function.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Neutrophil Function:** Neutrophils are the primary phagocytes responsible for clearing extracellular pathogens, including fungi like *Aspergillus*. They engulf the fungi (phagocytosis) and kill them intracellularly.
- **Neutrophil Killing Mechanisms:** Key mechanisms include:
    - **Phagocytosis:** Engulfment of the pathogen.
    - **Phagosome-Lysosome Fusion:** Formation of a phagolysosome where the pathogen is exposed to degradative enzymes and low pH.
    - **Reactive Oxygen Species (ROS) Production:** The "respiratory burst" generates superoxide radicals (O2-), hydrogen peroxide (H2O2), and hydroxyl radicals (•OH) via NADPH oxidase, which are highly toxic to microbes.
    - **Degranulation:** Release of antimicrobial peptides and enzymes (e.g., lysozyme, defensins) into the phagolysosome.
- ***Aspergillus* Pathogenesis:** *Aspergillus* conidia are inhaled and reach the alveoli. In immunocompetent individuals, neutrophils and macrophages clear them. In neutropenic patients, *Aspergillus* can invade blood vessels, leading to infarction (wedge-shaped opacity) and dissemination. Neutrophils are critical for controlling *Aspergillus* growth and preventing invasion.
- **Neutropenia and Fungal Infections:** Severe neutropenia (<500 cells/µL) significantly increases the risk of invasive fungal infections, particularly *Aspergillus* and *Candida*. The lack of sufficient neutrophils impairs phagocytosis and, critically, the respiratory burst (ROS production), which is essential for killing *Aspergillus* hyphae.
- **Option Analysis:**
    - (A) T-cell cytotoxicity is important for viral infections and intracellular pathogens, less so for extracellular fungi like *Aspergillus*. While immunosuppression affects T-cells, the primary defect in *Aspergillus* susceptibility in neutropenia is neutrophil dysfunction.
    - (B) Defective phagosome-lysosome fusion is characteristic of diseases like Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD), where NADPH oxidase is deficient, leading to impaired ROS production. While ROS production is impaired in neutropenia, the primary issue is the *lack* of neutrophils, not necessarily a defect in the fusion process itself in the few remaining neutrophils (though severe neutropenia can be associated with other defects). The most direct consequence of low neutrophil numbers is reduced overall killing capacity, particularly via ROS.
    - (C) Reduced ROS production is a critical function of neutrophils. Neutropenia directly reduces the *number* of neutrophils available to produce ROS. Therefore, the overall capacity for ROS-mediated killing is severely impaired. This is the most direct link between neutropenia and susceptibility to *Aspergillus*.
    - (D) Complement activation and opsonization are important for phagocytosis, but neutrophils are the primary effector cells for killing *Aspergillus*. While complement deficiencies can increase susceptibility to certain infections, neutropenia is the dominant risk factor here.
    - (E) Antibody production (B-cell function) is important for encapsulated bacteria and some fungi, but neutrophils are the main defense against invasive *Aspergillus*. Immunosuppression affects B-cells, but neutropenia is the more specific and critical factor for this presentation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Impaired T-cell mediated cytotoxicity is crucial for controlling intracellular pathogens (like viruses, intracellular bacteria, some fungi like *Histoplasma*) and tumor cells. While immunosuppressants affect T-cells, the primary defense against extracellular *Aspergillus* hyphae relies on neutrophils and macrophages, not T-cell cytotoxicity. This option confuses the role of T-cells with the primary phagocytic defense against *Aspergillus*.
- (B): Defective phagosome-lysosome fusion is the hallmark of Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD), where the NADPH oxidase complex is defective, leading to impaired ROS production despite normal phagocytosis and fusion. While ROS production is impaired in neutropenia, the fundamental problem here is the *absence* of sufficient neutrophils, not a defect in the fusion mechanism itself. Patients with CGD are susceptible to *Aspergillus*, but the mechanism is different (intrinsic neutrophil defect vs. lack of neutrophils).
- (D): Decreased complement activation impairs opsonization (e.g., C3b coating) and direct lysis. While opsonization aids phagocytosis, the primary killing mechanism against *Aspergillus* by neutrophils involves ROS and lysosomal enzymes. Complement deficiencies are less commonly associated with invasive *Aspergillus* compared to neutropenia.
- (E): Impaired antibody production affects humoral immunity, primarily targeting extracellular bacteria and toxins. While antibodies can play a role in fungal immunity, the immediate defense against invasive *Aspergillus* relies heavily on neutrophils. B-cell suppression is a consequence of immunosuppression, but not the primary reason for *Aspergillus* susceptibility in this neutropenic patient.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | *Aspergillus* Infection in Neutropenia | Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD) | T-cell Deficiency (e.g., AIDS) | Antibody Deficiency (e.g., CVID) |
|-------------------------|---------------------------------------|------------------------------------|---------------------------------|-----------------------------------|
| **Primary Defect**      | Lack of neutrophils                  | Defective NADPH oxidase (ROS)      | Impaired cell-mediated immunity | Impaired humoral immunity        |
| **Key Immune Cell**     | Neutrophils                           | Neutrophils                        | T-lymphocytes                   | B-lymphocytes                    |
| **Mechanism Impaired**  | Phagocytosis & Killing (esp. ROS)    | Killing (esp. ROS)                | Killing intracellular pathogens | Opsonization, Neutralization     |
| **Typical Pathogens**   | *Aspergillus*, *Candida*              | *Aspergillus*, *Staphylococcus*, *Serratia*, *Burkholderia*, *Nocardia* | *Pneumocystis*, CMV, Mycobacteria | Encapsulated bacteria (*S. pneumoniae*, *H. influenzae*) |
| **Microscopy**          | Septate hyphae, 45° branching         | Septate hyphae, 45° branching      | Variable, often opportunistic   | Variable, often opportunistic   |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Neutropenia is a major risk factor for invasive fungal infections, particularly *Aspergillus*. Neutrophils are essential for clearing fungi through phagocytosis and intracellular killing mechanisms, primarily involving the respiratory burst (ROS production). Understanding the role of neutrophils in host defense is crucial for managing immunocompromised patients. Key differentials for invasive fungal infections include CGD (defective ROS production) and T-cell deficiencies (susceptibility to intracellular fungi like *Pneumocystis*).
